Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Abhidhānappadīpikā

The Illuminator of Nomenclature.

Buddhappaṇāmo

Homage to the Buddha.

1.

1.

Tathāgato [M 0.0001] yo karuṇākaro karo,

Payāta’mosajja sukhappadaṃ padaṃ;

Akā paratthaṃ kalisambhave bhave,

Namāmi taṃ kevaladukkaraṃ karaṃ.

The Tathāgata, a mine of compassion, the doer, has attained the blissful state, having abandoned all ills; he acted for the welfare of others in this existence born of strife; I bow to him, the doer of the utterly difficult deed.

Dhammappaṇāmo

Homage to the Dhamma.

2.

2.

Apūjayuṃ yaṃ munikuñjarā jarā,

Rujādimuttā yahimuttare tare;

Ṭhitā tivaṭṭambunidhiṃ narānarā,

Tariṃsu taṃ dhammamaghappahaṃ pahaṃ.

Which the elephant-sages, free from decay and illness, honored, and by which they crossed over to the supreme state; standing firm, humans and non-humans crossed the ocean of the triple round of existence by means of that Dhamma, the dispeller of evil, the light.

Saṅghappaṇāmo

Homage to the Saṅgha.

3.

3.

Gataṃ munindo’rasasūnutaṃ nutaṃ,

Supuññakhettaṃ bhuvane sutaṃ sutaṃ;

Gaṇampi pāṇīkatasaṃvaraṃ varaṃ,

Sadā guṇoghena nirantara’ntaraṃ.

The assembly, having attained the state of being the true sons of the Lord of Sages, praised; an excellent field of merit renowned in the world; excellent, having undertaken restraint, and ever filled with an unbroken flood of virtues.

Paṭiññā

Pledge

4.

4.

Nāmaliṅgesu kosalla, matthanicchayakāraṇaṃ;

Yato mahabbalaṃ buddha, vacane pāṭavatthinaṃ.

Skill in names and genders is a cause for determining meanings, since it is a great strength for those proficient in the words of the Buddha.

5.

5.

Nāmaliṅgānya’to [M 0.0002] buddha, bhāsitassā’rahānya’haṃ;

Dassayanto pakāsessa, mabhidhānappadīpikaṃ.

Therefore, revealing the names and genders worthy of the Buddha's speech, I shall set forth the Abhidhānappadīpikā.

Paribhāsā

Technical Terms

6.

6.

Bhiyyo rūpantarā sāha, cariyena ca katthaci;

Kvacā’ haccavidhānena ñeyyaṃ thīpunnapuṃsakaṃ.

Further, the feminine, masculine, and neuter genders are to be known by change of form, sometimes by usage, and sometimes by a special rule.

7.

7.

Abhinnaliṅgānaṃyeva, dvando ca, liṅgavācakā;

Gāthāpādantamajjhaṭṭhā, pubbaṃ yantyapare paraṃ.

In a dvandva compound of words with different genders, and for gender-denoting words, those standing at the end or in the middle of a verse line come first, while others follow.

8.

8.

Pumitthiyaṃ padaṃ dvīsu, sabbaliṅge ca tīsviti;

Abhidhānantarārambhe, ñeyyaṃ tva’nta mathādi ca.

A word that is masculine and feminine is said to be in two genders, and one in all genders is in three. At the commencement of another section of nomenclature, the endings 'tva', 'nta', 'matha', etc., are to be understood.

9.

9.

Bhiyyo payoga māgamma, sogate āgame kvaci;

Nighaṇḍu yutti ñcānīya, nāmaliṅgaṃ kathīyatīti.

Further, having resorted to usage, and sometimes to the Sugata's scriptures, and having considered the glossary and reasoning, the names and genders are here explained.

1. Saggakaṇḍa

1. The Section on Heaven

1.

1.

Buddho dasabalo satthā, sabbaññū dvipaduttamo;

Munindo bhagavā nātho, cakkhuma’ṅgīraso muni.

The Buddha, the One with Ten Powers, the Teacher, the All-Knowing One, the Supreme among bipeds; the Lord of Sages, the Blessed One, the Protector, the Seeing One, Aṅgīrasa, the Sage.

2.

2.

Lokanātho’ nadhivaro, mahesi ca vināyako;

Samantacakkhu sugato, bhūripañño ca māraji.

The Lord of the World, the Unsurpassed One, the Great Sage and the Guide; the All-Seeing One, the Well-Gone One, of vast wisdom, and the Conqueror of Māra.

3.

3.

Narasīho naravaro, dhammarājā mahāmuni;

Devadevo lokagaru, dhammassāmī tathāgato.

The Lion among Men, the Best of Men, the King of the Dhamma, the Great Sage; the God of Gods, the Teacher of the World, the Master of the Dhamma, the Tathāgata.

4.

4.

Sayambhū sammāsambuddho, varapañño ca nāyako;

Jino, sakko tu siddhattho, suddhodani ca gotamo.

The Self-Become, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One, the One of Excellent Wisdom, and the Leader; the Conqueror, the Sakyan, Siddhattha, the Son of Suddhodana, and Gotama.

5.

5.

Sakyasīho tathā sakya, muni cādiccabandhu ca;

The Sakyan Lion, and the Sakyan Sage, and the Kinsman of the Sun.

6.

6.

Mokkho nirodho nibbānaṃ, dīpo taṇhakkhayo paraṃ;

Tāṇaṃ leṇa marūpañca, santaṃ sacca manālayaṃ.

Liberation, cessation, Nibbāna, the island, the extinction of craving, the supreme; refuge, shelter, the formless, the peaceful, truth, and non-abode.

7.

7.

Asaṅkhataṃ siva mamataṃ sududdasaṃ,

Parāyaṇaṃ saraṇa manītikaṃ tathā;

Anāsavaṃ dhuva manidassanā’ katā,

Palokitaṃ nipuṇa mananta makkharaṃ.

The Unconditioned, the auspicious, the deathless, the very hard to see; the final goal, the refuge, the sorrowless, likewise; the taintless, the permanent, the invisible, the unmade; the undecaying, the subtle, the endless, the imperishable.

8.

8.

Dukkhakkhayo [M 0.0003] byābajjhañca byāpajjaṃ (ṭīkā), vivaṭṭaṃ khema kevalaṃ;

Apavaggo virāgo ca, paṇīta maccutaṃ padaṃ.

The cessation of suffering, and non-affliction, the unrolling, safety, the absolute; emancipation, dispassion, and the sublime, deathless state.

9.

9.

Yogakkhemo pāra mapi, mutti santi visuddhiyo;

Vimutya’ saṅkhatadhātu, suddhi nibbutiyo siyuṃ.

Security from the yoke, and the far shore; freedom, peace, and purities; deliverance, the unconditioned element, purity, and extinguishment are these.

10.

10.

Khīṇāsavo tva’sekkho ca, vītarāgo tathā’ rahā;

Devaloko divo saggo nāko (sī.),

Tidivo tidasālayo.

One whose taints are destroyed, one beyond training, free from passion, and likewise an Arahant. The world of the devas, the celestial realm, heaven, the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, the abode of the thirty-three devas.

11.

11.

Tidasā tva’marā devā, vibudhā ca sudhāsino;

Surā marū divokā cā, matapā saggavāsino.

The Thirty-Three, the deathless ones, the devas, the wise ones, and the nectar-drinkers; the Suras, the Maruts, the heaven-dwellers, and the drinkers of ambrosia, the inhabitants of heaven.

12.

12.

Nijjarā’ nimisā dibbā, apume devatāni ca devatā eva devatāni, sakatthe nipaccayo (ṭīkā);

The ageless, the unblinking, the divine; and the deities, which are not of the masculine gender.

13.

13.

Siddho bhūto ca gandhabbo, guyhako yakkha rakkhasā;

Kumbhaṇḍo ca pisācā’dī, niddiṭṭhā devayoniyo.

The accomplished ones, spirits, Gandharvas, hidden ones, Yakkhas, Rakshasas, Kumbhaṇḍas, Pisācas, and others are designated as divine classes.

14.

14.

Pubbadevā suraripū, asurā dānavā pume;

Tabbisesā pahārādo, sambaro baliādayo.

The ancient devas, the enemies of the Suras, the Asuras, the Dānavas, are masculine; particular among them are Pahārāda, Sambara, Bali, and others.

15.

15.

Pitāmaho pitā brahmā, lokeso kamalāsano;

Tathā hiraññagabbho ca, surajeṭṭho pajāpati.

The Grandsire, the Father, Brahmā, the Lord of the World, the Lotus-seated; likewise Hiraññagabbha, the Chief of Devas, the Lord of Creatures.

16.

16.

Vāsudevo hari harī (ṭīkā) kaṇho, kesavo cakkapāṇya’tha;

Mahissaro sivo sūlī, issaro pasupatya’pi.

Vāsudeva, Hari, Kaṇha, Kesava, and the Discus-bearer; the Great Lord, Siva, the Trident-bearer, the Lord, and Pasupati.

17.

17.

Haro vutto kumāro tu, khandho sattidharo bhave;

Hara is a name given; and Kumāra is Skandha, the spear-bearer.

18.

18.

Sakko purindado deva, rājā vajirapāṇi ca;

Sujampati sahassakkho, mahindo vajirāvudho.

Sakka, the Giver of Cities, the King of Devas, and the Thunderbolt-handed; Sujampati, the Thousand-eyed, Mahinda, the Thunderbolt-weaponed.

19.

19.

Vāsavo ca dasasata, nayano tidivādhibhū;

Suranātho ca vajira, hattho ca bhūtapatya’pi.

Vāsava, the Thousand-eyed, the Lord of Tāvatiṃsa; the Lord of Suras, the Thunderbolt-handed, and also the Lord of Beings.

20.

20.

Maghavā kosiyo indo, vatrabhū pākasāsano;

Viḍojo tha sujā tassa sujātā’ssa (ṭīkā), bhariyā tha puraṃ bhave.

Maghavā, Kosiya, Indra, the Slayer of Vṛtra, the Chastiser of Pāka, and Viḍoja; Sujā is his wife, and his city is...

21.

21.

Masakkasārā [M 0.0004] masakkasāro (sī.) vassoka, sārā cevā’ marāvatī;

Vejayanto tu pāsādo,

Sudhammā tu sabhā matā.

Masakkasārā, Vassokasārā, and Amarāvatī are the city; Vejayanta is the palace, and Sudhammā is considered the assembly hall.

22.

22.

Vejayanto ratho tassa,

Vutto mātali sārathi;

Erāvaṇo gajo paṇḍu, kambalo tu silāsanaṃ.

Vejayanta is his chariot; Mātali is called the charioteer; Erāvaṇa is the elephant; and Paṇḍukambala is the stone seat.

23.

23.

Suvīroccādayo puttā, nandā pokkharaṇī bhave;

Nandanaṃ missakaṃ citta, latā phārusakaṃ vanā.

Suvīra and others are his sons; Nandā is the lotus pond; Nandana, Missaka, Cittalatā, and Phārusaka are the forests.

24.

24.

Asani dvīsu kulisaṃ, vajiraṃ punnapuṃsake;

Accharāyotthiyaṃ vuttā, rambhā alambusādayo;

Devitthiyo tha gandhabbā, pañcasikhoti ādayo.

Asani is of two genders; Kulisa and Vajira are masculine and neuter. Accharā is said to be feminine; Rambhā, Alambusā, and others are celestial women. And the Gandhabbas are such as Pañcasikha and others.

25.

25.

Vimāno nitthiyaṃ byamhaṃ, pīyūsaṃ tvamataṃ sudhā;

Sineru meru tidivā, dhāro neru sumeru ca.

Vimāna is not feminine; byamha is a celestial abode. Pīyūsa, amata, and sudhā are nectar. Sineru, Meru, the Support of Heaven, Neru, and Sumeru are names for the mountain.

26.

26.

Yugandharo īsadharo, karavīko sudassano;

Nemindharo vinatako, assakaṇṇo kulācalā.

Yugandhara, Īsadhara, Karavīka, Sudassana, Nemindhara, Vinataka, and Assakaṇṇa are the Kulācalā mountains.

27.

27.

Mandākinī tathā’kāsa, gaṅgā suranadī pyatha;

The Mandākinī, likewise the Ākāsagaṅgā, and also the Suranadī.

28.

28.

Koviḷāro tathā pāri, cchattako pārijātako;

Kapparukkho tu santānā, dayo devaddumā siyuṃ.

Koviḷāra, Pāricchattaka, Pārijātaka, the Kapparukkha, Santāna, and others are divine trees.

29.

29.

Pācī patījhu’ dīci’tthī, pubba pacchima uttarā;

Disātha dakkhiṇā’ pācī, vidisā’nudisā bhave.

Pācī, Patīci, and Udīci are feminine terms for the directions East, West, and North; also used are Pubba, Pacchima, and Uttarā. The southern direction is Dakkhinā or Apācī. Vidisā and Anudisā are intermediate and subsidiary directions.

30.

30.

Erāvato erāvaṇo (sī.), amarakose pana erāvato eva atthi puṇḍarīko, vāmano kumudo’ ñjano;

Pupphadanto sabbabhummo, suppatīko disāgajā.

Erāvata, Puṇḍarīka, Vāmana, Kumuda, Añjana, Pupphadanta, Sabbabhumma, and Suppatīka are the elephants of the directions.

31.

31.

Dhataraṭṭho ca gandhabbā, dhipo, kumbhaṇḍasāmi tu;

Viruḷhako, virūpakkho, tu nāgādhipatīrito.

Dhataraṭṭha is the lord of the Gandharvas; Viruḷhaka is the lord of the Kumbhaṇḍas; and Virūpakkha is called the lord of the Nāgas.

32.

32.

Yakkhādhipo vessavaṇo, kuvero naravāhano;

Aḷakā alakā (amarakosa) ḷakamandāssa, purī, paharaṇaṃ gadā;

Catuddisāna madhipā, pubbādīnaṃ kamā ime.

The lord of the Yakkhas is Vessavaṇa, also known as Kuvera, the Man-vehicled. Aḷakā and Aḷakamandā are his cities, and his weapon is the mace. These, in order from the east onwards, are the lords of the four directions.

33.

33.

Jātavedo [M 0.0005] sikhī joti, pāvako dahano’ nalo;

Hutāvaho’ ccimā dhūma, ketva’ggi gini bhānumā.

Jātaveda, the Flame-crested, Light, the Purifier, the Burner, Fire; the Oblation-bearer, the Radiant, the Smoke-bannered, Fire, Blaze, the Resplendent.

34.

34.

Tejo dhūmasikho vāyu, sakho ca kaṇhavattanī;

Vessānaro hutāso tha, sikhājāla’cci cāpume.

Tejo, the Smoke-crested, the Friend of Wind, and the Dark-pathed; Vessānara, Hutāsa; and sikhā (flame-crest), jālā (mass of flame), and acci (flame) are not masculine.

35.

35.

Vipphuliṅgaṃ phuliṅgañca, bhasmā ‘bhasmā’ tipadaṃ rājādigaṇe pariyāpannaṃ (rūpasiddhi-uṇādi), bhasmaṃ (sī.) tu seṭṭhi chārikā;

Vipphuliṅga and phuliṅga are a spark; bhasma, seṭṭhi, and chārikā are ash or cinders.

36.

36.

Kukkuḷo tu’ṇhabhasmasmi, maṅgāro’lāta mummukaṃ tikaṃ dittakaṭṭhādindhane (ṭīkā);

Samidhā idhumaṃ ce’dho, upādānaṃ tathindhanaṃ.

Kukkuḷa means hot ashes; aṅgāra is an ember, alāta a firebrand, and ummuka a live coal. Samidhā, idhuma, and idho mean firewood; upādāna and indhana likewise mean fuel.

37.

37.

Atho’bhāso pakāso cā,

'Loko’jjotā’tapā samā;

Māluto pavano vāyu, vāto’nila samīraṇā;

Gandhavāho tathā vāyo, samīro ca sadāgati.

Furthermore, obhāsa (radiance), pakāsa (light), āloka (illumination), ujjota (glow), and ātapa (sunlight) are similar in meaning. Māluta, pavana, vāyu, vāta, anila, and samīraṇa are names for the wind; likewise gandhavāha (the Scent-carrier), vāya, samīra, and sadāgati (the Ever-moving).

38.

38.

Vāyubhedā ime chu’ddha, ṅgamo cādhogamo tathā;

Kucchiṭṭho ca koṭṭhāsayo, assāsaṅgānusārino.

These are the six kinds of wind: the upward-moving, the downward-moving, that which is in the belly, that which is in the intestines, the in-breath, and that which moves through the limbs.

39.

39.

Atho apānaṃ passāso,

Assāso āna muccate.

Furthermore, apāna and passāsa mean the out-breath; assāsa and āna are said to be the in-breath.

40.

40.

Vego javo rayo khippaṃ, tu sīghaṃ turitaṃ lahu;

Āsu tuṇṇa maraṃ cāvi, lambitaṃ tuvaṭaṃpi ca.

Speed, swiftness, quickness; and swift, speedy, hurried, light; swift, quick, promptly; and undelayed, and also swift.

41.

41.

Satataṃ nicca maviratā, nārata santata manavaratañca dhuvaṃ;

Bhusa matisayo ca daḷhaṃ, tibbe’kantā’timatta, bāḷhāni;

Khippādī paṇḍake dabbe, dabbagā tesu ye tisu.

Always, constantly, without interruption, ceaselessly, continuously, incessantly, and permanently; greatly, exceedingly, and firmly; intense, absolute, excessive, and strong. The words beginning with 'khippa' are neuter; among them, those that can refer to a substance are in all three genders.

42.

42.

Aviggaho tu kāmo ca, manobhū madano bhave;

Antako vasavattī ca, pāpimā ca pajāpati.

Aviggaha, Kāma, Manobhū, and Madana are names for Māra; as are Antaka, Vasavattī, Pāpimā, and Pajāpati.

43.

43.

Pamattabandhu kaṇho ca, māro namuci, tassa tu;

Taṇhā’ratī ragā dhītū, hatthī tu girimekhalo.

Pamattabandhu (the Friend of the Heedless), Kaṇha, Māra, and Namuci. His daughters are Taṇhā (Craving), Aratī (Discontent), and Ragā (Lust); and his elephant is Girimekhala.

44.

44.

Yamarājā ca vesāyī, yamo’ssa nayanāvudhaṃ;

Vepacitti pulomo ca, kimpuriso tu kinnaro.

Yamarājā and Vesāyī; Yama, whose weapon is his eye; Vepacitti and Puloma; and Kimpurisa, indeed, is Kinnara.

45.

45.

Antalikkhaṃ kha’mādicca, patho’bbhaṃ gagana’mbaraṃ;

Vehāso cānilapatho, ākāso nitthiyaṃ nabhaṃ.

Antalikkha, Kha, Ādiccapatha (the sun's path), Abbha, Gagana, Ambara, Vehāsa, Anilapatha (the wind's path), and Ākāsa are synonyms for the sky; Nabha is not in the feminine gender.

46.

46.

Devo [M 0.0006] vehāyaso tārā,

Patho surapatho aghaṃ.

Deva, Vehāyasa, Tārāpatha, Surapatha, and Agha are further synonyms for the sky.

47.

47.

Megho valāhako devo, pajjunno’mbudharo ghano;

Dhārādharo ca jīmūto, vārivāho tathā’mbudo.

Megha, Valāhaka, Deva, Pajjunna, Ambudhara, Ghana, Dhārādhara, Jīmūta, Vārivāha, and likewise Ambuda, are synonyms for a cloud.

48.

48.

Abbhaṃ tīsvatha vassañca, vassanaṃ vuṭṭhi nāriyaṃ;

Sateratā’kkhaṇā vijju, vijjutā cācirappabhā.

The word Abbha is used in three genders. Vassa, Vassana, and Vuṭṭhi are synonyms for rain, with Vuṭṭhi being feminine. Sateratā, Akkhaṇā, Vijju, Vijjutā, and Acirappabhā are synonyms for lightning.

49.

49.

Meghanāde tu dhanitaṃ, gajjitaṃ rasitādi ca;

Indāvudhaṃ indadhanu, vātakkhittambu sīkaro.

For the sound of clouds, or thunder, there are the terms Dhanita, Gajjita, Rasita, and so forth. Indāvudha and Indadhanu are synonyms for a rainbow. Sīkara is water spray scattered by the wind.

50.

50.

Āsāro dhārā sampāto,

Karakā tu ghanopalaṃ;

Duddinaṃ meghacchannāhe, pidhānaṃ tvapadhāraṇaṃ.

Āsāra and Dhārāsampāta are synonyms for a downpour. Karakā and Ghanopala are synonyms for hail. Duddina refers to a cloud-covered day. Pidhāna and Apadhāraṇa are synonyms for a covering.

51.

51.

Tirodhāna’ntaradhānā, pidhāna chādanāni ca;

Indu cando ca nakkhatta, rājā somo nisākaro.

Tirodhāna, Antaradhāna, Pidhāna, and Chādana are synonyms for concealment or a covering. Indu, Canda, Nakkhattarājā (King of Stars), Soma, and Nisākara (the Night-maker) are synonyms for the moon.

52.

52.

Osadhīso himaraṃsi, sasaṅko candimā sasī;

Sītaraṃsi nisānātho, uḷurājā ca mā pume.

Osadhīsa (Lord of Herbs), Himaraṃsi (the Cool-rayed), Sasaṅka (the Hare-marked), Candimā, Sasī, Sītaraṃsi (the Cool-rayed), Nisānātha (Lord of Night), Uḷurājā (King of Stars), and Mā are masculine synonyms for the moon.

53.

53.

Kalā soḷasamo bhāgo, bimbaṃ tu maṇḍalaṃ bhave;

Aḍḍho tvaddho upaḍḍho ca, vā khaṇḍaṃ sakalaṃ pume.

A Kalā is the sixteenth part. Bimba and Maṇḍala signify a disk or circle. Aḍḍha, Addha, and Upaḍḍha mean half. Khaṇḍa means a fragment, and Sakala, meaning whole, is masculine.

54.

54.

Addhaṃ vuttaṃ same bhāge, pasādo tu pasannatā;

Komudī candikā juṇhā, kanti sobhā juti cchavi.

Addha is said of an equal part. Pasāda is indeed Pasannatā. Komudī, Candikā, and Juṇhā are synonyms for moonlight; Kanti, Sobhā, Juti, and Chavi are synonyms for splendor.

55.

55.

Kalaṅko lañchanaṃ lakkhaṃ, aṅko’bhiññāṇa lakkhaṇaṃ;

Cihanaṃ cāpi sobhā tu, paramā susamā tha ca.

Kalaṅka, Lañchana, Lakkha, Aṅka, Abhiññāṇa, Lakkhaṇa, and Cihana are synonyms for a mark or sign. Sobhā, indeed, is supreme beauty and also great comeliness.

56.

56.

Sītaṃ guṇe, guṇiliṅgā, sīta sisira sītalā;

Himaṃ tuhina mussāvo, nīhāro mahikā pyatha.

Sīta, meaning cold, is a quality and is used as an adjective. Sīta, Sisira, and Sītalā are synonyms for cool or cold. Hima and Tuhina mean frost or snow; Ussāva means dew; and Nīhāra and Mahikā mean mist or fog.

57.

57.

Nakkhattaṃ joti bhaṃ tārā, apume tārako’ḷu ca;

Nakkhatta, Joti, Bha, and Tārā are synonyms for a star or constellation; these are not in the masculine gender. Tāraka and Uḷu are also synonyms for a star.

58.

58.

Assayujo bharaṇitthī, sakattikā rohiṇī pica;

Migasira magasira (sī.) maddā ca punabbasu, phusso pusso (ṭī.) cāsilesa’pi.

Assayuja, Bharaṇī (which is feminine), Kattikā, and Rohiṇī; Migasira, Addā, Punabbasu, Phussa, and also Āsilesā are names of constellations.

59.

59.

Māghā magha (sī.) ca phaggunī dve ca, hatthā cittā ca svātipi;

Visākhā’ nurādhā jeṭṭhā, mūlā’ sāḷhā duve tathā.

Māghā and the two Phagguṇīs; Hattha, Cittā, and Svāti; Visākhā, Anurādhā, Jeṭṭhā, Mūla, and likewise the two Āsāḷhās, are names of constellations.

60.

60.

Savaṇo [M 0.0007] ca dhaniṭṭhā ca, satabhisajo pubbu’ttarabhaddapadā;

Revatyapīti kamato, sattādhikavīsanakkhattā.

Savaṇa and Dhaniṭṭhā; Satabhisaja; Pubbabhaddapadā and Uttarabhaddapadā; and also Revatī. Thus, in order, are the twenty-seven constellations.

61.

61.

Sobbhānu kathito rāhu, sūrādī tu navaggahā;

Rāsi mesādiko bhadda, padā poṭṭhapadā samā.

Sobbhānu is called Rāhu; the sun and the others are the nine planets. The zodiac begins with Mesa (Aries), O auspicious one. The Bhaddapadā and the Poṭṭhapadā constellations are the same.

62.

62.

Ādicco sūriyo sūro, sataraṃsi divākaro;

Verocano dinakaro, uṇharaṃsi pabhaṅkaro.

Ādicca, Sūriya, Sūra, Sataraṃsi, Divākara, Verocana, Dinakara, Uṇharaṃsi, and Pabhaṅkara are names for the sun.

63.

63.

Aṃsumālī dinapati, tapano ravi bhānumā;

Raṃsimā bhākaro bhānu, akko sahassaraṃsi ca.

Aṃsumālī, Dinapati, Tapana, Ravi, Bhānumā, Raṃsimā, Bhākara, Bhānu, Akka, and Sahassaraṃsi are names for the sun.

64.

64.

Raṃsi ābhā pabhā ditti, ruci bhā juti dīdhiti;

Marīci dvīsu bhānvaṃ’su, mayūkho kiraṇo karo.

Raṃsi, Ābhā, Pabhā, Ditti, Ruci, Bhā, Juti, and Dīdhiti are synonyms for radiance. Marīci is used in two genders; Bhānu, Aṃsu, Mayūkha, Kiraṇa, and Kara are synonyms for a ray of light.

65.

65.

Paridhi pariveso tha, marīci migataṇhikā;

Sūrassodayato pubbu’, ṭṭhitaraṃsi siyā’ ruṇo.

Paridhi and Parivesa are synonyms for a halo. Marīci and Migataṇhikā are synonyms for a mirage. The light which has arisen before sunrise is Aruṇa, the dawn.

66.

66.

Kālo’ddhā samayo velā,

Tabbisesā khaṇādayo;

Khaṇo dasaccharākālo,

Khaṇā dasa layo bhave.

Kāla, Addhā, Samaya, and Velā are synonyms for time. Its divisions are the Khaṇa (moment) and so forth. A Khaṇa is the time it takes to utter ten syllables. Ten Khaṇas make a Laya.

67.

67.

Layā dasa khaṇalayo,

Muhutto te siyā dasa;

Dasa khaṇamuhutto te, divaso tu ahaṃ dinaṃ.

Ten Layas are a Khaṇalaya. Ten of those would be a Muhutta. Ten of those are a Khaṇamuhutta. Divasa, Aha, and Dina are synonyms for day.

68.

68.

Pabhātañca vibhātañca, paccūso kalla mapyatha;

Abhidoso padoso tha,

Sāyo sañjhā dinaccayo.

Pabhāta, Vibhāta, Paccūsa, and Kalla are synonyms for dawn. Abhidosa, Padosa, Sāyo, Sañjhā, and Dinaccaya are synonyms for evening.

69.

69.

Nisā ca rajanī ratti, tiyāmā saṃvarī bhave;

Juṇhā tu candikāyuttā, tamussannā timisikā.

Nisā, Rajanī, Ratti, Tiyāmā, and Saṃvarī are synonyms for night. A moonlit night is called Juṇhā, and a night of abundant darkness is called Timisikā.

70.

70.

Nisītho majjhimā ratti, aḍḍharatto mahānisā;

Andhakāro tamo nitthī, timisaṃ timiraṃ mataṃ.

Nisītha, Majjhimā Ratti, Aḍḍharatta, and Mahānisā are synonyms for midnight. Andhakāra, Tama (which is not feminine), Timisa, and Timira are considered synonyms for darkness.

71.

71.

Caturaṅgatamaṃ evaṃ, kāḷapakkhacatuddasī;

Vanasaṇḍo ghano megha, paṭalaṃ cā’ḍḍharatti ca.

Thus is the four-limbed darkness composed: the fourteenth day of the dark fortnight, a dense forest thicket, a thick mass of clouds, and midnight.

72.

72.

Andhatamaṃ [M 0.0008] ghanatame, pahāro yāma, saññito;

Pāṭipado tu dutiyā, tatiyādī tithī tithi, tāyati pāletīti tithi, tā+iti (ṇvādi) dvisu.

Andhatama refers to the densest darkness. Pahāra and Yāma are terms for a watch of the night. Pāṭipada, Dutiyā, Tatiyā, and so forth are the Tithis, or lunar days. The word Tithi is derived from the root 'tā' with the suffix 'iti', meaning 'that which extends and protects', and is used in two genders.

73.

73.

Pannarasī pañcadasī, puṇṇamāsī tu puṇṇamā;

Amāvasī pyamāvāsī, thiyaṃ pannarasī’ parā.

Pannarasī and Pañcadasī are synonyms for the fifteenth day. Puṇṇamāsī and Puṇṇamā are synonyms for the full moon day. Amāvasī and Amāvāsī are synonyms for the new moon day. The other fifteenth day, the new moon, is also called Pannarasī and is feminine.

74.

74.

Ghaṭikā saṭṭhya’ horatto, pakkho te dasa pañca ca;

Te tu pubbāparā sukka,

Kāḷā, māso tu te duve.

Sixty ghaṭikās make an ahoratta. A fortnight consists of fifteen of those days. These are the bright (sukka) and the dark (kāḷa), the former and the latter. Two of these fortnights make a month.

75.

75.

Citto vesākha, jeṭṭho cā, sāḷho dvīsu ca sāvaṇo;

Poṭṭhapāda’ssayujā ca, māsā dvādasa kattiko.

Citta, Vesākha, Jeṭṭha, Āsāḷha, Sāvaṇa in two forms, Poṭṭhapāda, Assayuja, and Kattika are among the twelve months.

76.

76.

Māgasiro tathā phusso, kamena māgha phagguṇā;

Kattika’ssayujā māsā, pacchima pubbakattikā.

Māgasira, likewise Phussa, and in order Māgha and Phagguṇa. The months Kattika and Assayuja are the later and the earlier Kattika.

77.

77.

Sāvaṇo nikkhamanīyo,

Cittamāso tu rammako.

Sāvaṇa is the month of departure. The month of Citta is delightful.

78.

78.

Caturo caturo māsā, kattikakāḷapakkhato;

Kamā hemanta gimhāna, vassānā utuyo dvisu.

From the dark fortnight of Kattika, four months each, in order, are the seasons of winter, summer, and the rains.

79.

79.

Hemanto sisira mutū,

Cha vā vasanto ca gimha vassānā;

Saradoti kamā māsā, dve dve vuttānusārena.

Or, the six seasons are Hemanta, Sisira, Vasanta, Gimha, Vassāna, and Sarada; in order, they are two months each, according to the teaching.

80.

80.

Uṇho nidāgho gimhotha,

Vasso vassāna pāvusā;

Utūhi tīhi vassānā, dikehi dakkhiṇāyanaṃ.

Uṇha, nidāgha, and gimha are synonyms for summer. Vassa, vassāna, and pāvusā are synonyms for the rainy season. By the three seasons beginning with Vassāna is the Dakkhināyana (southern course) defined.

81.

81.

Uttarāyana maññehi, tīhi vassāyanadvayaṃ;

Vassa saṃvaccharā nitthī, sarado hāyano samā.

The Uttarāyana (northern course) is reckoned by the other three seasons. The two courses make a year. Vassa, saṃvacchara, sarada, hāyana, and samā are synonyms for year.

82.

82.

Kappakkhayo tu saṃvaṭṭo, yuganta palayā api;

Alakkhī kālakaṇṇitthī, atha lakkhī siri’tthiyaṃ.

Kappakkhaya, saṃvaṭṭa, yuganta, and palaya are synonyms for the end of an aeon. Alakkhī and kālakaṇṇī are feminine synonyms for ill-luck. Lakkhī and siri are feminine synonyms for good luck.

83.

83.

Danu dānavamātā tha, devamātā panā’diti;

Danu is the mother of the Dānavas, while Aditi is the mother of the devas.

84.

84.

Pāpañca kibbisaṃ verā, ghaṃ duccarita dukkaṭaṃ;

Apuññā’kusalaṃ kaṇhaṃ, kalusaṃ duritā’gu ca.

Pāpa, kibbisa, vera, agha, duccarita, dukkaṭa, apuñña, akusala, kaṇha, kalusa, durita, and āgu are synonyms for evil or demerit.

85.

85.

Kusalaṃ [M 0.0009] sukataṃ sukkaṃ, puññaṃ dhamma manitthiyaṃ;

Sucarita matho diṭṭha, dhammikaṃ ihalokikaṃ.

Kusala, sukata, sukka, puñña, and dhamma are neuter synonyms for merit. Sucarita, diṭṭhadhammika, and ihalokika are synonyms for good conduct or that which pertains to this world.

86.

86.

Sandiṭṭhika matho pāra, lokikaṃ samparāyikaṃ;

Takkālaṃ tu tadātvaṃ co,

Ttarakālo tu āyati.

And sandiṭṭhika means visible in this life; pāralokika and samparāyika mean pertaining to the next world. Takkāla and tadātva are synonyms for the present time; uttarakāla and āyati are for the future time.

87.

87.

Hāso’ ttamanatā pīti, vitti tuṭṭhi ca nāriyaṃ;

Ānando pamudā’modo, santoso nandi sammado.

Hāsa, attamanatā, pīti, vitti, and tuṭṭhi are feminine synonyms for joy. Ānanda, pamudā, āmoda, santosa, nandi, and sammada are synonyms for delight.

88.

88.

Pāmojjañca pamodo tha, sukhaṃ sātañca phāsva’tha;

Bhaddaṃ seyyo subhaṃ khemaṃ, kalyāṇaṃ maṅgalaṃ sivaṃ.

Pāmojja and pamoda are synonyms for joy. Sukha, sāta, and phāsu are synonyms for happiness and ease. Bhadda, seyya, subha, khema, kalyāṇa, maṅgala, and siva are synonyms for good or auspicious.

89.

89.

Dukkhañca kasiraṃ kicchaṃ, nīgho ca byasanaṃ aghaṃ;

Dabbe tu pāpapuññāni, tīsvākicchaṃ sukhādi ca.

Dukkha, kasira, kiccha, nīgha, byasana, and agha are synonyms for suffering. Pāpa (evil) and puñña (merit) are considered substances. Akiccha (ease), sukha (happiness), and so on, are used in the three genders.

90.

90.

Bhāgyaṃ niyati bhāgo ca, bhāgadheyyaṃ vidhī’rito;

Atho uppatti nibbatti, jāti janana mubbhavo.

Bhāgya, niyati, bhāga, bhāgadheyya, and vidhi are said to be synonyms for fortune or fate. Uppatti, nibbatti, jāti, janana, and ubbhava are synonyms for arising or birth.

91.

91.

Nimittaṃ kāraṇaṃ ṭhānaṃ, padaṃ bījaṃ nibandhanaṃ;

Nidānaṃ pabhavo hetu, sambhavo setu paccayo.

Nimitta, kāraṇa, ṭhāna, pada, bīja, nibandhana, nidāna, pabhava, hetu, sambhava, setu, and paccaya are synonyms for cause or origin.

92.

92.

Kāraṇaṃ yaṃ samāsannaṃ, padaṭṭhānanti taṃ mataṃ;

Jīvo tu puriso’ttā tha, padhānaṃ pakatitthiyaṃ.

A cause that is proximate is considered a proximate cause (padaṭṭhāna). Jīva, purisa, and attā are synonyms for living being or self. Padhāna and pakati are feminine synonyms for nature or primary matter.

93.

93.

Pāṇo sarīrī bhūtaṃ vā, satto dehī ca puggalo;

Jīvo pāṇī pajā jantu,

Jano loko tathāgato.

Pāṇa, sarīrī, bhūta, satta, dehī, puggalo, jīva, pāṇī, pajā, jantu, jana, loka, and tathāgata are synonyms for a living being or person.

94.

94.

Rūpaṃ saddo gandha rasā, phasso dhammo ca gocarā;

Ālambā visayā te chā, rammaṇā lambaṇāni ca.

Form, sound, smell, taste, touch, and mental objects are sense objects (gocara). These six are also called ārammaṇa, visaya, and ālambaṇa.

95.

95.

Sukko goro sito’dātā,

Dhavalo seta, paṇḍarā;

Soṇo tu lohito ratto,

Tamba mañjiṭṭha rohitā.

Sukka, gora, sita, odāta, dhavala, seta, and paṇḍara are synonyms for white. Soṇa, lohita, ratta, tamba, mañjiṭṭha, and rohita are synonyms for red.

96.

96.

Nīlo kaṇhā’sitā kāḷo,

Mecako sāma sāmalā;

Sitapītetu paṇḍu’tto, īsaṃpaṇḍutu dhūsaro.

Nīla, kaṇha, asita, kāḷa, mecaka, sāma, and sāmala are synonyms for blue or dark. Sitapīta, paṇḍu, īsaṃpaṇḍu, and dhūsara are synonyms for pale or greyish.

97.

97.

Aruṇo [M 0.0010] kiñciratto tha,

Pāṭalo setalohito;

Atho pīto halidyābho,

Palāso harito hari.

Aruṇa, kiñciratta, pāṭala, and setalohita are synonyms for reddish. Pīta and halidyābha are synonyms for yellow. Palāsa, harita, and hari are synonyms for green.

98.

98.

Kaḷāro kapilo nīla, pīte tha rocanappabhe;

Piṅgo pisaṅgo pyathavā, kaḷārādī tu piṅgale.

Kaḷāra and kapila are tawny. Piṅga and pisaṅga are also tawny. Or rather, the words beginning with kaḷāra are in the sense of piṅgala (tawny).

99.

99.

Kammāso sabalo citto,

Sāvo tu kaṇhapītake;

Vāccaliṅgā guṇinyete, guṇe sukkādayo pume.

Kammāsa, sabala, and citta are synonyms for spotted. Sāva is a synonym for dark-yellow. These are adjectives that agree with the gender of the noun they qualify. The words for the qualities themselves, such as sukka and so on, are masculine.

100.

100.

Naccaṃ naṭṭañca naṭanaṃ, nattanaṃ lāsanaṃ bhave;

Naccaṃ tu vāditaṃ gīta, miti nāṭumidaṃ tayaṃ.

Nacca, naṭṭa, naṭana, nattana, and lāsana are synonyms for dancing. Nacca (dance), vādita (instrumental music), and gīta (song)—these three are called nāṭa.

101.

101.

Naccaṭṭhānaṃ siyā raṅgo, bhinayo sūcyasūcanaṃ;

Aṅgahāro’ṅgavikkhepo, naṭṭako naṭako naṭo.

A dancing place is called raṅga. Abhinaya is indicating with gestures. Aṅgahāra and aṅgavikkhepa are synonyms for gestures or display of limbs. Naṭṭaka, naṭaka, and naṭa are synonyms for a dancer.

102.

102.

Siṅgāro karuṇo vīra, bbhuta hassa bhayānakā;

Santo bībhaccha ruddāni, nava nāṭyarasā ime.

Siṅgāra (the erotic), karuṇa (the compassionate), vīra (the heroic), abbhuta (the wondrous), hassa (the comic), bhayānaka (the fearful), santa (the peaceful), bībhaccha (the disgusting), and rudda (the wrathful)—these are the nine dramatic sentiments.

103.

103.

Posassa nāriyaṃ pose, itthiyā saṅgamaṃ pati;

Yā pihā esa siṅgāro, ratikīḷādikāraṇo.

The longing of a man for a woman, or of a woman for a man regarding union, is called siṅgāra (the erotic sentiment), which is the cause of love-play and so on.

104.

104.

Uttama ppakatippāyo, itthipurisahetuko;

So sambhogo viyogoti,

Siṅgāro duvidho mato.

Primarily of the highest nature, caused by man and woman, that erotic sentiment is considered twofold: union and separation.

105.

105.

Bhāsitaṃ [M 0.0011] lapitaṃ bhāsā, vohāro vacanaṃ vaco;

Utti vācā girā vāṇī, bhāratī kathitā kathā (katthaci) vacī.

Bhāsita, lapita, bhāsā, vohāra, vacana, vaco, utti, vācā, girā, vāṇī, bhāratī, kathitā, and vacī are synonyms for speech.

106.

106.

Ekākhyāto padacayo, siyā vākyaṃsakārako;

Āmeḍitanti viññeyyaṃ, dvattikkhattu mudīraṇaṃ.

A collection of words with one finite verb is a sentence. Āmeḍita is to be understood as an utterance repeated two or three times.

107.

107.

Bhaye kodhe pasaṃsāyaṃ, turite kotūhale’cchare;

Hāse soke pasāde ca, kare āmeḍitaṃ budho.

The wise use repetition in fear, anger, praise, haste, curiosity, wonder, laughter, sorrow, and joy.

108.

108.

Iru nārī yaju sāma, miti vedā tayo siyuṃ;

Ete eva tayī nārī, vedo manto sutitthiyaṃ.

The Rig, Yajur, and Sāma are the three Vedas. These same are the Trayī, a feminine term. Veda, mantra, and suti are synonyms for sacred text; suti is feminine.

109.

109.

Aṭṭhako vāmako vāma, devo caṅgīraso bhagu;

Yamadaggi ca vāsiṭṭho, bhāradvājo ca kassapo;

Vessāmittoti mantānaṃ, kattāro isayo ime.

Aṭṭhaka, Vāmaka, Vāmadeva, Aṅgirasa, Bhagu, Yamadaggi, Vāsiṭṭha, Bhāradvāja, Kassapa, and Vessāmitta—these are the sages, the composers of mantras.

110.

110.

Kappo byākaraṇaṃ joti, satthaṃ sikkhā nirutti ca;

Chandoviciti cetāni, vedaṅgāni vadanti cha.

Kappa (ritual), Byākaraṇa (grammar), Jotisatthaṃ (astronomy), Sikkhā (phonetics), Nirutti (etymology), and Chandoviciti (metrics)—these are said to be the six limbs of the Veda.

111.

111.

Itihāso purāvutta, ppabandho bhāratādiko;

Nāmappakāsakaṃ satthaṃ, rukkhādīnaṃ nighaṇḍu so.

Itihāsa is a composition of past events, such as the Bhārata and others. That Nighaṇḍu is a treatise that makes known the names of trees and so on.

112.

112.

Vitaṇḍasatthaṃ viññeyyaṃ, yaṃ taṃ lokāyataṃ iti;

Keṭubhaṃ tu kriyākappa, vikappo kavinaṃ hito.

The Vitaṇḍasattha is to be understood as that which is called Lokāyata. Keṭubha is the system of composition, and Vikappa is what is beneficial to poets.

113.

113.

Ākhyāyikopaladdhatthā, pabandhakappanā kathā;

Daṇḍanītya’tthasatthasmiṃ, vuttanto tu pavatti ca.

Ākhyāyikā, upaladdhatthā, pabandhakappanā, and kathā are synonyms for narrative or story. In the science of governance and the science of wealth, vuttanta and pavatti are synonyms for an account or event.

114.

114.

Saññā, khyā, vhā samaññā cā, bhidhānaṃ nāma mavhayo;

Nāmadheyyā’ dhivacanaṃ, paṭivākyaṃ tu uttaraṃ.

Saññā, ākhyā, avhā, samaññā, abhidhāna, nāma, avhaya, nāmadheyya, and adhivacana are synonyms for name or designation. Paṭivākya and uttara are synonyms for reply.

115.

115.

Pañho tīsva nuyogo ca, pucchā pyatha nidassanaṃ;

Upogghāto ca diṭṭhanto, tatho’dāharaṇaṃ bhave.

Pañha, anuyoga, and pucchā are synonyms for question or inquiry. Nidassana, upogghāta, diṭṭhanta, and udāharaṇa are synonyms for illustration or example.

116.

116.

Samā saṅkhepa saṃhārā, samāso saṅgaho pyatha;

Sataṃ dhārayasī tyādya, bbhakkhānaṃ tucchabhāsanaṃ.

Samāsa, saṅkhepa, saṃhāra, and saṅgaha are synonyms for summary or compendium. Then there is slander, beginning with ‘You owe one hundred,’ and empty talk.

117.

117.

Vohāro tu vivādo tha, sapanaṃ sapathopi ca;

Yaso siloko kittitthī,

Ghosanā tu’ccasaddanaṃ.

Vohāra and vivāda are synonyms for dispute. Sapana and sapatha are synonyms for oath. Yasa, siloka, and kitti are synonyms for fame; kitti is feminine. Ghosanā and uccasaddana are synonyms for loud proclamation.

118.

118.

Paṭighoso paṭiravo, tho’ paññāso vacīmukhaṃ;

Katthanā ca silāghā ca, vaṇṇanā ca nuti tthuti.

Paṭighosa and paṭirava are synonyms for echo. Upaññāsa and vacīmukha are synonyms for preface or introduction. Katthanā, silāghā, vaṇṇanā, nuti, and thuti are synonyms for praise or boasting.

119.

119.

Thomanañca thomanaṃ vā (katthaci thomanaṃ thomanā dviliṅge) pasaṃsātha, kekā nādo sikhaṇḍinaṃ;

Gajānaṃ koñcanādo tha,

Matā hesā hayaddhani.

Thomana and pasaṃsā are synonyms for praise. Kekā is the cry of peacocks. Koñcanāda is the trumpeting of elephants. Hesā is considered the neighing of horses.

120.

120.

Pariyāyo vevacanaṃ, sākacchā tu ca saṃkathā;

Upavādo cu’pakkosā, vaṇṇavādā’nuvādo ca;

Janavādā’pavādāpi, parivādo ca tulyatthā.

Pariyāya and vevacana are synonyms. Sākacchā and saṃkathā are synonyms for discussion. Upavāda, upakkosa, avaṇṇavāda, anuvāda, janavāda, apavāda, and parivāda are synonyms for reproach or criticism.

121.

121.

Khepo [M 0.0012] nindā tathā kucchā, jigucchā garahā bhave;

Nindāpubbo upārambho, paribhāsana muccate.

Khepa, nindā, kucchā, jigucchā, and garahā are synonyms for blame or censure. A reproach preceded by blame is called reviling.

122.

122.

Aṭṭhānariyavohāra, vasena yā pavattitā;

Abhivākyaṃ siyā vācā, sā vītikkamadīpanī.

Speech that proceeds by way of ignoble conduct is Abhivākya; it is that which reveals transgression.

123.

123.

Muhuṃbhāsā nulāpotha, palāpo natthikā girā;

Ādobhāsana mālāpo,

Vilāpo tu pariddavo.

Muhuṃbhāsā, anulāpa, palāpa, and natthikā girā are synonyms for idle talk or nonsense. Ādobhāsana and ālāpa are synonyms for address or conversation. Vilāpa and pariddava are synonyms for lamentation.

124.

124.

Vippalāpo virodhotti, sandesotti tu vācikaṃ;

Sambhāsanaṃ tu sallāpo, virodharahitaṃ mithu.

Vippalāpa and virodha-utti are synonyms for contradiction. Sandesa and vācika are synonyms for message. Sambhāsana and sallāpa are synonyms for conversation that is mutually free from dispute.

125.

125.

Pharusaṃ niṭṭhuraṃ vākyaṃ, manuññaṃ hadayaṅgamaṃ;

Saṃkulaṃ tu kiliṭṭhañca, pubbāparavirodhinī.

Harsh and cruel speech; pleasing and heart-touching speech; confused and corrupt speech; and speech that is self-contradictory.

126.

126.

Samudāyattharahitaṃ, abaddhamiti abandha (ka.) kittitaṃ;

Vitathaṃ tu musā cātha āhataṃ tu musātthakaṃ (katthaci, amarakose), pharusādī tiliṅgikā.

That which is devoid of collective meaning is called incoherent. Vitatha and musā are synonyms for falsehood. Pharusa (harsh) and the other preceding terms are of the three genders.

127.

127.

Sammā byayañcā vitathaṃ, saccaṃ tacchaṃ yathātathaṃ;

Tabbantā tīsva līkaṃ tva, saccaṃ micchā musā byayaṃ.

Sammā is an indeclinable. Avitatha, sacca, taccha, and yathātatha are synonyms for truth; these are adjectives in the three genders. Alīka and asacca are synonyms for falsehood. Micchā and musā are indeclinables.

128.

128.

Ravo ninādo ninado ca saddo,

Nigghosa nāda ddhanayo ca rāvo;

Ārāva saṃrāva virāva ghosā,

Ravā sutitthī sara nissanā ca.

Rava, nināda, ninada, sadda, nigghosa, nāda, dhani, rāva, ārāva, saṃrāva, virāva, ghosa, ravā, suti (feminine), sara, and nissāna are synonyms for sound or noise.

129.

129.

Vissaṭṭha mañju viññeyyā, savanīyā visārino;

Bindu gambhīra ninnādī, tyeva maṭṭhaṅgiko saro.

Clear, sweet, intelligible, pleasant to hear, unwavering, rounded, deep, and resonant—these are the eight qualities of a voice.

130.

130.

Tiracchānagatānañhi, rutaṃ vassita muccate;

Kolāhalo kalahalo,

Gītaṃ gānañca gītikā.

Ruta and vassita are said to be the cries of animals. Kolāhala and kalahala are synonyms for uproar. Gīta, gāna, and gītikā are synonyms for song.

131.

131.

Sarā satta tayo gāmā, cekavīsati mucchanā;

Tānā ṭhānāni (bahūsu) cekūnapaññāsa, iccetaṃ saramaṇḍalaṃ.

Seven notes, three scales, twenty-one modulations, and forty-nine tones—this is the system of music.

132.

132.

Usabho dhevato ceva, chajja gandhāra majjhimā;

Pañcamo ca nisādoti, satte’te gaditā sarā.

Usabha, Dhevata, Chajja, Gandhāra, Majjhima, Pañcama, and Nisāda—these are declared to be the seven notes.

133.

133.

Nadanti [M 0.0013] usabhaṃ gāvo, turagā dhevataṃ tathā;

Chajjaṃ mayūrā gandhāra, majā koñcā ca majjhimaṃ.

Cattle sound the Usabha note, and horses likewise the Dhevata; peacocks the Chajja note, goats the Gandhāra, and herons the Majjhima.

134.

134.

Pañcamaṃ parapuṭṭhādī, nisādampi ca vāraṇā;

Chajjo ca majjhimo gāmā,

Tayo sādhāraṇoti ca.

The Pañcama note is from the cuckoo and others, and the Nisāda note from elephants; Chajja and Majjhima are scales, and three are called 'common'.

135.

135.

Saresu tesu pacceke, tisso tisso hi mucchanā;

Siyuṃ tatheva tānāni ṭhānāni (bahūsu), satta satteva labbhare.

In each of those notes, there are indeed three modulations; likewise, seven melodies each are obtained.

136.

136.

Tisso duve catasso ca, catasso kamato sare;

Tisso duve catassoti, dvāvīsati sutī siyuṃ.

Three, two, and four, and four in succession in the notes; three, two, and four—thus there are twenty-two micro-intervals (suti).

137.

137.

Uccatare rave tāro, thābyatte madhure kalo;

Gambhīre tu rave mando, tārādī tīsvatho kale;

Kākalī sukhume vutto,

Kriyādisamatā layo.

In a higher sound, it is sharp (tāra); in a clearly expressed, sweet sound, it is mellow (kala); but in a deep sound, it is low (manda). The registers beginning with tāra are three, as is the mellow (kala). Kākalī is said to be subtle; laya is equality in action and so forth.

138.

138.

Vīṇā ca vallakī satta, tantī sā parivādinī;

Pokkharo doṇi vīṇāya,

Upavīṇo tu veṭhako.

The vīṇā and the vallakī with seven strings is a parivādinī; the pokkhara is the sound-box of the vīṇā; and the upavīṇa is the neck (veṭhaka).

139.

139.

Ātatañceva vitata, mātatavitataṃ ghanaṃ;

Susiraṃ ceti tūriyaṃ, pañcaṅgika mudīritaṃ.

Ātata (covered on one side), vitata (covered on both sides), ātata-vitata (bound instruments), ghana (solid), and susira (hollow)—thus the musical instruments are declared to be five-fold.

140.

140.

Ātataṃ nāma cammāva, naddhesu bheriyādisu;

Tale’kekayutaṃ kumbha, thuṇa daddarikādikaṃ.

Ātata by name are instruments bound with leather, such as kettledrums and so forth; the kumbha, thuṇa, daddarikā, and so forth are endowed with a single surface.

141.

141.

Vitataṃ co’bhayatalaṃ, tūriyaṃ murajādikaṃ;

Ātatavitataṃ sabba, vinaddhaṃ paṇavādikaṃ.

Vitata is an instrument with two surfaces, such as the muraja drum and so forth; ātata-vitata comprises all bound instruments, such as the paṇava drum and so forth.

142.

142.

Susiraṃ vaṃsasaṅkhādi, sammatālādikaṃ ghanaṃ;

Ātojjaṃ tu ca vādittaṃ, vāditaṃ vajja muccate.

Susira comprises instruments such as the bamboo flute and conch; ghana comprises instruments such as cymbals and so forth. Ātojja is also a musical instrument (vāditta); the act of playing (vāditaṃ) is called music (vajja).

143.

143.

Bherī (bheri) dundubhi vutto tha,

Mudiṅgo murajossa tu;

Āliṅga, ṅkyo, ddhakā bhedā,

Tiṇavo tu ca ḍiṇḍimo.

Bherī and dundubhi are mentioned; mudiṅga is also muraja. Āliṅga, aṅkya, and addhakā are varieties thereof; and there are also the tiṇava and ḍiṇḍima.

144.

144.

Ālambaro [M 0.0014] tu paṇavo, koṇo vīṇādivādanaṃ;

Daddarī paṭaho bheri, ppabhedā maddalādayo.

Ālambara is the paṇava; koṇa is that which plays the vīṇā and other instruments. Daddarī, paṭaha, bheri, maddala, and so forth are varieties.

145.

145.

Janappiye vimadduṭṭhe, gandhe parimalo bhave;

So tvā modo dūragāmī, vissantā tīsvito paraṃ.

A scent pleasing to people, arising from being rubbed, becomes a fragrance (parimalo); that, in turn, is a far-reaching scent (āmoda), spreading out beyond these three.

146.

146.

Iṭṭhagandho ca surabhi, sugandho ca sugandhi ca;

Pūtigandhi tu duggandho, tha vissaṃ āmagandhi yaṃ.

A pleasant scent (iṭṭhagandha) is called surabhi, sugandha, and sugandhi; a foul smell (pūtigandha) is called duggandha; and that which has a raw smell (āmagandha) is called vissa.

147.

147.

Kuṅkumañceva yavana, pupphañca tagaraṃ tathā;

Turukkhoti catujjāti, gandhā ete pakāsitā.

Saffron, yavanapuppha, and tagara, likewise turukkha—these are declared as the four kinds of scents.

148.

148.

Kasāvo nitthiyaṃ titto, madhuro lavaṇo ime;

Ambilo kaṭuko ceti, cha rasā tabbatī tisu.

Astringent, bitter, sweet, salty, sour, and pungent—these are the six tastes, present in things of three kinds.

149.

149.

Siyā phasso ca phoṭṭhabbo,

Visayī tvakkha mindriyaṃ;

Nayanaṃ tvakkhi nettañca, locanaṃ ca’cchi cakkhu ca.

Phassa and phoṭṭhabba relate to touch; visayī, akkha, and indriya relate to the senses. Nayana, akkhi, netta, locana, acchi, and cakkhu are synonyms for the eye.

150.

150.

Sotaṃ saddaggaho kaṇṇo, savanaṃ suti natthu tu;

Nāsā ca nāsikā ghānaṃ, jivhātu rasanā bhave.

Sota, saddaggaha, kaṇṇa, savana, and suti relate to the ear and hearing. Natthu, nāsā, nāsikā, and ghāna are synonyms for the nose. Jivhā is also rasanā, the tongue.

151.

151.

Sarīraṃ vapu gattañcā, ttabhāvo bondi viggaho;

Dehaṃ vā purise kāyo, thiyaṃ tanu kaḷevaraṃ.

Sarīra, vapu, gatta, attabhāva, bondi, and viggaha are synonyms for body. Deha and kāya are used for a man; tanu and kaḷevara for a woman.

152.

152.

Cittaṃ ceto mano nitthī, viññāṇaṃ hadayaṃ tathā;

Mānasaṃ dhī tu paññā ca, buddhi medhā mati muti.

Citta, ceto, and mano are synonyms for mind; likewise viññāṇa, hadaya, and mānasa. Dhī, paññā, buddhi, medhā, mati, and muti are synonyms for wisdom.

153.

153.

Bhūrī mantā ca paññāṇaṃ, ñāṇaṃ vijjā ca yoni ca;

Paṭibhāna mamoho tha, paññābhedā vipassanā.

Bhūrī, mantā, paññāṇa, ñāṇa, vijjā, and yoni are synonyms for wisdom; as are paṭibhāna and amoha. Vipassanā is a type of wisdom.

154.

154.

Sammādiṭṭhi pabhutikā, vīmaṃsā tu vicāraṇā;

Sampajaññaṃ tu nepakkaṃ, vedayitaṃ tu vedanā.

Sammādiṭṭhi and so forth are mentioned; vīmaṃsā is vicāraṇā (investigation); sampajañña is nepakka (clear comprehension); and vedayita is vedanā (feeling).

155.

155.

Takko vitakko saṅkappo,

Ppano’ hā’ yu tu jīvitaṃ;

Ekaggatā tu samatho, avikkhepo samādhi ca.

Takka, vitakka, and saṅkappa are synonyms for thought. Pāṇa (breath), āhāra (sustenance), and āyu (life-span) constitute jīvita (life). Ekaggatā is samatha (tranquility), and avikkhepa is samādhi (concentration).

156.

156.

Ussāhā’ tappa paggāhā, vāyāmo ca parakkamo;

Padhānaṃ vīriyaṃ cehā, uyyāmo ca dhiti tthiyaṃ.

Ussāha, ātappa, paggāha, vāyāma, and parakkama are synonyms for exertion; as are padhāna, vīriya, and īhā; and also uyyāma and dhiti.

157.

157.

Cattāri vīriyaṅgāni, tacassa ca nahāruno;

Avasissana maṭṭhissa, maṃsalohitasussanaṃ.

The four limbs of effort are: the remaining of skin and sinews, the remaining of bones, and the drying up of flesh and blood.

158.

158.

Ussoḷhī [M 0.0015] tva dhimattehā, sati tva nussati tthiya;

Lajjā hirī samānā tha, ottappaṃ pāpabhīrutā.

Ussoḷhī is great effort; sati is anussati (recollection). Lajjā and hirī are synonymous; ottappa is the fear of wrongdoing.

159.

159.

Majjhattatā tu’pekkhā ca, adukkhamasukhā siyā;

Cittābhogo manakkāro,

Adhimokkho tu nicchayo.

Majjhattatā is upekkhā (equanimity), which is neither painful nor pleasant. Cittābhoga is manasikāra (attention); adhimokkha is nicchaya (resolve).

160.

160.

Dayā’ nukampā kāruññaṃ, karuṇā ca anuddayā;

Thiyaṃ veramaṇī ceva, viratyā’ rati cāpyatha.

Dayā, anukampā, kāruñña, karuṇā, and anuddayā are synonyms for compassion. Veramaṇī and virati are synonyms for abstinence; there is also arati (discontent).

161.

161.

Titikkhā khanti khamanaṃ, khamā mettā tu metya’tha;

Dassanaṃ diṭṭhi laddhitthī, siddhanto samayo bhave.

Titikkhā, khanti, khamana, and khamā are synonyms for patience. Mettā is loving-kindness. Dassana, diṭṭhi, and laddhi are synonyms for view. Siddhanta is samaya (doctrine).

162.

162.

Taṇhā ca tasiṇā ejā, jālinī ca visattikā;

Chando jaṭā nikantyā’sā, sibbinī bhavanetti ca.

Taṇhā, tasiṇā, ejā, jālinī, and visattikā are synonyms for craving; chanda, jaṭā, nikanti, āsā, sibbinī, and bhavanetti are synonyms for desire.

163.

163.

Abhijjhā vanatho vānaṃ, lobho rāgo ca ālayo;

Pihā manoratho icchā, bhilāso kāma dohaḷā;

Ākaṅkhā ruci vuttā sā, tvadhikā lālasā dvisu.

Abhijjhā, vanatha, vāna, lobha, rāga, and ālayo are synonyms for greed and attachment. Pihā, manoratha, icchā, abhilāsa, kāma, and dohaḷa are synonyms for wish and desire. Ākaṅkhā and ruci are also said to be desire. Excessive desire is lālasā, which is used in two genders.

164.

164.

Veraṃ virodho viddeso, doso ca paṭighañca vā;

Kodhā’ ghātā kopa rosā,

Byāpādo’ nabhiraddhi ca.

Hatred, opposition, enmity, ill will, or aversion; anger, animosity, wrath, fury; and malevolence and discontent.

165.

165.

Baddhavera mupanāho, siyā soko tu socanaṃ;

Roditaṃ kanditaṃ ruṇṇaṃ, paridevo pariddavo.

Bound enmity is a grudge; sorrow, indeed, is lamenting. Crying, wailing, weeping; lamentation and distress.

166.

166.

Bhītitthi bhaya muttāso, bheravaṃ tu mahabbhayaṃ;

Fear, fright, and terror; dread, however, is great fear.

167.

167.

Bheravaṃ bhīsanaṃ bhīmaṃ, dāruṇañca bhayānakaṃ;

Ghoraṃ paṭibhayaṃ bhesmaṃ, bhayaṅkara mime tīsu.

Dreadful, horrific, terrible, awful, and frightening; horrible, perilous, dreadful, and fear-causing—these are in the three genders.

168.

168.

Issā usūyā maccheraṃ, tu macchariya maccharaṃ;

Moho’vijjā tathā’ñāṇaṃ, māno vidhā ca unnati.

Envy and jealousy; stinginess, avarice, and selfishness; likewise delusion, ignorance, and non-knowledge; conceit, arrogance, and haughtiness.

169.

169.

Uddhacca muddhaṭaṃ cātha uddhavaṃuddhaṃ dhāvati citta metenāti uddhavaṃ (ṭīkā), tāpo kukkuccameva ca;

Pacchātāpo nutāpo ca, vippaṭisāro pakāsito.

Restlessness and agitation; also distress and remorse. Regret, remorse, and compunction are declared.

170.

170.

Manovilekha sandehā, saṃsayo ca kathaṃkathā;

Dveḷhakaṃ vicikicchā ca, kaṅkhā saṅkā vimatyapi.

Perplexity, doubt, uncertainty, and questioning; ambiguity, doubt, uncertainty, apprehension, and also perplexity.

171.

171.

Gabbo [M 0.0016] bhimāno’haṃkāro, cintātu jhāna muccate;

Nicchayo niṇṇayo vutto, paṭiññā tu paṭissavo.

Pride, conceit, and egoism; but thought is called absorption. Certainty is called decision; a promise, however, is an affirmation.

172.

172.

Avamānaṃ tirakkāro, paribhavo pya’ nādaro;

Parābhavo pya’ vaññā tha, ummādo cittavibbhamo.

Disrespect, reproach, contempt, and irreverence; disgrace and also disdain; madness is mental confusion.

173.

173.

Pemaṃ sineho sneho tha, cittapīḷā visaññitā;

Pamādo sativossaggo, kotūhalaṃ kutūhalaṃ.

Love, affection, and fondness; mental distress is bewilderment. Heedlessness is the abandonment of mindfulness; curiosity is excitement.

174.

174.

Vilāso lalitaṃ līlā, hāvo heḷā ca vibbhamo;

Iccādikā siyuṃ nāri, siṅgārabhāvajā kiriyā.

Charm, grace, playfulness, coquetry, flirtation, and amorous gestures; these and the like are actions of a woman born from the amorous sentiment.

175.

175.

Hasanaṃ hasitaṃ hāso, mando so mihitaṃ sitaṃ;

Aṭṭahāso mahāhāso,

Romañco lomahaṃsanaṃ.

Laughing, a laugh, and laughter; a gentle one is a smile. Loud laughter is great laughter; horripilation is hair-raising.

176.

176.

Parihāso davo khiḍḍā, keḷi kīḷā ca kīḷitaṃ;

Niddā tu supinaṃ soppaṃ, middhañca pacalāyikā.

Jesting, sport, play, amusement, sport, and playing; but sleep, dreaming, slumber, torpor, and drowsiness.

177.

177.

Thiyaṃ nikati kūṭañca, dambho sāṭhyañca ketavaṃ;

Sabhāvo tu nissaggo ca, sarūpaṃ pakatitthiyaṃ.

Deceit (feminine), fraud, hypocrisy, craftiness, and cheating; but nature is also disposition; own form is nature (feminine).

178.

178.

Sīlañca lakkhaṇaṃ bhāvo,

Ussavo tu chaṇo maho mato (ṭī.).

Conduct, characteristic, and nature; a festival, however, is a celebration and a great fete.

179.

179.

Dhārento jantu sasneha, mabhidhānappadīpikaṃ;

Khuddakādyatthajātāni khuddakānyatthajātāni (ka.), sampassati yathāsukhaṃ.

A person who, with affection, masters the Lamp of Nouns, clearly sees, at his pleasure, the various meanings of minor things and so forth.

Saggakaṇḍo niṭṭhito.

The Celestial Section is concluded.

2. Bhūkaṇḍa

2. The Earth Section

1. Bhūmivagga

1. The Chapter on the Earth.

180.

180.

Vaggā bhūmi, purī, macca, catubbaṇṇa, vanādihi;

Pātālena ca vuccante, saṅgo’paṅgehi’dha’kkamā.

Here, the chapters are told in sequence concerning the earth, cities, mortals, the four castes, forests and so forth, and the netherworld, with their main and minor divisions.

181.

181.

Vasundharā chamā bhūmi, pathavī medanī mahī;

Ubbī vasumatī go ku, vasudhā dharaṇī dharā;

Puthavī jagatī bhūrī, bhū ca bhūtadharā’ vanī.

Vasundharā, Chamā, Bhūmi, Pathavī, Medanī, Mahī, Ubbī, Vasumatī, Go, Ku, Vasudhā, Dharaṇī, Dharā, Puthavī, Jagatī, Bhūrī, Bhū, Bhūtadharā, and Avanī are names for the earth.

182.

182.

Khārā [M 0.0017] tu mattikā ūso, ūsavā tūsaro tisu;

Thalaṃ thalītthī bhūbhāge, thaddhalūkhamhi jaṅgalo.

Saline soil is called ūsa; ūsava and ūsara are in the three genders. Thala and thalī (feminine) refer to a region of dry land; jaṅgala refers to a hard and rough place.

183.

183.

Pubbavideho cāpara, goyānaṃ jambudīpo ca;

Uttarakuru ceti siyuṃ, cattārome mahādīpā.

Pubbavideha, Aparagoyāna, Jambudīpa, and Uttarakuru—these are said to be the four great continents.

184.

184.

Pumbahutte kurū sakkā, kosalā magadhā sivī;

Kaliṅgā’vanti pañcālā, vajjī gandhāra cetayo.

Kurū, Sakkā, Kosalā, Magadhā, Sivī, Kaliṅgā, Avanti, Pañcālā, Vajjī, Gandhāra, and Cetayo are in the masculine plural.

185.

185.

Vaṅgā videhā kambojā, maddā bhagga’ṅga sīhaḷā;

Kasmīrā kāsi paṇḍavādī, siyuṃ janapadantarā.

Vaṅgā, Videhā, Kambojā, Maddā, Bhaggā, Aṅga, Sīhaḷā, Kasmīrā, Kāsi, Paṇḍava, and so forth, are other countries.

186.

186.

Loko ca bhuvanaṃ vuttaṃ, deso tu visayo pyatha;

Milakkhadeso paccanto, majjhadeso tu majjhimo.

Loka and Bhuvana are said to be the world; a country is also a region. A barbarian land is a borderland; the middle country, however, is the central region.

187.

187.

Anūpo salilappāyo, kacchaṃ puma napuṃsake;

Saddalo harite dese, tiṇenā, bhinavena hi.

An anūpa is a watery region; kaccha is masculine and neuter. A saddala is a green region, indeed one with fresh grass.

188.

188.

Nadyambujīvano deso, vuṭṭhinippajjasassako;

Yo nadīmātiko deva,

Mātiko ca kamena so.

A land sustained by rivers and water, and having crops produced by rain, is called nadīmātika and devamātika respectively.

189.

189.

Tīsvanūpādyatho canda, sūrādo sassatīrito;

Raṭṭhaṃ tu vijitañcātha, purise setu āliyaṃ.

Anūpa and so forth are in the three genders; sassatīrita is like canda, sūra, and so on. A kingdom is raṭṭha and vijita; setu and āli are a dam, in the masculine.

190.

190.

Upāntabhū parisaro, goṭṭhaṃ tu gokulaṃ vajo;

Maggo pantho patho addhā, añjasaṃ vaṭumaṃ tathā.

Upāntabhū and Parisara mean vicinity; Goṭṭha, Gokula, and Vaja mean a cow-pen; likewise Magga, Pantha, Patha, Addhā, Añjasa, and Vaṭuma mean a path.

191.

191.

Pajjo pajjā…. (ka.), yanañca padavī, vattanī paddhatitthiyaṃ;

Tabbhedā jaṅgha, sakaṭa, maggā te ca mahaddhani.

Pajja, Yāna, Padavī, Vattanī, and Paddhati, which is feminine, are synonyms for a path; their distinctions are Jaṅgha, a footpath; Sakaṭa, a cart-road; and Magga, a main road; they are also called Mahaddhani.

192.

192.

Ekapadyekapadike, kantāro tu ca duggame;

Ekapadī and Ekapadikā are a single path; Kantāra is a wilderness, indeed hard to traverse.

193.

193.

Paṭimaggo paṭipatho, addhānaṃ dīgha mañjasaṃ;

Suppatho tu supantho ca, uppathaṃ tvapathaṃ bhave.

Paṭimagga and Paṭipatha are a counter-path; Addhāna is a long path, and Añjasa is a direct path; Suppatha and Supantha are a good path; Uppatha and Apatha are a wrong path.

194.

194.

Chattiṃsaparamāṇūna, meko ṇu ca chattiṃsa te;

Tajjārī tāpi chattiṃsa, rathareṇu chattiṃsa te.

Thirty-six ultimate particles make one atom; thirty-six atoms make one speck of dust; and thirty-six specks of dust make one chariot-wheel dust particle.

195.

195.

Likkhātā satta ūkā tā, dhaññamāsoti satta te;

Satta ṅgula’ mamu dviccha, vidatthi tā duve siyuṃ.

Seven likkhā make one ūkā; seven ūkā make one dhaññamāsa; seven aṅgula make one mū; and two mū make one vidatthi.

196.

196.

Ratanaṃ [M 0.0018] tāni satteva, yaṭṭhi tā vīsatū sabhaṃ;

Gāvuta musabhāsīti, yojanaṃ catugāvutaṃ.

Seven of the preceding units make one ratana; twenty yaṭṭhi make one usabha; eighty usabha make one gāvuta; and four gāvuta make one yojana.

197.

197.

Dhanupañcasataṃ koso, karīsaṃ caturambaṇaṃ;

Abbhantaraṃ tu hatthāna, maṭṭhavīsa pamāṇato.

Five hundred dhanu make one kosa; four ambaṇa make one karīsa; and the measurement of an abbhantara is twenty-eight cubits.

Bhūmivaggo niṭṭhito.

The Chapter on Earth is concluded.

2. Bhūkaṇḍa

2. The Earth Section

2. Puravagga

2. The Chapter on Cities.

198.

198.

Puraṃ nagara mitthī vā, ṭhānīyaṃ puṭabhedanaṃ;

Thiyaṃ tu rājadhānī rājaṭhānī (ṭī.) ca, khandhāvāro bhave tha ca.

Pura and Nagara, which can be feminine, are synonyms for a city; Ṭhānīya and Puṭabhedana are a market town; Rājadhānī, which is feminine, is a capital city; and Khandhāvāra is a military camp.

199.

199.

Sākhānagara maññatra, yaṃ taṃ mūlapurā puraṃ;

Bārāṇasī ca sāvatthi, vesālī mithilā, ḷavī.

Sākhānagara is a branch city, distinct from the root city; Bārāṇasī, Sāvatthī, Vesālī, Mithilā, and Āḷavī are cities.

200.

200.

Kosambu, jjeniyo takka, silā campā ca sāgalaṃ;

Susumāragiraṃ saṃsumāra (ṭīkā) rāja, gahaṃ kapilavatthu ca.

Kosambī, Ujjenī, Takkasilā, Campā, Sāgala, Susumāragira, Rājagaha, and Kapilavatthu are cities.

201.

201.

Sāketa, mindapatthañco, kkaṭṭhā pāṭaliputtakaṃ;

Jetuttarañca saṅkassaṃ, kusinārādayo purī.

Sāketa, Indapattha, Ukkaṭṭhā, Pāṭaliputta, Jetuttara, Saṅkassa, Kusinārā, and so forth, are cities.

202.

202.

Racchā ca visikhā vuttā, rathikā vīthi cāpyatha;

Byūho racchā anibbiddhā, nibbiddhā tu pathaddhi ca.

Racchā, Visikhā, Rathikā, and Vīthi are said to be a street; Byūha is a continuous street, and Pathaddhi is an interrupted street.

203.

203.

Catukkaṃ caccare magga, sandhi siṅghāṭakaṃ bhave;

Pākāro varaṇo cātha, udāpo uddāpa, uddāpa upakārikā.

Catukka, Caccara, Maggasandhi, and Siṅghāṭaka are a crossroads; Pākāra and Varaṇa are a rampart; Udāpa is a well, and Upakārikā is an outbuilding.

204.

204.

Kuṭṭaṃ tu bhitti nārī tha, gopuraṃ dvārakoṭṭhako;

Esikā indakhīlo ca, aṭṭo tvaṭṭālako bhave.

Kuṭṭa and Bhitti, which is feminine, are a wall; Gopura and Dvārakoṭṭhaka are a gateway tower; Esikā and Indakhīla are a threshold pillar; Aṭṭa and Aṭṭālaka are a watchtower.

205.

205.

Toraṇaṃ tu bahidvāraṃ, parikhātu ca dīghikā;

Mandiraṃ sadanā, gāraṃ, nikāyo nilayā, layo.

Toraṇa and Bahidvāra are an outer gate; Parikhā and Dīghikā are a moat; Mandira, Sadana, Āgāra, Nikāya, Nilaya, and Laya are a dwelling.

206.

206.

Āvāso bhavanaṃ vesmaṃ, niketanaṃ nivesanaṃ;

Gharaṃ gahañcā, vasatho, saraṇañca patissayo.

Āvāsa, Bhavana, Vesma, Niketana, Nivesana, Ghara, Gaha, Avasatha, Saraṇa, and Patissaya are a dwelling.

207.

207.

Okaṃ sālā khayo vāso, thiyaṃ kuṭi vasatya’pi;

Gehañcā, nitthi sadumaṃ, cetiyā, yatanāni tu.

Oka, Sālā, Khaya, and Vāsa are a dwelling; Kuṭi and Vasati, in the feminine gender, are a hut; Geha and Saduma, in the neuter gender, are a house; Cetiya and Āyatana are a shrine.

208.

208.

Pāsādo [M 0.0019] ceva yūpo tha, muṇḍacchado ca hammiyaṃ;

Yūpotu gajakumbhamhi, hatthinakho patiṭṭhito.

Pāsāda, Yūpa, Muṇḍacchada, and Hammiya are a mansion; Yūpa also refers to the frontal boss of an elephant; Hatthinakha is a type of pillar.

209.

209.

Supaṇṇavaṅkacchadana, maḍḍhayogo siyā tha ca;

Ekakūṭayuto māḷo,

Pāsādo caturassako.

Aḍḍhayoga and Supaṇṇavaṅkacchadana are a building with a curved roof; Ekakūṭayuta Māḷa is a single-peaked mansion; and Caturassaka Pāsāda is a four-cornered palace.

210.

210.

Sabhāyañca sabhā cātha, maṇḍapaṃ vā janālayo;

Atho āsanasālāyaṃ, paṭikkamana mīritaṃ.

Sabhā, Maṇḍapa, and Janālaya are an assembly hall or pavilion; Āsanasālā is a hall for seating, and Paṭikkamana is said to be a place for retreating.

211.

211.

Jinassa vāsabhavana, mitthī gandhakuṭi pyatha;

Thiyaṃ rasavatī pāka, ṭṭhānañceva mahānasaṃ.

The dwelling place of the Jina is the Gandhakuṭi, or perfumed chamber, which is feminine; Rasavatī, which is feminine, Pākaṭṭhāna, and Mahānasa are synonyms for a kitchen.

212.

212.

Āvesanaṃ sippasālā, soṇḍā tu pānamandiraṃ;

Vaccaṭṭhānaṃ vaccakuṭi, munīnaṃ ṭhāna massamo.

Āvesana and Sippasālā are a workshop; Soṇḍā and Pānamandira are a tavern; Vaccaṭṭhāna and Vaccakuṭi are a privy; and an Assama is the dwelling of sages.

213.

213.

Paṇyavikkayasālā ku, āpaṇo paṇyavīthikā;

Udosito bhaṇḍasālā, caṅkamanaṃ tu caṅkamo.

Paṇyavikkayasālā, Āpaṇa, and Paṇyavīthikā are a marketplace or shop; Udosita and Bhaṇḍasālā are a storehouse; and Caṅkamana and Caṅkama are a walking path.

214.

214.

Jantāgharaṃ tvaggisālā, papā pānīyasālikā;

Gabbho ovarako vāsā, gāraṃ tu sayaniggahaṃ.

Jantāghara and Aggisālā are a hot-bath house; Papā and Pānīyasālikā are a water-shed; Gabbha and Ovaraka are an inner chamber; and Vāsāgāra and Sayaniggaha are a sleeping chamber.

215.

215.

Itthāgāraṃ tu orodho, suddhanto’ ntepurampi ca;

Asabbavisayaṭṭhānaṃ, raññaṃ kacchantaraṃ mataṃ.

Itthāgāra, Orodha, Suddhanta, and Antepura are the women's quarters or inner palace; Kacchantara is considered the king's inner chamber, a place inaccessible to all.

216.

216.

Sopāno vā’rohaṇañca,

Nisseṇī sā, dhirohiṇī;

Vātapānaṃ gavakkho ca, jālañca sīhapañjaraṃ.

Sopāna and Ārohaṇa are a staircase; Nisseṇī, which is feminine, and Adhirohiṇī are a ladder; Vātapāna, Gavakkha, Jāla, and Sīhapañjara are a window.

217.

217.

Ālokasandhi vutto tha, laṅgī’tthī paligho bhave;

Kapisīso, ggalatthambho, nibbaṃ tu chaddakoṭiyaṃ.

Ālokasandhi is said to be a light gap; Laṅgī, which is feminine, and Paligha are a bar or bolt; Kapisīsa and Aggalatthambha are a latch or door-bar post; and Nibba is the end of a roof.

218.

218.

Chadanaṃ paṭalaṃ chadda, majiraṃ caccaro, ṅgaṇaṃ;

Paghāno paghanā, lindo, pamukhaṃ dvārabandhanaṃ.

Chadana, Paṭala, and Chadda are a roof covering; Ajira, Caccara, and Aṅgaṇa are a courtyard; Paghāna, Paghanā, and Alinda are a porch or veranda; and Pamukha and Dvārabandhana are a door-frame.

219.

219.

Piṭṭhasaṅghāṭakaṃ dvāra, bāhā kūṭaṃ tu kaṇṇikā;

Dvārañca paṭihāro tha, ummāro dehanī, tthiyaṃ.

Piṭṭhasaṅghāṭaka and Dvārabāhā are a door panel; Kūṭa and Kaṇṇikā are a pinnacle or roof-peak; Dvāra and Paṭihāra are a door or gate; and Ummāra and Dehalī are a threshold (the latter being feminine).

220.

220.

Eḷako indakhīlo tha, thambho thūṇo pumitthiyaṃ;

Pāṭikā, ḍḍhendupāsāṇe, giñjakā tu ca iṭṭhakā.

Eḷaka, Indakhīla, Thambha, and Thūṇa are a pillar (Thūṇa being masculine or feminine); Pāṭikā and Aḍḍhendupāsāṇa are a paving stone; and Giñjakā and Iṭṭhakā are a brick.

221.

221.

Valabhicchādidārumhi, vaṅke gopānasī, tthiyaṃ;

Kapotapālikāyaṃ tu, viṭaṅko nitthiyaṃ bhave.

Valabhi is a pinnacle roof timber; Gopānasī is a curved rafter (feminine); Kapotapālikā and Viṭaṅka are a cornice or pigeon-hole (Viṭaṅka is non-feminine).

222.

222.

Kuñcikāvivaraṃ [M 0.0020] tāḷa, cchiggalo pyatha kuñcikā;

Tāḷo’vāpuraṇaṃ cātha, vedikā vedi kathyate.

Kuñcikāvivara and Tāḷacchiggala are a keyhole; Kuñcikā is a key; Tāḷa and Avāpuraṇa are a lock; and Vedikā and Vedi are a railing.

223.

223.

Saṅghāto pakkhapāso ca, mandiraṅgā tulā api;

Thiyaṃ sammujjanī ceva, sammajjanī ca sodhanī.

Saṅghāta is a door-post and Pakkhapāsa is a side-wall; Mandiraṅga is a part of a building, and Tulā is a beam; Sammujjanī, Sammajjanī, and Sodhanī are a broom (feminine).

224.

224.

Saṅkaṭīraṃ tu saṅkāra, ṭṭhānaṃ saṅkārakūṭakaṃ;

Atho kacavaro, klāpo, saṅkāro ca kasambupi.

Saṅkaṭīra, Saṅkāraṭṭhāna, and Saṅkārakūṭaka are a rubbish heap; Kacavara, Kalāpa, Saṅkāra, and Kasambu are refuse or sweepings.

225.

225.

Gharādibhūmi taṃ vatthu, gāmo saṃvasatho tha so;

Pākaṭo nigamo bhoga, maccādibhyo dhi tūdito ‘adhibhū’ti īrito kathito (ṭī.).

Gharādibhūmi and Vatthu are a building site; Gāma and Saṃvasatha are a village; Nigama is a market town; and Bhoga is a fief granted by a king or other authority.

226.

226.

Sīmā ca mariyādā tha,

Ghoso gopālagāmakoti.

Sīmā and Mariyādā are a boundary; Ghosa is a cowherd village.

Puravaggo niṭṭhito.

The Chapter on Cities is concluded.

3. Naravagga

3. The Chapter on Humankind

227.

227.

Manusso mānuso macco, mānavo manujo naro;

Poso pumā ca puriso,

Poriso pyatha paṇḍito.

Manussa, mānusa, macca, mānava, manuja, nara, posa, pumā, purisa, and porisa are terms for a human being; paṇḍita is a wise person.

228.

228.

Budho vidvā vibhāvī ca, santo sappañña kovidā;

Dhīmā sudhī kavi byatto, vicakkhaṇo visārado.

Budha, vidvā, vibhāvī, santa, sappañña, kovida, dhīmā, sudhī, kavi, byatta, vicakkhaṇa, and visārada are terms for a wise, learned, or discerning person.

229.

229.

Medhāvī matimā pañño, viññū ca vidūro vidū;

Dhīro vipassī dosaññū, buddho ca dabba viddasu.

Medhāvī, matimā, pañña, viññū, vidūra, vidū, dhīra, vipassī, dosaññū, buddha, dabba, and viddasu are terms for an intelligent, wise, or discerning person.

230.

230.

Itthī sīmantinī nārī, thī vadhū vanitā, ṅganā;

Pamadā sundarī kantā, ramaṇī dayitā, balā.

Itthī, sīmantinī, nārī, thī, vadhū, vanitā, aṅganā, pamadā, sundarī, kantā, ramaṇī, dayitā, and balā are terms for a woman.

231.

231.

Mātugāmo ca mahilā, lalanā bhīru kāminī;

Kumārikā tu kaññā tha, yuvatī taruṇī bhave.

Mātugāma, mahilā, lalanā, bhīru (a timid woman), and kāminī (a passionate woman) are terms for a woman. Kumārikā and kaññā are terms for a maiden. Yuvatī and taruṇī are terms for a young woman.

232.

232.

Mahesī sābhisekāññā,

Bhoginī rājanāriyo;

Dhavatthinī tu saṅketaṃ, yāti yā sā, bhisārikā.

Mahesī is a consecrated queen; Bhoginī is another royal consort. She who goes to a rendezvous to meet a lover is called an Abhisārikā.

233.

233.

Gaṇikā [M 0.0021] vesiyā vaṇṇa, dāsī nagarasobhinī;

Rūpūpajīvinī vesī, kulaṭā tu ca bandhakī.

Gaṇikā, vesiyā, vaṇṇadāsī, nagarasobhinī, rūpūpajīvinī, and vesī are terms for a courtesan or prostitute. Kulaṭā and bandhakī are terms for an unchaste woman.

234.

234.

Varāroho, ttamā matta, kāsinī varavaṇṇinī;

Patibbatā tvapi satī, kulitthī kulapālikā.

Varārohā, uttamā, mattakāsinī, and varavaṇṇinī are terms for an excellent or beautiful woman. Patibbatā, satī, kulitthī, and kulapālikā are terms for a devoted, virtuous, or high-born woman.

235.

235.

Vidhavā patisuññā tha, patimbarā sayambarā;

Vijātā tu pasūtā ca, jātāpaccā pasūtikā.

Vidhavā and patisuññā are terms for a widow. Patimbarā and sayambarā are terms for a woman who chooses her own husband. Vijātā, pasūtā, jātāpaccā, and pasūtikā are terms for a woman who has given birth.

236.

236.

Dūtī sañcārikā dāsī, tu ceṭī kuṭadhārikā;

Vāruṇī, kkhaṇikā tulyā, khattiyānī tu khattiyā.

Dūtī, sañcārikā, dāsī, ceṭī, and kuṭadhārikā are terms for a female messenger or servant. Vāruṇī, akkhaṇikā, and tulyā are also terms for women. Khattiyānī and khattiyā are terms for a Khattiya woman.

237.

237.

Dāro jāyā kalattañca, gharaṇī bhariyā piyā,

Pajāpatī ca dutiyā, sā pādaparicārikā.

Dāra, jāyā, kalatta, gharaṇī, bhariyā, piyā, pajāpatī (mistress of the house), dutiyā (companion), and pādaparicārikā (one who serves at the feet) are terms for a wife.

238.

238.

Sakhī tvā’lī vayassā tha, jārī cevā’ticārinī;

Pume tū’tu rajo pupphaṃ, utunī tu rajassalā.

Sakhī, ālī, and vayassā are terms for a female friend. Jārī and aticārinī are terms for an adulteress. Utu, raja, and puppha are terms for menstruation. Utunī and rajassalā are terms for a menstruating woman.

239.

239.

Pupphavatī garugabbhā, pannasattā ca gabbhinī;

Gabbhāsayo jalābupi, kalalaṃ punnapuṃsake.

Pupphavatī, garugabbhā, āpannasattā, and gabbhinī are terms for a pregnant woman; gabbhāsaya is the womb, and jalābu is the placenta; kalala is the embryo (and is masculine or neuter).

240.

240.

Dhavotu sāmiko bhattā, kanto pati varo piyo;

Atho papati jāro thā,

Paccaṃ putto’trajo suto.

Dhava, sāmika, bhattā, kanta, pati, vara, and piya are terms for a husband; papati is a wicked husband, and jāra is a paramour. Apacca, putta, atraja, and suta are terms for offspring or a son.

241.

241.

Tanujo tanayo sūnu, puttādī dhītari’tthiyaṃ;

Nāriyaṃ duhitā dhītā,

Sajāto tvo’raso suto.

Tanuja, tanaya, and sūnu are terms for a son. The feminine of putta and similar terms means daughter. Duhitā and dhītā are also terms for a daughter. Sajāta and orasa suta are terms for a legitimate son.

242.

242.

Jāyāpatī janipatī, jayampatī tu dampatī dampatīti padaṃ pulliṅga bahuvacanantaṃ ikārantaṃ, tudampati (ṭī.);

Atha vassavaro vutto, paṇḍako ca napuṃsakaṃ.

Jāyāpatī, jayampatī, and dampatī are terms for a husband and wife, or a couple; janipati is a lord of people. Vassavara, paṇḍaka, and napuṃsaka are terms for a eunuch or one of neuter gender.

243.

243.

Bandhavo bandhu sajano, sagotto ñāti ñātako;

Sālohito sapiṇḍo ca,

Tāto tu janako pitā.

Bandhava, bandhu, sajana, sagotta, ñāti, ñātaka, sālohita, and sapiṇḍa are terms for a relative or kinsman. Tāta, janaka, and pitā are terms for a father.

244.

244.

Amma, mbā jananī mātā, janetti janikā bhave;

Upamātā tu dhāti’tthī,

Sālo jāyāya bhātiko.

Ammā, ambā, jananī, mātā, janettī, and janikā are terms for a mother. Upamātā is a foster-mother, and dhātī is a wet nurse. Sāla is a wife's brother (jāyāya bhātika).

245.

245.

Nanandā [M 0.0022] sāmibhaginī, mātāmahī tu ayyikā;

Mātulo mātubhātā,ssa, mātulānī pajāpati.

Nanandā is a husband's sister (sāmibhaginī). Mātāmahī and ayyikā are terms for a maternal grandmother. Mātula is a maternal uncle (mātubhātā); his wife is a mātulānī.

246.

246.

Jāyāpatīnaṃ jananī, sassu vuttātha tappitā;

Sasuro bhāgineyyotu, putto bhaginiyā bhave.

`Sassu` is a mother-in-law, the mother of one's spouse. `Sasuro` is a father-in-law. `Bhāgineyyo` and `bhaginiyā putto` are terms for a nephew, a sister's son.

247.

247.

Nattā vutto paputto tha, sāmibhātā tu devaro;

Dhītupati tu jāmātā,

Ayyako tu pitāmaho.

`Nattā` and `paputto` are terms for a grandson. `Sāmibhātā` and `devaro` are terms for a husband's brother. `Dhītupati` and `jāmātā` are terms for a son-in-law. `Ayyako` and `pitāmaho` are terms for a grandfather.

248.

248.

Mātucchā mātubhaginī, pitucchā bhaginī pitu;

Papitāmaho payyako,

Suṇhā tu suṇisā husā.

`Mātucchā` and `mātubhaginī` are terms for a maternal aunt (mother's sister). `Pitucchā` and `pitu bhaginī` are terms for a paternal aunt (father's sister). `Papitāmaho` and `payyako` are terms for a great-grandfather. `Suṇhā`, `suṇisā`, and `husā` are terms for a daughter-in-law.

249.

249.

Sodariyo sagabbho ca, sodaro sahajo pyatha;

Mātāpitū te pitaro, puttā tu puttadhītaro.

`Sodariyo`, `sagabbho`, `sodaro`, and `sahajo` are terms for a brother. `Mātāpitū` and `pitaro` are terms for parents. `Puttā` and `puttadhītaro` are terms for children (sons and daughters).

250.

250.

Sasurā sassusasurā, bhātubhaginī bhātaro;

Bālattaṃ bālatā bālyaṃ, yobbaññaṃtu ca yobbanaṃ.

`Sasurā` and `sassusasurā` are terms for parents-in-law. `Bhātubhaginī` and `bhātaro` are terms for siblings (brothers and sisters). `Bālattaṃ`, `bālatā`, and `bālyaṃ` are terms for childhood. `Yobbaññaṃ` and `yobbanaṃ` are terms for youth.

251.

251.

Sukkā tu palitaṃ kesā, dayo tha jaratā jarā;

Puthuko pillako chāpo, kumāro bāla potakā.

`Palitaṃ` is a term for white or gray hair. `Jaratā` and `jarā` are terms for old age. `Puthuko`, `pillako`, `chāpo`, `kumāro`, `bāla`, and `potakā` are terms for a young child or boy.

252.

252.

Athu’ ttānasayu’ttāna, seyyakā thanapopi ca;

`Uttānasayu`, `uttānaseyyakā`, and `thanapo` are terms for an infant lying on its back or a suckling.

253.

253.

Taruṇo ca vayaṭṭho ca, daharo ca yuvā susu;

Māṇavodārakocātha, sukumāro sukhedhito.

`Taruṇo`, `vayaṭṭho`, `daharo`, `yuvā`, and `susu` are terms for a young man or youth. `Māṇavo` and `dārako` are terms for a boy or young man. `Sukumāro` and `sukhedhito` are terms for one who is tender or well-nurtured.

254.

254.

Mahallako ca vuddho ca, thero jiṇṇo ca jiṇṇako;

Aggajo pubbajo jeṭṭho, kaniyo kaniṭṭho nujo.

`Mahallako`, `vuddho`, `thero`, `jiṇṇo`, and `jiṇṇako` are terms for an old or aged person. `Aggajo`, `pubbajo`, and `jeṭṭho` are terms for an elder or first-born. `Kaniyo`, `kaniṭṭho`, and `anujo` are terms for a younger person.

255.

255.

Valittaco tu valino; Tīsu’ttānasayādayo;

`Valittaco` and `valino` are terms for a person with wrinkled skin. The phrase `tīsu uttānasayādayo` indicates that terms such as `uttānasaya` apply in all three genders.

256.

256.

Sīso’ttamaṅgāni siro, muddhā ca matthako bhave;

Keso tu kuntalo vālo, ttamaṅgaruha muddhajā.

`Sīsaṃ`, `uttamaṅgaṃ`, `siro`, `muddhā`, and `matthako` are terms for the head. `Keso`, `kuntalo`, `vālo`, `uttamaṅgaruha`, and `muddhajā` are terms for hair.

257.

257.

Dhammillo saṃyatā kesā,

Kākapakkho sikhaṇḍako;

Pāso hattho kesacaye;

Tāpasānaṃ tahiṃ jaṭā.

`Dhammillo` is a term for braided or bound hair (`saṃyatā kesā`). `Kākapakkho` and `sikhaṇḍako` are terms for a side-lock or topknot. `Pāso` and `hattho` are terms for a mass of hair (`kesacayo`). `Jaṭā` are the matted locks of ascetics.

258.

258.

Thiyaṃ veṇī paveṇī ca;

Atho cūḷā sikhā siyā;

Sīmanto tu mato nāri, kesamajjhamhi paddhati.

For a woman, `veṇī` and `paveṇī` are terms for a braid of hair. `Cūḷā` and `sikhā` are terms for a topknot or crest. `Sīmanto` is the parting of the hair on a woman's head.

259.

259.

Lomaṃ [M 0.0023] tanuruhaṃ romaṃ, pamhaṃ pakhuma makkhigaṃ;

Massu vuttaṃ pumamukhe, bhū tvitthī bhamuko bhamu.

`Lomaṃ`, `tanuruhaṃ`, and `romaṃ` are terms for body hair. `Pamhaṃ`, `pakhumaṃ`, and `akkhigaṃ` are terms for eyelashes. `Massu` is the beard on a man's face. `Bhū`, `bhamuko`, and `bhamu` are terms for eyebrows.

260.

260.

Bappo khappo (ṭī.) nettajala’ssūni, nettatārā kanīnikā;

Vadanaṃ tu mukhaṃ tuṇḍaṃ, vattaṃ lapana mānanaṃ.

`Bappo`, `nettajalaṃ`, and `assu` are terms for tears. `Nettatārā` and `kanīnikā` are terms for the pupil of the eye. `Vadanaṃ`, `mukhaṃ`, `tuṇḍaṃ`, `vattaṃ`, `lapanaṃ`, and `ānanaṃ` are terms for the face or mouth.

261.

261.

Dvijo lapanajo danto, dasano radano rado;

Dāṭhā tudantabhedasmiṃ, apāṅgo tvakkhikoṭisu.

`Dvijo`, `lapanajo`, `danto`, `dasano`, `radano`, and `rado` are terms for a tooth. `Dāṭhā` is a large tooth or tusk. `Apāṅgo` is the outer corner of the eye.

262.

262.

Dantāvaraṇa moṭṭho cā, pya’dharo dasanacchado;

Gaṇḍo kapolo hanvitthī gaṇḍatthī (ṭī.) hanvatthī=hanu+itthī; hana+u (ṇvādi),

Cubukaṃ tva’ dharā adho.

`Dantāvaraṇaṃ`, `oṭṭho`, `adharo`, and `dasanacchado` are terms for the lip. `Gaṇḍo` and `kapolo` are terms for the cheek. `Hanu` is the jaw. `Cubukaṃ` is the chin, below the lower lip.

263.

263.

Galo ca kaṇṭho gīvā ca, kandharā ca sirodharā;

Kambugīvā tu yā gīvā, suvaṇṇāliṅgasannibhā;

Aṅkitā tīhi lekhāhi, kambugīvā thavā matā.

`Galo`, `kaṇṭho`, `gīvā`, `kandharā`, and `sirodharā` are terms for the neck or throat. A `kambugīvā` is a conch-like neck, resembling a golden shell and marked with three lines; such a neck is considered praiseworthy.

264.

264.

Aṃso nitthī bhujasiro, khandho tassandhi jattu taṃ;

Bāhumūlaṃ tu kaccho, dho, tva’ssa passa manitthiyaṃ.

`Aṃso`, `bhujasiro`, and `khandho` are terms for the shoulder. The joint of the shoulder is `jattu`. `Bāhumūlaṃ` and `kaccho` are terms for the armpit. Below that is the `passaṃ`, or side.

265.

265.

Bāhu bhujādvīsu bāhā, hattho tu kara pāṇayo;

Maṇibandho pakoṭṭhanto, kapparo tu kapoṇya’tha.

`Bāhu`, `bhujā`, and `bāhā` are terms for the arm. `Hattho`, `kara`, and `pāṇi` are terms for the hand. `Maṇibandho` is the wrist, and `pakoṭṭho` is the forearm. `Kapparo` and `kapoṇi` are terms for the elbow.

266.

266.

Maṇibandha kaniṭṭhānaṃ, pāṇissa karabho,ntaraṃ;

Karasākhā, ṅgulī tā tu, pañca, ṅguṭṭho ca tajjanī;

Majjhimā nāmikā cāpi, kaniṭṭhā’ti kamā siyuṃ.

`Karabho` is the part of the hand between the wrist and the fingers. The fingers (`karasākhā`, `aṅgulī`) are five: `aṅguṭṭho` (thumb), `tajjanī` (forefinger), `majjhimā` (middle finger), `nāmikā` (ring finger), and `kaniṭṭhā` (little finger), in that order.

267.

267.

Padeso tālagokaṇṇā, vidatthi,tthī kamā tate;

Tajjanyādiyute’ṅguṭṭhe, pasato pāṇi kuñcito.

`Padeso` (span from thumb to forefinger), `tālagokaṇṇa`, and `vidatthi` (span from thumb to little finger) are measures of an extended hand. A `pasato` is a cupped or bent hand (`kuñcito pāṇi`).

268.

268.

Ratanaṃ kukku hattho tha, pume karapuṭo,ñjali;

Karajo tu nakho nitthī, khaṭako muṭṭhi ca dvīsu.

`Ratanaṃ`, `kukku`, and `hattho` are terms for a cubit. `Karapuṭo` and `añjali` are terms for hands cupped in reverence. `Karajo` and `nakho` are terms for a fingernail. `Khaṭako` and `muṭṭhi` are terms for a fist.

269.

269.

Byāmo sahakarā bāhu, dve passadvayavitthatā;

Uddhantata bhujaposa, ppamāṇe porisaṃ tisu.

`Byāmo` is a fathom, the measure of the two outstretched arms. `Porisaṃ` is the measure of a man's height with his arm stretched upwards.

270.

270.

Uro ca hadayaṃ cātha, thano kuca payodharā;

Cūcukaṃ tu thanaggasmiṃ, piṭṭhaṃ tu piṭṭhi nāriyaṃ.

`Uro` and `hadayaṃ` are terms for the chest or heart. `Thano`, `kuca`, and `payodharā` are terms for the breast. `Cūcukaṃ` is the nipple, the tip of the breast. `Piṭṭhaṃ` and `piṭṭhi` are terms for the back.

271.

271.

Majjho’nitthī [M 0.0024] vilaggo ca, majjhimaṃ kucchi catukkaṃ udare; 944-gāthāpi passitabbā tu dvisu;

Gahaṇītthyudaraṃ gabbho, koṭṭhonto kucchisambhave.

`Majjho`, `vilaggo`, and `majjhimaṃ` are terms for the waist or middle. `Kucchi`, `udaraṃ`, `gabbho` are terms for the abdomen or womb. `Gahaṇī` is the small intestine. `Koṭṭhanto` refers to the contents of the abdomen.

272.

272.

Jaghanaṃ tu nitambo ca, soṇī ca kaṭi nāriyaṃ;

Aṅgajātaṃ rahassaṅgaṃ, vatthaguyhañca mehanaṃ.

`Jaghanaṃ`, `nitambo`, `soṇī`, and `kaṭi` are terms for the hips, buttocks, or waist. `Aṅgajātaṃ`, `rahassaṅgaṃ`, `vatthaguyhaṃ`, and `mehanaṃ` are terms for the genitals or private parts.

273.

273.

Nimittañca varaṅgañca, bījañca phalameva ca;

Liṅgaṃ aṇḍaṃ tu koso ca,

Yoni tvitthīpumebhagaṃ.

`Nimittaṃ`, `varaṅgaṃ`, `bījaṃ`, and `phalaṃ` are terms for the genitals. `Liṅgaṃ` is the male organ. `Aṇḍaṃ` is a testicle, and `koso` is the scrotum. `Yoni` and `bhagaṃ` are terms for the female genitals.

274.

274.

Asuci sambhavo sukkaṃ, pāyu tu purise gudaṃ;

Vā pume gūtha karīsa, vaccāni ca malaṃ chakaṃ.

`Asuci`, `sambhava`, and `sukkaṃ` are terms for semen. `Pāyu` and `gudaṃ` are terms for the anus. `Gūtha`, `karīsa`, `vaccaṃ`, `malaṃ`, and `chakaṃ` are terms for feces.

275.

275.

Uccāro mīḷha mukkāro, passāvo mutta muccate;

Pūtimuttañca gomutte, ssādīnaṃ chakaṇaṃ male.

`Uccāro`, `mīḷha`, and `mukkāro` are terms for excrement. `Passāvo` and `muttaṃ` are terms for urine. `Pūtimuttaṃ` is foul urine, and `gomuttaṃ` is cow urine. `Chakaṇaṃ` is the term for the dung of horses and other animals.

276.

276.

Dvīsvadho nābhiyā vatthi, ucchaṅga’ṅkā tu’bho pume;

Ūru satthi pume ūru, pabbaṃ tu jāṇu jaṇṇu ca.

The navel is `nābhi`; the bladder, `vatthi`. `Ucchaṅga` and `aṅka` are both masculine terms for the lap. `Ūru` and `satthi` are masculine terms for the thigh. `Pabbaṃ`, `jāṇu`, and `jaṇṇu` are terms for a joint or the knee.

277.

277.

Gopphako pādagaṇṭhipi, pume tu paṇhi pāsaṇi;

Pādaggaṃ papado pādo, tu pado caraṇañca vā.

`Gopphaka` and `pādagaṇṭhi` are terms for the ankle or foot-joint. `Paṇhi` and `pāsaṇi` are masculine terms for the heel. `Pādaggaṃ` is the tip of the foot; `papado`, the forepart of the foot. `Pādo`, `pado`, and `caraṇa` are terms for the foot.

278.

278.

Aṅgaṃtva’vayavo vutto, phāsulikā tu phāsukā;

Paṇḍake aṭṭhi dhātvitthī, galantaṭṭhi tu akkhako.

`Aṅgaṃ` and `avayavo` are said to be a limb or body part. `Phāsulikā` and `phāsukā` are terms for a rib. `Aṭṭhi` (bone) is neuter; `dhātu` (element) is feminine. `Galantaṭṭhi` and `akkhako` are terms for the collarbone.

279.

279.

Kapparo tu kapālaṃ vā, kaṇḍarā tu mahāsirā;

Pume nhāru citthī sirā, dhamanī tha rasaggasā.

`Kappara` and `kapālaṃ` are terms for the skull or a potsherd. `Kaṇḍarā` and `mahāsirā` are terms for a tendon or large vein. `Nhāru` (sinew) is masculine, and `sirā` (vein) is feminine. `Dhamanī` and `rasaggasā` are terms for an artery or a vessel carrying vital fluids.

280.

280.

Rasaharaṇya’tho maṃsa, māmisaṃ pisitaṃ bhave;

Tiliṅgikaṃ tu vallūra, muttattaṃ atha lohitaṃ.

`Rasaharaṇī` is a fluid-carrying vessel. `Maṃsa`, `āmisaṃ`, and `pisitaṃ` are terms for flesh or meat. `Vallūra`, meaning dried meat, is of three genders. `Muttattaṃ` is melted fat; `lohitaṃ` is blood.

281.

281.

Rudhiraṃ soṇitaṃ rattaṃ, lālā kheḷo elā bhave;

Purise māyu pittañca, semho nitthī silesumo.

`Rudhiraṃ`, `soṇitaṃ`, and `rattaṃ` are terms for blood. `Lālā`, `kheḷo`, and `elā` are terms for saliva or spittle. `Māyu` and `pittaṃ` are masculine terms for bile. `Semho` and `silesumo` are feminine terms for phlegm.

282.

282.

Vasā vilīnasneho tha, medo ceva vapā bhave;

Ākappo veso nepacchaṃ, maṇḍanaṃ tu pasādhanaṃ.

`Vasā`, `vilīnasneho`, `medo`, and `vapā` are terms for fat or grease. `Ākappo`, `veso`, and `nepacchaṃ` are terms for appearance or dress. `Maṇḍanaṃ` and `pasādhanaṃ` are terms for adornment or decoration.

283.

283.

Vibhūsanaṃ cābharaṇaṃ, alaṅkāro pilandhanaṃ;

Kirīṭa makuṭā’nitthī, cūḷāmaṇi siromaṇi.
