Bandhuko jayasumanaṃ, bhaṇḍiko bandhujīvako.

Sephālikā is also Nīlikā; Apphoṭā is Vanamallikā. Bandhuka is Jayasumana; Bhaṇḍika is Bandhujīvaka.

576.

576.

Sumanā jātisumanā, mālatī jāti vassikī;

Yūthikā māgadhī cātha, sattalā navamallikā.

Sumanā is Jātisumanā; Mālatī, Jāti, and Vassikī are synonyms. Yūthikā and Māgadhī are synonyms; Sattalā is Navamallikā.

577.

577.

Vāsantī,tthī atimutto, karavīro’ssamārako;

Mātuluṅgo bījapūro, ummatto tu ca mātulo.

Vāsantī, which is feminine, and Atimutta are synonyms. Karavīra is Assamāraka. Mātuluṅga is Bījapūra; Ummatta is also Mātula.

578.

578.

Karamando suseno ca, kundaṃ tu māghya muccate;

Devatāso devatāḍo (sī. amarakosa) tu jīmūto,

Thā’milāto mahāsahā.

Karamanda and Susena are synonyms; Kunda is called Māghya. Devatāsa is Jīmūta; Thāmilāta is Mahāsahā.

579.

579.

Atho sereyyako dāsī,

Kiṃ kirāto kuraṇṭako;

Ajjuko sitapaṇṇāso, samīraṇo phaṇijjako.

Furthermore, Sereyyaka and Dāsī are synonyms; Kiṃkirāta is Kuraṇṭaka. Ajjuka is Sitapaṇṇāsa; Samīraṇa is Phaṇijjaka.

580.

580.

Japā tu jayasumanaṃ, karīro kakaco bhave;

Rukkhādanī ca vandākā, cittako tva’ggisaññito.

Japā is Jayasumana; Karīra is Kakaca. Rukkhādanī and Vandākā are synonyms; Cittaka is known as Aggisaññita.

581.

581.

Akko vikīraṇo tasmiṃ,

Tva’ ḷakko setapupphake;

Pūtilatā gaḷocī ca, mubbā madhurasā pyatha.

Akka is Vikīraṇa; Aḷakka refers to the white-flowered variety thereof. Pūtilatā and Gaḷocī are synonyms; Mubbā is also Madhurasā.

582.

582.

Kapikacchu duphasso tha, mañjiṭṭhā vikasā bhave;

Ambaṭṭhā ca tathā pāṭhā, kaṭukā kaṭurohiṇī.

Kapikacchu is also Duphassa; Mañjiṭṭhā is Vikasā. Ambaṭṭhā and likewise Pāṭhā are synonyms; Kaṭukā is Kaṭurohiṇī.

583.

583.

Apāmaggo [M 0.0049] sekhariko, pippalī māgadhī matā;

Gokaṇṭako ca siṅghāṭo, kolavallī’bhapippalī.

Apāmagga is Sekharika; Pippalī is considered Māgadhī. Gokaṇṭaka and Siṅghāṭa are synonyms; Kolavallī is Ibhapippalī.

584.

584.

Golomī tu vacā cātha, girikaṇya’parājitā;

Sīhapucchī pañhipaṇṇī, sālapaṇṇī tu ca’tthirā; (Cathirā).

Golomī and Vacā are synonyms; Girikaṇṇī is Aparājitā. Sīhapucchī is Paṇhipaṇṇī; Sālapaṇṇī is also Atthiṛā.

585.

585.

Nididdhikā tu byagghī ca, atha nīlī ca nīlinī;

Jiñjuko jiñjukā (ka.) ceva guñjā tha, satamūlī satāvarī.

Nididdhikā and Byagghī are synonyms; furthermore, Nīlī and Nīlinī are synonyms. Jiñjuka is also Guñjā; Satamūlī is Satāvarī.

586.

586.

Mahosadhaṃ tva’tivisā, bākucī somavallikā;

Dābbī dāruhaliddā tha, biḷaṅgaṃ citrataṇḍulā.

Mahosadha is Ativisā; Bākucī is Somavallikā. Dābbī is also Dāruhaliddā; Biḷaṅga is Citrataṇḍulā.

587.

587.

Nuhī ceva mahānāmo, muddikā tu madhurasā;

Athāpi madhukaṃ yaṭṭhi, madhukāmadhuyaṭṭhikā madhulaṭṭhikā (sī. ṭī.).

Nuhī is also Mahānāma; Muddikā is Madhurasā. Furthermore, Madhuka, Yaṭṭhi, Madhukā, and Madhuyaṭṭhikā are synonyms.

588.

588.

Vātiṅgaṇo ca bhaṇḍākī, vāttākī brahatī pyatha;

Nāgabalā ceva jhasā, lāṅgalī tu ca sāradī.

Vātiṅgaṇa and Bhaṇḍākī are synonyms; Vāttākī is also Brahatī. Nāgabalā is also Jhasā; Lāṅgalī is also Sāradī.

589.

589.

Rambhā ca kadalī moco, kappāsī badarā bhave;

Nāgalatā tu tambūlī, aggijālā tu dhātakī.

Rambhā, Kadalī, and Moca are synonyms; Kappāsī is Badarā. Nāgalatā is Tambūlī; Aggijālā is Dhātakī.

590.

590.

Tivutā tipuṭā cātha, sāmā kāḷā ca kathyate;

Atho siṅgī ca usabho, reṇukā kapiḷā bhave.

Tivutā and Tipuṭā are synonyms; Sāmā and Kāḷā are also said to be synonyms. Furthermore, Siṅgī and Usabha are synonyms; Reṇukā is Kapiḷā.

591.

591.

Hiriverañca vālañca, rattaphalā tu bimbikā;

Seleyya’ masmapupphañca, elā tu bahulā bhave.

Hirivera and Vāla are synonyms; Rattaphalā is Bimbikā. Seleyya and Asmapuppha are synonyms; Elā is Bahulā.

592.

592.

Kuṭṭhañca byādhi kathito, vāneyyaṃ tu kuṭannaṭaṃ;

Osadhi jātimattamhi, osadhaṃ sabba’ majātiyaṃ.

Kuṭṭha is also called Byādhi; Vāneyya is Kuṭannaṭa. Osadhi refers to a single species, whereas Osadha refers to any and all species.

593.

593.

Mūlaṃ pattaṃ kaḷīra’ggaṃ, kandaṃ miñjā phalaṃ tathā;

Taco pupphañca chattanti, sākaṃ dasavidhaṃ mataṃ.

The root, leaf, shoot-tip, bulb, pith, and fruit, as well as bark, flower, and mushroom—these are considered the ten kinds of vegetables.

594.

594.

Papunnāṭo eḷagalo,

Taṇḍuleyyo’ppamāriso;

Jīvanti jīvanī cātha, madhurako ca jīvako.

Papunnāṭa is Eḷagala; Taṇḍuleyya is Appamārisa. Jīvantī and Jīvanī are synonyms; Madhuraka and Jīvaka are synonyms.

595.

595.

Mahākando ca lasuṇaṃ, palaṇḍu tu sukandako;

Paṭolo tittako cātha, bhiṅgarājo ca makkavo.

Mahākanda and Lasuṇa are synonyms; Palaṇḍu is Sukandaka. Paṭola and Tittaka are synonyms; Bhiṅgarāja and Makkava are synonyms.

596.

596.

Punannavā [M 0.0050] sothaghātī, vitunnaṃ sunisaṇṇakaṃ;

Kāravello tu susavī, tumbyā’lābu ca lābu sā.

Punannavā is Sothaghātī; Vitunna is Sunisaṇṇaka. Kāravella is Susavī; Tumbī, Ālābu, and Lābu are synonyms.

597.

597.

Eḷālukañca kakkārī, kumbhaṇḍo tu ca vallibho;

Indavāruṇī visālā, vatthukaṃ vatthuleyyako.

Eḷāluka and kakkārī are synonyms. Kumbhaṇḍa and vallibha are synonyms. Indavāruṇī and visālā are synonyms. Vatthuka and vatthuleyyaka are synonyms.

598.

598.

Mūlako nitthiyaṃ cuccu, tambako ca kalambako;

Sākabhedā kāsamadda, jhajjharī phaggavā’dayo.

Mūlaka and cuccu are synonyms; cuccu is feminine. Tambaka and kalambaka are synonyms. Kāsamadda, jhajjharī, phaggavā, and others are kinds of greens.

599.

599.

Saddalo ceva dubbā ca, golomī sā sitā bhave;

Gundā ca bhaddamuttañca, rasālo tu’cchu veḷu tu.

Saddala and dubbā are synonyms. Golomī and sitā are synonyms. Gundā and bhaddamutta are synonyms. Rasāla, ucchu, and veḷu are synonyms.

600.

600.

Tacasāro veṇu vaṃso, pabbaṃ tu phalu gaṇṭhiso;

Kīcakā te siyuṃ veṇū, ye nadantyā’niladdhutā.

Tacasāra, veṇu, and vaṃsa are synonyms for bamboo. Pabba, phalu, and gaṇṭhiso are synonyms for a joint or knot. Those bamboos which resound when shaken by the wind are kīcakā.

601.

601.

Naḷo ca dhamano poṭa, galo tu kāsa mitthi na;

Tejano tu saro, mūlaṃ, tū’ sīraṃ bīraṇassa hi.

Naḷa, dhamana, and poṭa are synonyms. Gala and kāsa are synonyms; kāsa is not feminine. Tejano is sara. Mūla is a root; indeed, usīra is the root of the bīraṇa grass.

602.

602.

Kuso varahisaṃ dabbo, bhūtiṇakaṃ tu bhūtiṇaṃ;

Ghāso tu yavaso cātha,

Pūgo tu kamuko bhave.

Kusa, varahisa, and dabba are synonyms. Bhūtiṇaka is bhūtiṇa. Ghāsa is also yavasa. Pūga is kamuka.

603.

603.

Tālo vibhedikā cātha, khajjurī sindi vuccati;

Tāla is also vibhedikā; khajjurī is called sindi.

604.

604.

Hintāla, tāla, khajjūrī, nālikerā tatheva ca;

Tālī ca ketakī nārī, pūgo ca tiṇapādapāti.

Hintāla, tāla, khajjūrī, and nālikera, as well as tālī, ketakī (which is feminine), and pūga, are known as grass-trees.

Iti araññavaggo.

Thus ends the Forest Chapter.

6. Araññādivagga

6. The Chapter on the Forest and So Forth

605.

605.

Pabbato giri selo’ddi, nagā’cala, siluccayā;

Sikharī bhūdharotha bbha, pāsāṇā’smo’palo silā.

Pabbata, giri, sela, addi, naga, acala, and siluccaya are synonyms for mountain. Sikharī, bhūdhara, and abbha are also synonyms for mountain. Pāsāṇa, asma, upala, and silā are synonyms for rock or stone.

606.

606.

Gijjhakūṭo ca vebhāro, vepullo’sigilī nagā;

Viñjho paṇḍava vaṅkādī, pubbaselo tu co’dayo;

Mandaro paraselo’ttho, himavā tu himācalo.

Gijjhakūṭa, Vebhāra, Vepulla, and Isigili are mountains. Viñjha, Paṇḍava, and Vaṅka are other such mountains. Pubbasela is Udaya; Mandara is Parasela; Himavā is Himācala.

607.

607.

Gandhamādana kelāsa, cittakūṭa sudassanā;

Kālakūṭo tikūṭā’ssa, pattho tu sānu nitthiyaṃ.

Gandhamādana, Kelāsa, Cittakūṭa, Sudassana, Kālakūṭa, and Tikūṭa are names of mountains. Pattha and sānu are synonyms for a mountain plateau; sānu is feminine.

608.

608.

Kūṭo [M 0.0051] vā sikharaṃ siṅgaṃ, papāto tu taṭo bhave;

Nitambo kaṭako nitthī, nijjharo pasavo’mbuno.

Kūṭa, sikhara, and siṅga are synonyms for a peak or summit. Papāta is a precipice or cliff. Nitamba and kaṭaka are synonyms for a mountain's slope; kaṭaka is also feminine. Nijjhara is a spring or waterfall.

609.

609.

Darī’tthī kandaro dvīsu, leṇaṃ tu gabbharaṃ guhā;

Silāpokkharaṇī soṇḍī, kuñjaṃ nikuñja mitthi na.

Darī, which is feminine, and kandara, which is in two genders, are synonyms for a ravine. Leṇa, gabbhara, and guhā are synonyms for a cave. Silāpokkharaṇī and soṇḍī are synonyms for a rocky pool. Kuñja and nikuñja are synonyms for a bower; nikuñja is not feminine.

610.

610.

Uddha madhiccakā sela, ssāsannā bhūmyu paccakā;

Pādo tu’pantaselo tha,

Dhātu’tto gerikādiko.

Adhiccakā is the high ground of a mountain; upaccakā is the land near its base. Pāda is the foot of the mountain. Dhātu refers to minerals, such as red ochre and so forth.

Iti selavaggo.

Thus ends the Mountain Chapter.

611.

611.

Migindo kesarī sīho, taraccho tu migādano;

Byaggho tu puṇḍarīko tha, saddūlo dīpinī’rito.

Miginda, kesarī, and sīha are synonyms for a lion. Taraccha is migādana, the hyena. Byaggha is puṇḍarīka, the tiger. Saddūla is said to be dīpinī, the leopard.

612.

612.

Accho ikko ca isso tu,

Kāḷasīho iso pyatha;

Rohiso rohito cātha,

Gokaṇṇo gaṇi kaṇṭakā.

Accha, ikka, and issa are synonyms for a bear. Kāḷasīha and isa are also animal names. Rohisa and rohita are synonyms for a red deer. Gokaṇṇa, gaṇi, and kaṇṭaka are types of deer.

613.

613.

Khagga khaggavisāṇā tu, palāsādo ca gaṇḍako;

Byagghādike vāḷamigo, sāpado tha plavaṅgamo.

Khagga and khaggavisāṇa are synonyms for rhinoceros; palāsāda and gaṇḍaka are also synonyms. Tigers and the like are called vāḷamiga or sāpada, beasts of prey. Plavaṅgama is a monkey.

614.

614.

Makkaṭo vānaro sākhā, migo kapi valīmukho;

Palavaṅgo, kaṇhatuṇḍo,

Gonaṅgulo gonaṅgalo (ṭī.) ti so mato.

Makkaṭa, vānara, sākhāmiga, kapi, valīmukha, palavaṅga, kaṇhatuṇḍa, and gonaṅgula are all considered synonyms for a monkey.

615.

615.

Siṅgālo sigālo (sī.) jambuko kotthu, bheravo ca sivā pyatha;

Biḷāro babbu mañjāro, koko tu ca vako bhave.

Siṅgāla, jambuka, kotthu, bherava, and sivā are synonyms for a jackal. Biḷāra, babbu, and mañjāra are synonyms for a cat. Koka is also vaka, a wolf.

616.

616.

Mahiṃso mahiso (sī.) ca lulāyo tha,

Gavajo gavayo samā;

Sallo tu sallako thā’ssa,

Lomamhi salalaṃ salaṃ.

Mahiṃsa and lulāya are synonyms for a buffalo. Gavaja and gavaya are similar, meaning a wild ox. Salla is sallaka, the porcupine; its quills are called salala and sala.

617.

617.

Hariṇo miga sāraṅgā, mago ajinayoni ca;

Sūkaro tu varoho tha,

Pelako ca saso bhave.

Hariṇa, miga, sāraṅga, maga, and ajinayoni are synonyms for deer. Sūkara is varoha, the boar. Pelaka is a type of deer, and sasa is the hare.

618.

618.

Eṇeyyo [M 0.0052] eṇīmigo ca, pampaṭako tu pampako;

Vātamigo tu calanī, mūsiko tvā’khu unduro.

Eṇeyya and eṇīmiga are terms for the black antelope. Pampaṭaka is pampaka, a type of antelope. Vātamiga is calanī, a swift antelope. Mūsika, ākhu, and undura are synonyms for a mouse or rat.

619.

619.

Camaro pasado ceva, kuruṅgo migamātukā;

Ruru raṅku ca nīko ca, sarabhādī migantarā.

Camara and pasada are types of deer, as are kuruṅga and migamātukā. Ruru, raṅku, and nīka are also types of deer. Sarabha and others are further kinds of deer.

620.

620.

Piyako camūru kadalī, migādī cammayonayo;

Migā tu pasavo sīhā, dayo sabbacatuppadā.

Piyaka, camūru, kadalī, and other deer are animals valued for their hides. Deer are beasts, as are lions and all other four-footed creatures.

621.

621.

Lūtā tu lūtikā uṇṇa, nābhi makkaṭako siyā;

Vicchiko tvā’ḷi kathito, sarabū gharagoḷikā.

Lūtā, lūtikā, uṇṇanābhi, and makkaṭaka are synonyms for a spider. Vicchika is also called āḷi, the scorpion. Sarabū is gharagoḷikā, the house lizard.

622.

622.

Godhā kuṇḍo pyatho kaṇṇa, jalūkā satapadyatha;

Kalandako kāḷakā tha, nakulo maṅguso bhave.

Godhā is the iguana. Jalūkā is the leech, and satapadī is the centipede. Kalandaka and kāḷaka are synonyms for the squirrel. Nakula is maṅgusa, the mongoose.

623.

623.

Kakaṇṭako ca saraṭo, kīṭo tu puḷavo kimi;

Pāṇako cāpyatho uccā,

Liṅgo lomasapāṇako.

Kakaṇṭaka and saraṭa are synonyms for a chameleon or lizard. Kīṭa, puḷava, and kimi are synonyms for an insect or worm. Pāṇaka, uccāliṅga, and lomasapāṇaka are also names for insects.

624.

624.

Vihaṅgo vihago pakkhī, vihaṅgama khaga’ṇḍajā;

Sakuṇo ca sakunto vi, pataṅgo sakuṇī dvijo.

Vihaṅga, vihaga, pakkhī, vihaṅgama, khaga, and aṇḍaja (egg-born); and sakuṇa, sakunta, vi, pataṅga, sakuṇī, and dvija (twice-born) are synonyms for a bird.

625.

625.

Vakkaṅgo pattayāno ca, patanto nīḷajo bhave;

Tabbhedā vaṭṭakā jīva, ñjīvo cakora tittirā.

Vakkaṅga, pattayāna (wing-goer), patanta (flying), and nīḷaja (nest-born) are synonyms for a bird. Its varieties are the vaṭṭaka (quail), jīvañjīva, cakora, and tittira (partridge).

626.

626.

Sāḷikā karavīko ca, ravihaṃso kukutthako;

Kāraṇḍavo ca pilavo bilavo (ṭī.), pokkharasātakā’dayo.

Sāḷikā, karavīka, ravihaṃsa, and kukutthaka; likewise kāraṇḍava, pilava, pokkharasātaka, and others are types of birds.

627.

627.

Patattaṃ pekhuṇaṃ pattaṃ, pakkho piñchaṃ chado garu;

Aṇḍaṃ tu pakkhibīje tha, nīḷo nitthī kulāvakaṃ.

Patatta, pekhuṇa, patta, and pakkha are synonyms for a wing or feather; piñcha, chada, and garu are synonyms for a feather. Aṇḍa means a bird's egg. Nīḷa (masculine), nitthī (feminine), and kulāvaka (neuter) are synonyms for a nest.

628.

628.

Supaṇṇamātā vinatā, mithunaṃ thīpumadvayaṃ;

Yugaṃ tu yugalaṃ dvandaṃ, yamakaṃ yamalaṃ yamaṃ.

Vinatā is Supaṇṇamātā (the mother of the Supaṇṇas). Mithuna is a pair of a female and a male. Yuga, yugala, dvanda, yamaka, yamala, and yama are synonyms for a pair.

629.

629.

Samūho gaṇa saṅghātā, samudāyo ca sañcayo;

Sandoho nivaho ogho, visaro nikaro cayo.

Samūha, gaṇa, saṅghāta, samudāya, and sañcaya; likewise sandoha, nivaha, ogha, visara, nikara, and caya are synonyms for a multitude, mass, or collection.

630.

630.

Kāyo khandho samudayo, ghaṭā samiti saṃhati;

Rāsi puñjo samavāyo, pūgo jātaṃ kadambakaṃ.

Kāya, khandha, samudaya, and ghaṭā; likewise samiti, saṃhati, rāsi, puñja, samavāya, pūga, jāta, and kadambaka are synonyms for an aggregate, collection, or mass.

631.

631.

Byūho [M 0.0053] vitāna gumbā ca, kalāpo jāla maṇḍalaṃ;

Samānānaṃ gaṇo vaggo,

Saṅgho sattho tu jantunaṃ.

Byūha (array), vitāna (canopy), gumba (thicket), kalāpa (bundle), jāla (net), and maṇḍala (circle) are synonyms for a collection. Gaṇa and vagga refer to a group of equals. Saṅgha is an assembly, and sattha is a troop of living beings.

632.

632.

Sajātikānaṃ tu kulaṃ, nikāyo tu sadhamminaṃ;

Yūtho nitthī sajātiya, tiracchānānamuccate.

Kula is a group of those of the same kind; nikāya is a group of those of the same doctrine. Yūtha is said of a herd of animals of the same species.

633.

633.

Supaṇṇo venateyyo ca, garuḷo vihagādhipo;

Parapuṭṭho parabhato, kuṇālo kokilo piko.

Supaṇṇa, venateyya, garuḷa, and vihagādhipa (lord of birds) are synonyms for the Garuḍa. Parapuṭṭha, parabhata, kuṇāla, kokila, and pika are synonyms for the cuckoo.

634.

634.

Moro mayūro varahī, nīlagīva sikhaṇḍino;

Kalāpī ca sikhī kekī, cūḷā tu ca sikhā bhave.

Mora, mayūra, varahī, nīlagīva (blue-necked), sikhaṇḍin (crested), kalāpī, sikhī, and kekī are synonyms for a peacock. Cūḷā and sikhā are synonyms for a crest.

635.

635.

Sikhaṇḍo varahañceva, kalāpo piñcha mapyatha;

Candako mecako cātha, chappado ca madhubbato.

Sikhaṇḍa and varaha are synonyms for a crest; kalāpa and piñcha are synonyms for a tail-feather. Candaka and mecaka refer to the eye-spot on a peacock's tail. Chappada and madhubbata are synonyms for a bee.

636.

636.

Madhulīho madhukaro, madhupo bhamaro ali;

Pārāvato kapoto ca, kakuṭo ca pārevato.

Madhulīha, madhukara, madhupa, bhamara, and ali are synonyms for a bee. Pārāvata, kapota, kakuṭa, and pārevata are synonyms for a pigeon or dove.

637.

637.

Gijjho gaddhotha kulalo, seno byagghīnaso pyatha;

Tabbhedā sakuṇagghi’tthī, āṭo dabbimukhadvijo.

Gijjha and gaddha are synonyms for a vulture; kulala is an osprey and sena is a hawk. Byagghīnasa is a type of bird of prey; sakuṇagghī (feminine) is a variety of these. Āṭa and dabbimukhadvija are also birds of prey.

638.

638.

Uhuṅkāro ulūko ca, kosiyo vāyasāri ca;

Kāko tva’riṭṭho dhaṅko ca, balipuṭṭho ca vāyaso.

Uhuṅkāra, ulūka, kosiya, and vāyasāri (enemy of the crow) are synonyms for an owl. Kāka, ariṭṭha, dhaṅka, balipuṭṭha, and vāyasa are synonyms for a crow.

639.

639.

Kākolo vanakāko tha,

Lāpo laṭukikā pyatha;

Vāraṇo hatthiliṅgo ca, hatthisoṇḍavihaṅgamo.

Kākola is a synonym for vanakāka (forest crow). Lāpa is a synonym for laṭukikā (quail). Vāraṇa, hatthiliṅga, and hatthisoṇḍavihaṅgama are synonyms for a mythical elephant-bird.

640.

640.

Ukkuso kuraro kola,ṭṭhipakkhimhi ca kukkuho;

Suvo tu kīro ca suko, tambacūḷo tu kukkuṭo.

Ukkusa and kurara are synonyms for an osprey; kolaṭṭhipakkhī is a woodpecker. Kukkuha is a wild cock. Suva, kīra, and suka are synonyms for a parrot. Tambacūḷa is a synonym for kukkuṭa (a cock).

641.

641.

Vanakukkuṭo ca nijjivho, atha koñcā ca kuntanī;

Cakkavāko tu cakkavho, sāraṅgotu ca cātako.

Vanakukkuṭa (jungle fowl) and nijjivha are types of birds. Koñca and kuntanī are synonyms for a curlew or crane. Cakkavāka and cakkavha are synonyms for the ruddy sheldrake. Sāraṅga and cātaka are synonyms for the pied crested cuckoo.

642.

642.

Tuliyo pakkhibiḷālo,

Satapatto tu sāraso;

Bako tu sukkakākotha,

Balākā visakaṇṭhikā.

Tuliya and pakkhibiḷāla (bird-cat) are synonyms for an owl. Satapatta and sārasa are synonyms for the Sarus crane. Baka and sukkakāka (white crow) are synonyms for a heron. Balākā and visakaṇṭhikā are synonyms for a crane or egret.

643.

643.

Lohapiṭṭho tathā kaṅko, khañjarīṭo tu khañjano;

Kalaviṅko tu cāṭako, dindibho tu kikī bhave.

Lohapiṭṭha and kaṅka are synonyms for a heron. Khañjarīṭa and khañjana are synonyms for the wagtail. Kalaviṅka and cāṭaka are synonyms for the sparrow. Dindibha and kikī are synonyms for the blue jay.

644.

644.

Kādambo [M 0.0054] kāḷahaṃsotha, sakunto bhāsapakkhini;

Dhūmyāṭo tu kaliṅgotha,

Dātyūho kāḷakaṇṭhako.

Kādamba and kāḷahaṃsa are synonyms for a goose. Sakunta and bhāsapakkhinī are synonyms for a vulture. Dhūmyāṭa and kaliṅga are synonyms for the fork-tailed shrike. Dātyūha and kāḷakaṇṭhaka are synonyms for the gallinule.

645.

645.

Khuddādī makkhikābhedā, ḍaṃso piṅgalamakkhikā;

Āsāṭikā makkhikāṇḍaṃ, pataṅgo salabho bhave.

Khuddā and others are varieties of fly. Ḍaṃsa is a gadfly, and piṅgalamakkhikā is a tawny fly. Āsāṭikā is a type of fly, and makkhikāṇḍa is a fly's egg. Pataṅga and salabha are synonyms for a moth or grasshopper.

646.

646.

Sūcimukho ca makaso, cīrī tu jhallikā jhillikā (ka.) tha ca;

Jatukā jinapattā tha, haṃso setacchado bhave.

Sūcimukha and makasa are synonyms for a mosquito. Cīrī and jhallikā are synonyms for a cricket. Jatukā and jinapatta are synonyms for a bat. Haṃsa and setacchada (white-winged) are synonyms for a goose or swan.

647.

647.

Te rājahaṃsā rattehi, pādatuṇḍehi bhāsitā;

Mallikā’khyā dhataraṭṭhā, malīnehya’sitehi ca.

Royal geese (rājahaṃsa) are described as having red feet and beaks. Dhataraṭṭha geese, which have dusky or black feet and beaks, are known as Mallikā.

648. Tiraccho tu tiracchāno, tiracchānagato siyāti.

648. Tiraccha and tiracchāna are synonyms for an animal. Tiracchānagata means one who has gone to the animal realm, one reborn as an animal.

Iti araññādivaggo.

Thus ends the Chapter on the Forest and so forth.

7. Pātālavagga

7. The Chapter on the Underworld

649.

649.

Adhobhuvanaṃ pātālaṃ, nāgaloko rasātalaṃ;

Randhaṃ tu vivaraṃ chiddaṃ, kuharaṃ susiraṃ bilaṃ.

Adhobhuvana, pātāla, nāgaloka, and rasātala are synonyms for the underworld. Randha, vivara, chidda, kuhara, susira, and bila are synonyms for a hole, cavity, or opening.

650.

650.

Susi’tthī chiggalaṃ sobbhaṃ, sacchidde susiraṃ tisu;

Thiyaṃ tu kāsu āvāṭo, sapparājā tu vāsukī.

Susi (feminine), chiggala, and sobbha are synonyms for a pit or hole. The word susira, meaning 'hollow' or 'having a hole', is used in all three genders. Kāsu (feminine) and āvāṭa are synonyms for a pit. Vāsukī is a king of serpents.

651.

651.

Ananto nāgarājā tha, vāhaso’jagaro bhave;

Gonaso tu tiliccho tha,

Deḍḍubho rājulo bhave.

Ananta is a Nāga king. Vāhasa and ajagara are synonyms for a python. Gonasa and tiliccha are synonyms for a type of viper. Deḍḍubha and rājula are synonyms for a water snake.

652.

652.

Kambalo’ssataro meru, pāde nāgātha dhammanī;

Silutto gharasappo tha, nīlasappo silābhu ca.

Kambala, Assatara, Meru, and Pādanāga are Nāgas. Then, Dhammanī is a vein. Siluṭṭa is a house snake, and Nīlasappa is also Silābhu.

653.

653.

Āsiviso bhujaṅgo’hi, bhujago ca bhujaṅgamo;

Sarīsapo phaṇī sappā, lagaddā bhogi pannagā.

Āsīvisa, bhujaṅga, ahi, bhujaga, bhujaṅgama, sarīsapa, phaṇī, sappa, lagadda, bhogī, and pannaga are all synonyms for snake or serpent.

654.

654.

Dvijivho urago vāḷo, dīgho ca dīghapiṭṭhiko;

Pādūdaro visadharo, bhogo tu phaṇino tanu.

Dvijivha, uraga, vāḷa, dīgha, dīghapiṭṭhika, pādūdara, and visadhara are also synonyms for snake or serpent. Bhoga is the body of a hooded snake.

655.

655.

Āsī’tthī [M 0.0055] sappadāṭhā tha, nimmoko kañcuko samā;

Visaṃ tva’nitthī garaḷaṃ, tabbhedā vā halāhalo.

Āsī (feminine) is a snake's fang. Nimmoka and kañcuka are synonyms for a cast-off skin. Visa (neuter) and garaḷa are synonyms for poison; Halāhala is a type thereof.

656.

656.

Kāḷakūṭādayo cātha, vāḷaggātya’hituṇḍiko;

Kāḷakūṭa and others are types of poison. Then, Vāḷaggāhī and Ahituṇḍika are snake charmers.

657.

657.

Nirayo duggati’tthī ca, narako, so mahā’ṭṭhadhā;

Sañjīvo kāḷasutto ca, mahāroruva roruvā;

Patāpano avīci’tthī, saṅghāto tāpano iti.

Niraya, and Duggati (feminine), are synonyms for a bad bourn or hell; Naraka is also hell. It is of eight great kinds: Sañjīva, Kāḷasutta, Mahāroruva, Roruva, Patāpana, Avīci (feminine), Saṅghāta, and Tāpana.

658.

658.

Thiyaṃ vetaraṇī loha, kumbhī tattha jalāsayā;

Kāraṇiko nirayapo,

Nerayiko tu nārako.

Vetaraṇī (feminine) and Lohakumbhī are reservoirs there. Kāraṇika and Nirayapa are synonyms for a hell-warden. Nerayika and Nārakā are synonyms for a hell-dweller.

659.

659.

Aṇṇavo sāgaro sindhu, samuddo ratanākaro;

Jalanijhu’ dadhi, tassa, bhedā khīraṇṇavādayo.

Aṇṇava, Sāgara, Sindhu, Samudda, and Ratanākara are synonyms for ocean; Jalanidhi and Udadhi are also synonyms. Its divisions include the Milk Ocean and others.

660.

660.

Velā’ssa kūladeso tha,

Āvaṭṭo salilabbhamo;

Thevo tu bindu phusitaṃ, bhamo tu jalaniggamo.

Velā is its shore or bank. Āvaṭṭa is a whirlpool of water. Theva, bindu, and phusita are synonyms for a drop. Bhamo is the outflow of water.

661.

661.

Āpo payo jalaṃ vāri, pānīyaṃ salilaṃ dakaṃ;

Aṇṇo nīraṃ vanaṃ vālaṃ, toyaṃ ambu’dakañca kaṃ.

Āpa, paya, jala, vāri, pānīya, salila, daka, aṇṇa, nīra, vana, vāla, toya, ambu, udaka, and ka are all synonyms for water.

662.

662.

Taraṅgo ca tathā bhaṅgo, ūmi vīci pumitthiyaṃ;

Ullolo tu ca kallolo, mahāvīcīsu kathyate.

Taraṅga and bhaṅga are synonyms for a wave. Ūmi and vīci are synonyms for a wave, used in both the masculine and feminine genders. Ullola and kallola are said to be great waves.

663.

663.

Jambālo kalalaṃ paṅko, cikkhallaṃ kaddamo pyatha;

Pulinaṃ vālukā vaṇṇu, marū’ru sikatā bhave.

Jambāla, kalala, paṅka, cikkhalla, and kaddama are synonyms for mud or mire. Pulina, vālukā, vaṇṇu, maru, uru, and sikatā are synonyms for sand.

664.

664.

Antarīpañca dīpo vā, jalamajjhagataṃ thalaṃ;

Tīraṃ tu kūlaṃ rodhañca, patīrañca taṭaṃ tisu.

Antarīpa and dīpa are synonyms for land situated in the middle of water (an island or islet). Tīra, kūla, rodha, patīra, and taṭa are synonyms for shore, used in the three genders.

665.

665.

Pāraṃ paramhi tīramhi, oraṃ tva’pāra muccate;

Uḷumpo uḷupo (ka.) tu plavo kullo, taro ca paccarī’tthiyaṃ.

Pāra refers to the far shore; ora refers to the near shore. Uḷumpa, plava, kulla, and tara are synonyms for a raft; Paccarī (feminine) is also a raft.

666.

666.

Taraṇī tari nāvā ca, kūpako tu ca kumbhakaṃ;

Pacchābandho goṭaviso, kaṇṇadhāro tu nāviko.

Taraṇī, tari, and nāvā are synonyms for a boat or ship. Kūpaka and kumbhaka are synonyms for a mast. Pacchābandha and goṭavisa are synonyms for the stern-binding. Kaṇṇadhāra and nāvika are synonyms for a helmsman or boatman.

667.

667.

Arittaṃ kenipāto tha,

Potavāho niyāmako;

Saṃyattikā tu nāvāya, vāṇijjamācaranti ye.

Aritta and kenipāta are synonyms for a rudder or oar. Potavāha and niyāmaka are synonyms for a pilot or helmsman. Saṃyattika refers to those who engage in trade by boat.

668.

668.

Nāvāya’ṅgā’ [M 0.0056] laṅkāro ca, vaṭākāro phiyādayo;

Poto pavahanaṃ vuttaṃ, doṇi tvi’tthī tathā’mbaṇaṃ ammaṇaṃ (sī.).

The parts and adornments of a boat are round components, oars, and so forth. Pota and pavahana are said to be conveyances. Doṇi (feminine) and ambaṇa are synonyms for a canoe or small boat.

669.

669.

Gabhīra ninna gambhīrā, tho ttānaṃ tabbipakkhake;

Agādhaṃ tva’talamphassaṃ, anaccho kalusā’vilā.

Gabhīra, ninna, and gambhīra are synonyms for deep. Uttāna is its opposite (shallow). Agādha and atalamphassa are synonyms for bottomless or unfathomable. Anaccha, kalusa, and āvila are synonyms for turbid or murky.

670.

670.

Accho pasanno vimalo, gabhīrappabhutī tisu;

Dhīvaro macchiko maccha, bandha kevaṭṭa jālikā.

Accha, pasanna, and vimala are synonyms for clear, serene, or spotless. Gabhīra and the preceding terms are used in the three genders. Dhīvara, macchika, macchabandha, kevaṭṭa, and jālika are synonyms for a fisherman.

671.

671.

Maccho mīno jalacaro, puthulomo’mbujo jhaso;

Rohito magguro siṅgī, balajo muñja pāvusā.

Maccha, mīna, jalacara, puthuloma, ambuja, and jhasa are synonyms for fish. Rohita, maggura, siṅgī, balaja, muñja, and pāvusa are types of fish.

672.

672.

Sattavaṅko savaṅko ca, naḷamīno ca gaṇḍako;

Susukā sapharī maccha, ppabhedā makarādayo.

Sattavaṅka, savaṅka, naḷamīna, and gaṇḍaka are types of fish. Susukā and sapharī are also types of fish. Makara and others are major types of fish.

673.

673.

Mahāmacchā timi timi, ṅgalo timirapiṅgalo;

Ānando timinando ca, ajjhāroho mahātimi.

The great fish (mahāmacchā) are Timi, Timiṅgala, Timirapiṅgalo, Ānanda, Timinanda, Ajjhāroha, and Mahātimi.

674.

674.

Pāsāṇamaccho pāṭhīno, vaṅko tu baḷiso bhave;

Susumāro saṃsumāro (ṭī.), suṃsumāro (sī.) tu kumbhīlo,

Nakko kummo tu kacchapo.

Pāsāṇamaccha and pāṭhīna are synonyms for a sheat-fish. Vaṅka and baḷisa are synonyms for a fishhook. Susumāra and kumbhīla are synonyms for a crocodile. Nakka, kumma, and kacchapa are synonyms for a turtle.

675.

675.

Kakkaṭako kuḷīro ca, jalūkā tu ca rattapā;

Maṇḍūko dadduro bheko;

Gaṇḍuppādo mahīlatā.

Kakkaṭaka and kuḷīra are synonyms for a crab. Jalūkā and rattapā are synonyms for a leech. Maṇḍūka, daddura, and bheka are synonyms for a frog. Gaṇḍuppāda and mahīlatā are synonyms for an earthworm.

676.

676.

Atha sippī ca sutti’tthī, saṅkhe tu kambu’nitthiyaṃ;

Khuddasaṅkhye saṅkhanakho, jalasutti ca sambuko.

Then, sippī and sutti (feminine) are synonyms for an oyster or pearl-oyster. Saṅkha and kambu (neuter) are synonyms for a conch. Khuddasaṅkha and saṅkhanakha are synonyms for a small conch. Jalasutti and sambuka are synonyms for a water-oyster or clam.

677.

677.

Jalāsayo jalādhāro, gambhīro rahado tha ca;

Udapāno pānakūpo, khātaṃ pokkharaṇī’tthiyaṃ.

Jalāsaya and jalādhāra are synonyms for a reservoir or pond. Gambhīra and rahada are synonyms for a deep lake. Udapāna and pānakūpa are synonyms for a well or drinking well. Khāta and pokkharaṇī (feminine) are synonyms for an excavated tank or lotus pond.

678.

678.

Taḷāko ca saro’nitthī, vāpī ca sarasī’tthiyaṃ;

Daho’mbujākaro cātha, pallalaṃ khuddako saro.

Taḷāka and sara (neuter) are synonyms for a lake. Vāpī and sarasī (feminine) are synonyms for a reservoir or natural lake. Daha and ambujākara are synonyms for a deep pool or lotus pond. Pallala is a small lake.

679.

679.

Anotatto tathā kaṇṇa, muṇḍo ca rathakārako;

Chaddanto ca kuṇālo ca, vuttā mandākinī’tthiyaṃ.

Anotatta, Kaṇṇamuṇḍa, Rathakāraka, Chaddanta, and Kuṇāla are said to be great lakes, as is Mandākinī (feminine).

680.

680.

Tathā sīhappapātoti, ete satta mahāsarā;

Āhāvo tu nipānañcā, khātaṃ tu devakhātakaṃ.

Likewise Sīhappapāta; these are the seven great lakes. Āhāva and nipāna are synonyms for a trough or drinking place. Khāta and devakhātaka are synonyms for a pit or a pit dug by devas.

681.

681.

Savantī [M 0.0057] ninnagā sindhu, saritā āpagā nadī;

Bhāgīrathī tu gaṅgā tha, sambhedo sindhusaṅgamo.

Savantī, ninnagā, sindhu, saritā, āpagā, and nadī are synonyms for a river. Bhāgīrathī and Gaṅgā are synonyms for the Ganges. Sambheda and sindhusaṅgama are synonyms for a confluence or junction of rivers.

682.

682.

Gaṅgā’ciravatī ceva, yamunā sarabhū (sarabū sarayū (ka.) ) mahī;

Imā mahānadī pañca, candabhāgā sarassatī sarasvatī (sī. ṭī.).

The Gaṅgā, Aciravatī, Yamunā, Sarabhū, and Mahī are the five great rivers; also the Candabhāgā and the Sarassatī.

683.

683.

Nerañjarā ca kāverī, nammadādī ca ninnagā;

Vārimaggo paṇālī’tthī panāḷī (ṭī.), pume candanikā tu ca.

The Nerañjarā, Kāverī, Nammadā, and others are rivers. A watercourse is termed `vārimagga` or `paṇālī` (feminine), and also `candanikā` (masculine).

684.

684.

Jambālī oligallo ca, gāmadvāramhi kāsuyaṃ;

Saroruhaṃ satapattaṃ, aravindañca vārijaṃ.

`Jambālī` and `oligalla` denote a drain or muddy ditch at a village gate. `Saroruha`, `satapatta`, `aravinda`, and `vārija` are names for the lotus.

685.

685.

Anitthī padumaṃ paṅke, ruhaṃ nalina pokkharaṃ;

Muḷālapupphaṃ kamalaṃ, bhisapupphaṃ kusesayaṃ.

`Paduma` (neuter), `paṅkeruha`, `nalina`, `pokkhara`, `muḷālapuppha`, `kamala`, `bhisapuppha`, and `kusesaya` are all names for the lotus.

686.

686.

Puṇḍarīkaṃ sitaṃ, rattaṃ, kokanadaṃ kokāsako;

Kiñjakkho kesaro nitthī, daṇḍo tu nāla muccate.

A white lotus is a `puṇḍarīka`. A red lotus is a `kokanada` or `kokāsaka`. A filament or stamen is a `kiñjakkha` or `kesara` (neuter). A stalk or stem is called `daṇḍa` or `nāḷa`.

687.

687.

Bhisaṃ muḷālo nitthī ca, bījakoso tu kaṇṇikā;

Padumādisamūhe tu, bhave saṇḍamanitthiyaṃ.

`Bhisa` and `muḷāla` (neuter) are terms for a lotus root or stalk. `Bījakosa` and `kaṇṇikā` are terms for a seed-receptacle or pericarp. A cluster of lotuses and the like is called a `saṇḍa` (neuter).

688.

688.

Uppalaṃ kuvalayañca, nīlaṃ tvi’ndīvaraṃ siyā;

Setetu kumudañcassa, kando sālūka muccate.

`Uppala` and `kuvalaya` are terms for a water lily. The blue water lily is an `indīvara`. The white one is a `kumuda`. Its bulb is called `sālūka`.

689.

689.

Sogandhikaṃ kallahāraṃ, dakasītalikaṃ pyatha;

Sevālo nīlikā cātha, bhisinya’mbujinī bhave.

`Sogandhika`, `kallahāra`, and `dakasītalika` are types of water lilies. `Sevālo` and `nīlikā` are terms for water moss or algae. A lotus pond is a `bhisinī` or `ambujinī`.

690. Sevālā tilabījañca, saṅkhe ca paṇakādayoti.

690. Also mentioned are `sevāla`, `tilabīja`, `saṅkha`, `paṇaka`, and others.

Iti pātālavaggo.

Thus ends the Chapter on the Underworld.

Bhūkaṇḍo dutiyo.

The Second Section: The Earth.

3. Sāmaññakaṇḍa

3. The General Section

1. Visesyādhīnavagga

1. The Chapter on Terms Dependent on the Substantive

691.

691.

Visesyādhīna saṃkiṇṇā, nekatthehya’byayehi ca;

Sā’ṅgo’pāṅgehi kathyante, kaṇḍe vaggā iha kkamā.

Here, in this section, the chapters are set forth in order, dealing with terms dependent on the substantive, mixed with indeclinables of various meanings, and described with their main and secondary parts.

692.

692.

Guṇadabbakriyāsaddā, [M 0.0058] siyuṃ sabbe visesanā;

Visesyādhīnabhāvena, visesyasamaliṅgino.

Words denoting qualities, substances, actions, and sounds are all adjectives; being dependent on the substantive, they take the same gender as the substantive.

693.

693.

Sobhanaṃ ruciraṃ sādhu, manuññaṃ cāru sundaraṃ;

Vaggu manoramaṃ kantaṃ, hārī mañju ca pesalaṃ.

Lovely, delightful, good, pleasing, beautiful, fair; graceful, charming, attractive, elegant, sweet, and amiable.

694.

694.

Bhaddaṃ vāmañca kalyāṇaṃ, manāpaṃ laddhakaṃ subhaṃ;

Uttamo pavaro jeṭṭho, pamukhā’nuttaro varo.

Good, auspicious, lovely, pleasing, fortunate, beautiful; highest, excellent, foremost, chief, unsurpassed, and best.

695.

695.

Mukhyo padhānaṃ pāmokkho, para maggañña muttaraṃ;

Paṇītaṃ paramaṃ seyyo, gāmaṇī seṭṭha sattamā.

Chief, principal, foremost; supreme, pre-eminent, highest; exquisite, supreme, superior; leader, best, and most excellent.

696.

696.

Visiṭṭhā’riya nāge’ko, sabhaggā mokkha puṅgavā;

Sīha kuñjara saddūlā, dī tu samāsagā pume.

Distinguished, noble, elephant, unique; foremost in an assembly, chief, bull; lion, elephant, tiger—these and others, at the end of a compound, are masculine.

697.

697.

Citta’kkhi pītijanana, mabyāseka masecanaṃ;

Iṭṭhaṃ tu subhagaṃ hajjaṃ, dayitaṃ vallabhaṃ piyaṃ.

Generating joy for the mind and eye, flawless, captivating; desirable, lovely, delightful, cherished, beloved, and dear.

698.

698.

Tucchañca rittakaṃ suññaṃ, athā’sārañca pheggu ca;

Mejjhaṃ pūtaṃ pavitto tha, aviraddho apaṇṇako.

Empty, hollow, void; insubstantial and pithless. Pure, purified, holy; successful and certain.

699.

699.

Ukkaṭṭho ca pakaṭṭho tha, nihīno hīna lāmakā;

Patikiṭṭhaṃ nikiṭṭhañca, ittarā’vajja kucchitā.

Exalted and prominent; inferior, base, vile; repulsive, degraded; trivial, blameworthy, and disgusting.

700.

700.

Adhamo’maka gārayhā,

Malīno tu malīmaso;

Brahā mahantaṃ vipulaṃ, visālaṃ puthulaṃ puthu.

Lowest, mean, reprehensible; defiled and utterly filthy. Great, vast, extensive, broad, wide, and spacious.

701.

701.

Garu’ru vitthiṇṇa matho, pīnaṃ thūlañca pīvaraṃ;

Thullañca vaṭharañcā tha, ācitaṃ nicitaṃ bhave.

Heavy, vast, extensive; plump, stout, corpulent; large, immense; accumulated and heaped up.

702.

702.

Sabbaṃ samatta makhilaṃ, nikhilaṃ sakalaṃ tathā;

Nissesaṃ kasiṇā’sesaṃ, samaggañca anūnakaṃ,

All, entire, complete, whole, total; without remainder, all-embracing, exhaustive; united, and without lack.

703.

703.

Bhūri pahutaṃ pacuraṃ, bhiyyo sambahulaṃ bahu;

Yebhuyyaṃ bahulaṃ cātha, bāhiraṃ paribāhiraṃ.

Abundant, plentiful, numerous; more, very many, many; mostly, frequent; outer and external.

704.

704.

Parosatādī te, yesaṃ, paraṃ mattaṃ satādito;

Parittaṃ sukhumaṃ khuddaṃ, thoka mappaṃ kisaṃ tanu.

`Parosata` and the like refer to that which is more than a hundred. Limited, subtle, small, little, scanty, lean, and thin.

705.

705.

Cullaṃ matte’tthiyaṃ lesa,

Lavā’ṇuhi kaṇo pume;

Samīpaṃ nikaṭā’sanno, pakaṭṭhā’bhyāsa santikaṃ.

Small; a trifle, a hint (feminine); a particle, an atom, a grain (masculine). Near, close, adjacent, proximate, in the vicinity, and in the presence of.

706.

706.

Avidūrañca [M 0.0059] sāmantaṃ, sannikaṭṭha mupantikaṃ;

Sakāsaṃ antikaṃ ñattaṃ, dūraṃ tu vippakaṭṭhakaṃ.

Not far, neighboring, adjacent, proximate; in the presence of, near, known. Far, distant, and remote.

707.

707.

Nirantaraṃ ghanaṃ sandaṃ, viraḷaṃ pelavaṃ tanu;

Athā yataṃ dīgha matho, nittalaṃ vaṭṭa vaṭṭulaṃ.

Continuous, dense, compact; sparse, pliable, thin. Then, extended, long; then, flat, round, and rounded.

708.

708.

Ucco tu unnato tuṅgo, udaggo ceva ucchito;

Nīco rasso vāmano tha, ajimho paguṇo uju.

High, lofty, towering, elevated, upraised; low, short, dwarfed; not crooked, skilled, upright.

709.

709.

Aḷāraṃ vellitaṃ vaṅkaṃ, kuṭilaṃ jimha kuñcitaṃ;

Dhuvo ca sassato nicco, sadātana sanantanā.

Bent, wavy, curved, crooked, twisted, contorted; firm, eternal, constant, perpetual, and ancient.

710.

710.

Kūṭaṭṭho tve’karūpena, kālabyāpī pakāsito;

Lahu sallahukaṃ cātha, saṅkhyātaṃ gaṇitaṃ mitaṃ.

Unchanging, of one form, pervading time, declared; light, very light; counted, reckoned, measured.

711.

711.

Tiṇhaṃ tu tikhiṇaṃ tibbaṃ, caṇḍaṃ uggaṃ kharaṃ bhave;

Jaṅgamañca carañceva, tasaṃ ñeyyaṃ carācaraṃ.

Sharp, keen, intense, fierce, violent, harsh; moving, wandering, trembling; the animate and inanimate are to be known.

712.

712.

Kampanaṃ calanaṃ cātha, atiritto tathā’dhiko;

Thāvaro jaṅgamā añño, lolaṃ tu cañcalaṃ calaṃ.

Shaking, trembling; excessive, likewise superior; stationary is that which is other than moving; unsteady, fickle, unstable.

713.

713.

Taralañca purāṇo tu, purātana sanantanā;

Cirantano tha paccaggho, nūtano’bhinavo navo.

Trembling; and ancient, primeval, eternal, long-standing; then fresh, new, recent, novel.

714.

714.

Kurūraṃ kaṭhinaṃ daḷhaṃ, niṭṭhuraṃ kakkhaḷaṃ bhave;

Anitthya’nto pariyanto, panto ca pacchima’ntimā.

Cruel, hard, firm, harsh, rough; end, boundary, limit, final, and ultimate.

715.

715.

Jighaññaṃ carimaṃ pubbaṃ, tva’ggaṃ paṭhama mādi so;

Patirūpo nucchavikaṃ, atha moghaṃ niratthakaṃ.

Lowest, last; former, foremost, first, beginning; suitable, proper; then vain, meaningless.

716.

716.

Byattaṃ puṭa phuṭaṃ (sī.) ñca mudu tu, sukumārañca komalaṃ;

Paccakkhaṃ indriyaggayhaṃ, apaccakkhaṃ atindriyaṃ.

Skilled, clear; and soft, tender, and gentle; directly perceived, graspable by the senses; not directly perceived, beyond the senses.

717.

717.

Itarā’ññataro eko, añño bahuvidho tu ca;

Nānārūpo ca vividho, abādhaṃ tu niraggalaṃ.

Other, a certain one, one, another; and manifold, of various forms, and diverse; unobstructed and unimpeded.

718.

718.

Athe’kākī ca ekacco, eko ca ekako samā;

Sādhāraṇañca sāmaññaṃ, sambādho tu ca saṃkaṭaṃ.

Solitary, a certain one, one, and single are the same; common and general; congested and narrow.

719.

719.

Vāmaṃ kaḷevaraṃ sabyaṃ; Apasabyaṃ tu dakkhiṇaṃ;

Paṭikūlaṃ tva’pasabyaṃ, gahanaṃ kalilaṃ samā.

Left, `sabya`; `apasabya`, right; contrary, `apasabya`; dense and confused are the same.

720.

720.

Uccāvacaṃ [M 0.0060] bahubhedaṃ, saṃkiṇṇā’ kiṇṇa saṃkulā;

Katahattho ca kusalo, pavīṇā’bhiñña sikkhitā.

High and low, manifold; mixed, crowded, confused; skilled-handed, skillful, expert, experienced, and trained.

721.

721.

Nipuṇo ca paṭu cheko, cāturo dakkha pesalā;

Bālo dattu jalo mūḷho, mando viññū ca bāliso.

Subtle, sharp, skilled, clever, adroit, amiable; foolish, stupid, idiotic, bewildered, dull-witted, and childish; and also the wise.

722.

722.

Puññavā sukatī dhañño, mahussāho mahādhiti;

Mahātaṇho mahiccho tha, hadayī hadayālu ca.

Meritorious, well-doing, fortunate; greatly energetic, greatly determined; of great craving, of great ambition; compassionate, kind-hearted.

723.

723.

Sumano haṭṭhacitto tha, dummano vimano pyatha;

Vadāniyo vadaññū ca, dānasoṇḍo bahuppado.

Cheerful, joyful in heart; then downcast, disheartened; liberal, bountiful; fond of giving, giving much.

724.

724.

Khyāto patīto paññāto,

Bhiññāto pathito suto,

Vissuto vidito ceva, pasiddho pākaṭo bhave.

Famous, known, well-known, recognized, celebrated, heard of, widely known, understood, renowned, and manifest.

725.

725.

Issaro nāyako sāmī, patī’sā’dhipatī pabhū;

Ayyā’dhipā’dhibhū netā,

Ibbho tva’ḍḍho tathā dhanī.

Lord, leader, master, ruler, sovereign, powerful one; noble, chief, conqueror, guide; rich, wealthy, and affluent.

726.

726.

Dānāraho dakkhiṇeyyo, siniddho tu ca vacchalo;

Parikkhako kāraṇiko,

Āsatto tu ca tapparo.

Worthy of gifts, deserving of offerings; affectionate and kind; an examiner, a reasoner; attached and zealous.

727.

727.

Kāruṇiko dayālupi, sūrato ussuko tu ca;

Iṭṭhatthe uyyuto cātha, dīghasutto cirakriyo.

Compassionate, kind; brave and energetic; striving for a desirable goal; procrastinating, slow in action.

728.

728.

Parādhīno parāyatto, āyatto tu ca santako;

Pariggaho adhīno ca, sacchando tu ca serini.

Dependent on others, reliant on others, dependent, belonging to; possessed, controlled; self-willed, free.

729.

729.

Anisammakārī jammo, atitaṇho tu lolupo;

Giddho tu luddho lolo tha,

Kuṇṭho mando kriyāsu hi.

One who acts without reflection, a wretch; excessively craving, covetous; greedy, covetous, unsteady; dull, slow in actions.

730.

730.

Kāmayitā tu kamitā, kāmano kāmi kāmuko;

Soṇḍo matto vidheyyo tu,

Assavo subbaco samā.

Desirous, loving, lustful, passionate, sensual; addicted, intoxicated; compliant, obedient, and docile are the same.

731.

731.

Pagabbho [M 0.0061] paṭibhāyutto, bhīsīlo bhīru bhīruko;

Adhīro avīro (ṭī.) kātaro cātha,

Hiṃsāsīlo ca ghātuko.

Bold, eloquent; of a fearful nature, fearful, timid; faint-hearted, cowardly; of violent habits, and murderous.

732.

732.

Kodhano rosano dosano (sī.) kopī,

Caṇḍo tvaccantakodhano;

Sahano khamano khantā, titikkhavā ca khantimā.

Wrathful, resentful, irascible; fierce, extremely wrathful; enduring, forbearing, patient, tolerant, and possessed of patience.

733.

733.

Saddhāyutto tu saddhālu, dhajavā tu dhajālu ca lajjālutu ca lajjavā (ka.);

Niddālu niddāsīlo tha, bhassaro bhāsuro bhave.

Endowed with faith, faithful; possessing a banner, fond of banners; sleepy, habitually sleepy; radiant, shining.

734.

734.

Naggo digambaro’vattho, ghasmaro tu ca bhakkhako;

Eḷamūgo tu vattuñca, sotuṃ cā’kusalo bhave.

Naked, sky-clad, unrobed; gluttonous and a voracious eater; a deaf-mute is one unskilled in both speaking and listening.

735.

735.

Mukharo dummukhā’baddha, mukhā cāppiyavādini;

Vācālo bahugārayha, vace vattā tu so vado.

Talkative, foul-mouthed, of unrestrained speech, and speaking unpleasant words; loquacious, much to be blamed; one who utters speech is indeed a speaker.

736.

736.

Nijo sako attaniyo,

Vimhayo’cchariya’bbhuto;

Vihattho byākulo cātha,

Ātatāyī vadhudyato.

One's own, belonging to oneself, personal; astonishing, marvelous, wonderful; distressed, agitated; an aggressor, intent on murder.

737.

737.

Sīsacchejjamhi vajjho tha, nikato ca saṭho’nuju;

Sūcako pisuṇo kaṇṇe,

Japo dhutto tu vañcako.

One deserving beheading, punishable by death; deceitful, crafty, and crooked; an informer, a slanderer, a whisperer in the ear; a rogue and a deceiver.

738.

738.

Anisamma hi yo kiccaṃ, puriso vadhabandhanādi mācarati;

Avinicchitakārittā, sokhalu capaloti viññeyyo.

Indeed, the person who performs an action without reflection, such as killing and binding, is to be known as fickle on account of acting without investigation.

739.

739.

Khuddo kadariyo thaddha, maccharī kapaṇo pyatha;

Akiñcano daliddo ca, dīno niddhana duggatā.

Mean, miserly, obstinate, selfish, and also wretched; having nothing, and poor, destitute, penniless, unfortunate.

740.

740.

Asambhāvitasampattaṃ, kākatāliya muccate;

Atha yācanako atthī, yācako ca vanibbako.

An unforeseen attainment is called 'crow-and-palm-fruit' [a chance occurrence]; then, there is the supplicant, the asker, and the mendicant.

741.

741.

Aṇḍajā pakkhisappādī, narādī tu jalābujā;

Sedajā kimiḍaṃsādī, devādī tvo’ papātikā.

Egg-born are birds and serpents; human beings are womb-born; moisture-born are insects and gnats; gods are spontaneously born.

742.

742.

Jaṇṇutaggho [M 0.0062] jaṇṇumatto, kappo tu kiñcidūnake;

Antaggataṃ bhu pariyā, panna mantogadho’gadhā.

Knee-high, knee-deep; 'kappo' is in the sense of 'a little less'; contained within, pervaded, fallen, included, deep, and shallow.

743.

743.

Rādhito sādhito cātha, nippakkaṃ kuthitaṃ bhave;

Āpanno tvā’padappatto, vivaso tvavaso bhave.

Accomplished, and achieved; uncooked, rotten; having fallen into misfortune, having met with calamity; powerless, and without control.

744.

744.

Nuṇṇo nuttā’tta, khittā ce’, ritā viddhā tha kampito;

Dhūto ādhūta calitā, nisitaṃ tu ca tejitaṃ.

Thrust, expelled, taken, thrown, impelled, pierced, and shaken; shaken, agitated, moved; and sharpened, whetted.

745.

745.

Pattabbaṃ gamma māpajjaṃ āsajjaṃ (ka.), pakkaṃ pariṇataṃ samā;

Veṭhitaṃ tu valayitaṃ, ruddhaṃ saṃvuta māvutaṃ.

To be attained, reachable, to be reached; ripe, matured, same; encircled, coiled; blocked, shut up, covered.

746.

746.

Parikkhittañca nivutaṃ, visaṭaṃ vitthataṃ tataṃ;

Litto tu diddho gūḷho tu,

Gutto puṭṭho tu posito.

Surrounded and enclosed; scattered, spread, extended; smeared, daubed; concealed; guarded; nourished, supported.

747.

747.

Lajjito hīḷito cātha, sanitaṃ dhanitaṃ pyatha;

Sandānito sito baddho,

Kīlito saṃyato bhave.

Ashamed, and humiliated; sounded, and also resonated; fastened, bound, tied, pegged, and restrained.

748.

748.

Siddhe nipphanna nibbattā, dārite bhinna bheditā;

Channo tu chādite cātha, viddhe chiddita vedhitā.

Accomplished, finished, produced; split, broken, divided; covered, and concealed; pierced, perforated, penetrated.

749.

749.

Āhaṭo ābhatā’nītā,

Danto tu damito siyā;

Santo tu samito ceva,

Puṇṇo tu pūrito bhave.

Brought, fetched, led; tamed, and subdued; peaceful, and calmed; full, and filled.

750.

750.

Apacāyito mahito, pūjitā’rahito’ccito;

Mānito cā’pacito ca, tacchitaṃ tutanūkate.

Revered, honored, worshipped, venerated; respected, and revered; pared, and made thin.

751.

751.

Santatto dhūpito copa,

Carito tu upāsito;

Bhaṭṭhaṃ tu galitaṃ pannaṃ, cutañca dhaṃsitaṃ bhave.

Heated, fumigated, and scorched; practiced, and attended upon; fallen, dripped, dropped; and passed away, destroyed.

752.

752.

Pīto pamudito haṭṭho,

Matto tuṭṭho tha kantito;

Sañchinno lūna dātā tha,

Pasattho vaṇṇito thuto.

Pleased, delighted, elated; intoxicated, satisfied, and desired; cut off, severed, mown; praised, lauded, extolled.

753.

753.

Tinto’lla’dda [M 0.0063] kilinno’nnā, maggitaṃ pariyesitaṃ;

Anvesitaṃ gavesitaṃ, laddhaṃ tu patta muccate.

Wet, moist, damp, soaked, drenched; searched for, sought after; investigated, searched; obtained, and reached, it is said.

754.

754.

Rakkhitaṃ gopitaṃ guttaṃ, tātaṃ gopāyitā’vitā;

Pālitaṃ atha ossaṭṭhaṃ, cattaṃ hīnaṃ samujjhitaṃ.

Guarded, protected, secured; sheltered, guarded, defended, and protected; then relinquished, given up, abandoned, utterly abandoned.

755.

755.

Bhāsitaṃ lapitaṃ vuttā, bhihitā’khyāta jappitā;

Udīritañca kathitaṃ, gaditaṃ bhaṇito’ditā.

Spoken, chattered, uttered, declared, related, recited; and expressed, narrated, said, spoken, told.

756.

756.

Avaññātā’vagaṇitā, paribhūtā’vamānitā;

Jighacchito tu khudito,

Chāto ceva bubhukkhito.

Disregarded, scorned, despised, humiliated; hungry, and famished; starved, and hungry.

757.

757.

Buddhaṃ ñātaṃ paṭipannaṃ, viditā’vagataṃ mataṃ;

Gilito khādito bhutto,

Bhakkhito’jjhohaṭā’sitā.

Understood, known, practiced, cognized, comprehended, considered; swallowed, chewed, consumed, eaten, ingested, devoured.

Iti visesyādhīnavaggo.

Thus ends the Chapter on Adjectives.

2. Saṃkiṇṇavagga

2. The Miscellaneous Chapter

758.

758.

Ñeyyaṃ liṅga miha kvāpi, paccayatthavasena ca;

Kriyā tu kiriyaṃ kammaṃ,

Santi tu samatho samo;

Damo ca damatho danti, vattaṃ tu suddhakammani;

Atho āsaṅgavacanaṃ, tīsu vuttaṃ parāyaṇaṃ.

Here, gender is to be known in some cases also by way of cause and meaning; 'kriyā' means action ('kiriyaṃ') and deed ('kammaṃ'); 'santi' means tranquility ('samatho') and calm ('samo'); 'damo' means taming ('damatho') and self-control ('danti'); 'vattaṃ' is in the sense of pure conduct; and 'parāyaṇaṃ', a word for attachment, is used in the three genders.

759.

759.

Bhedo vidāro phuṭanaṃ, tappanaṃ tu ca pīṇanaṃ;

Akkosana mabhisaṅgo,

Bhikkhā tu yācanā’tthanā.

Breaking, splitting, bursting; satisfying, and gladdening; insulting, accusation; alms, requesting, and asking.

760.

760.

Ninnimittaṃ yadicchā thā’, pucchanā nandanāni ca;

Sabhājana matho ñāyo,

Nayo phāti tu vuddhiyaṃ.

Signlessness, spontaneity; asking, and enjoyments; courtesy, and then justice; method and prosperity are in the sense of growth.

761.

761.

Kilamatho kilamanaṃ, pasavo tu pasūtiyaṃ;

Ukkaṃso tvatisayo tha,

Jayo ca jayanaṃ jiti.

'Kilamatho' means weariness; 'pasavo' is in the sense of birth; 'ukkaṃso' means excellence; and 'jayo' means conquering and victory.

762.

762.

Vaso [M 0.0064] kanti, byadho vedho,

Gaho gāho varo vuti;

Pacā pāko havo huti hūbhi (sī. amarakosa),

Vedo vedana mitthi vā.

'Vaso' means desire; 'byadho', piercing; 'gaho', seizure; 'varo', choice; 'pacā', cooking; 'havo', invocation; 'vedo' means feeling ('vedanā'), and is feminine.

763.

763.

Jīraṇaṃ jāni tāṇaṃ tu, rakkhaṇaṃ pamitippamā;

Sileso sandhi ca khayo,

Tvapacayo ravo raṇo.

'Jīraṇaṃ' means decay; 'tāṇaṃ', protection; 'pamiti', measure; 'sileso', joining; 'khayo', dwindling; 'ravo', sound.

764.

764.

Nigādo nigado mādo, mado pasiti bandhanaṃ;

Ākaro tviṅgitaṃ iṅgo,

Atha’tthāpagamo byayo.

'Nigādo' means a shout; 'mādo', intoxication; 'pasiti', a snare or bond; 'ākāro' means gesture or sign; then, loss of wealth means expense or destruction.

765.

765.

Antarāyo ca paccūho,

Vikāro tu vikatya’pi;

Pavisileso vidhuraṃ, upavesanamāsanaṃ.

Obstruction and obstacle; 'vikāro' also means transformation; separation means being apart; sitting down means a seat.

766.

766.

Ajjhāsayo adhippāyo, āsayo cābhisandhi ca;

Bhāvo dhimutti chando tha,

Doso ādīnavo bhave.

Disposition, intention, inclination, and purpose; state, resolve, and desire; then, fault and danger.

767.

767.

Ānisaṃso guṇo cātha,

Majjhaṃ vemajjha muccate;

Majjhanhiko majjhantiko (ṭī. sī. pī.) tu majjhanho, vemattaṃ tu ca nānatā.

Benefit and quality; then, the middle is called impartiality; midday is noon, and difference is variety.

768.

768.

Vā jāgaro jāgariyaṃ jāgariyo (ṭī.), pavāho tu pavatti ca;

Byāso papañco vitthāro,

Yāmo tu saṃyamo yamo.

Wakefulness and vigilance; flow and continuity; diffusion, proliferation, and expansion; restraint and the lord of death.

769.

769.

Sambāhanaṃ maddanañca, pasaro tu visappanaṃ;

Santhavo tu paricayo,

Melako saṅga saṅgamā.

Massaging and rubbing; spreading and diffusion; intimacy and acquaintance; assembly, association, and meeting.

770.

770.

Sannidhi sannikaṭṭhamhi, vināso tu adassanaṃ;

Lavo bhilāvo lavanaṃ,

Patthāvo’vasaro samā.

Proximity and nearness; destruction and disappearance; cutting, splitting, and reaping; proposal and opportunity are alike.

771.

771.

Osānaṃ [M 0.0065] pariyosānaṃ, ukkaṃso’tisayo bhave avasānaṃ samāpanaṃ (?);

Sanniveso ca saṇṭhānaṃ, athā’bbhantara mantaraṃ.

Ending and conclusion; excellence is eminence; settlement and configuration; then, the interior and the inside.

772.

772.

Pāṭihīraṃ pāṭiheraṃ, pāṭihāriya muccate;

Kiccaṃ tu karaṇīyañca, saṅkhāro vāsanā bhave.

Marvel, wonder, and miracle are said to be the same; duty and what is to be done; a formation is a latent tendency.

773.

773.

Pavanaṃ pava nippāvā, tasaro suttaveṭhanaṃ;

Saṅkamo duggasañcāro, pakkamo tu upakkamo.

Winnowing and sifting; a shuttle and a spool of thread; a bridge and a difficult passage; undertaking and approach.

774.

774.

Pāṭho nipāṭho nipaṭho, vicayo magganā pume;

Āliṅganaṃ parissaṅgo, sileso upagūhanaṃ.

Recitation, reading, and study; investigation and search, in the masculine gender; embracing and hugging; adherence and clasping.

775.

775.

Ālokanañca nijjhānaṃ, ikkhaṇaṃ dassanaṃ pyatha;

Paccādeso nirasanaṃ, paccakkhānaṃ nirākati.

Looking and contemplating, viewing and seeing; rejecting and refusing, denying and repudiating.

776.

776.

Vipallāso’ññathābhāvo, byattayo viparīyayo;

Vipariyāso’tikkamo, tva’tipāto upaccayo.

Perversion and alteration; reversal and inversion; contrariety and transgression; offence and transgression.

Iti saṃkiṇṇavaggo.

Thus the Miscellaneous Chapter.

3. Anekatthavagga

3. The Chapter on Various Meanings

777.

777.

Anekatthe pavakkhāmi, gāthā’ddhapādato kamā;

Ettha liṅgavisesattha, mekassa punaruttatā.

I shall explain the various meanings in sequence, from half a verse onward; herein are gender distinctions, specific meanings, and the repetition of a single word.

778.

778.

Samayo samavāye ca, samūhe kāraṇe khaṇe;

Paṭivedhe siyā kāle, pahāne lābha diṭṭhisu.

Samaya means: combination, collection, cause, and moment; it may also mean penetration, time, abandoning, gain, and views.

779.

779.

Vaṇṇo saṇṭhāna rūpesu, jāti,cchavīsu kāraṇe;

Pamāṇe ca pasaṃsāyaṃ, akkhare ca yase guṇe.

Vaṇṇa means: shape and form, class and complexion, and cause; also measure and praise, a syllable, fame, and quality.

780.

780.

Uddese pātimokkhassa, paṇṇattiya muposatho;

Upavāse ca aṭṭhaṅge, uposathadine siyā.

Uposatha refers to the recitation of the Pātimokkha and its precepts; also to fasting, the eight-factored observance, and the Uposatha day itself.

781.

781.

Rathaṅge lakkhaṇe dhammo, racakke’svīriyāpathe;

Cakkaṃ sampattiyaṃ cakka, ratane maṇḍale bale.

Cakka means: a chariot part, a mark, the Dhamma, a wheel, and deportment; also fortune, a jewel, a sphere, and power.

782.

782.

Kulālabhaṇḍe āṇāya, māyudhe dāna rāsisu;

in a potter’s vessel, in command, in a weapon, in gifts, and in heaps.

783.

783.

Dānasmiṃ [M 0.0066] brahmacariya, mappamaññāsu sāsane;

Methunāratiyaṃ veyyā, vacce sadāratuṭṭhiyaṃ;

Pañcasīlā’riyamaggo, posathaṅga dhitīsu ṭhitīsu (ka.) ca.

The holy life consists in: giving, the immeasurables, the teaching, abstention from sexual intercourse, service, contentment with one's own wife, the five precepts, the noble path, the factors of the Uposatha, and fortitude.

784.

784.

Dhammo sabhāve pariyattipaññā,

Ñāyesu saccappakatīsu puññe;

Ñeyye guṇā’cāra samādhisūpi,

Nissattatā’pattisu kāraṇādo.

Dhamma means: nature, scripture, and wisdom; methods, truths, and natural states; merit, the knowable, virtue, conduct, and also concentration; soullessness, offences, and cause.

785.

785.

Attho payojane saddā, bhidheyye vuddhiyaṃ dhane;

Vatthumhi kāraṇe nāse, hite pacchimapabbate.

Attha means: purpose, the meaning of a word, prosperity, and wealth; an object, a cause, destruction, welfare, and the western mountain.

786.

786.

Yebhuyyatā’byāmissesu, visaṃyoge ca kevalaṃ;

Daḷhatthe’natireke cā, navasesamhi taṃ tisu.

Kevalaṃ means: majority, non-mixture, and separation; firmness, non-excess, and that which is without remainder; it is used in all three genders.

787.

787.

Guṇo paṭala rāsīsu, ānisaṃse ca bandhane;

Appadhāne ca sīlādo, sukkādimhi jiyāya ca.

Guṇa means: a layer and a heap, a benefit and a bond; what is subordinate, virtue and so on, qualities such as whiteness, and a bowstring.

788.

788.

Rukkhādo vijjamāne cā, rahante khandhapañcake;

Bhūto satta mahābhūtā, manussesu na nāriyaṃ.

Bhūta means: trees and so on, what exists, an Arahant, and the five aggregates; also a being, the great elements, and humans, but not a woman.

789.

789.

Vāccaliṅgo atītasmiṃ, jāte patte same mato;

The adjectival past participle is used in the sense of the past, of being born, of being attained, of being equal, and of being dead.

790.

790.

Sundare daḷhikamme cā, yācane sampaṭicchane;

Sajjane sampahaṃsāyaṃ, sādhvā’bhidheyyaliṅgikaṃ.

Sādhu means: the beautiful, confirmation, requesting, and acceptance; a good person and rejoicing; it is an indeclinable.

791.

791.

Anto nitthī samīpe cā, vasāne padapūraṇe;

Dehāvayave koṭṭhāse, nāsa sīmāsu lāmake.

Anta (not feminine) means: the inside, the vicinity, the end, and an expletive; a body part, a portion, destruction, a boundary, and the inferior.

792.

792.

Nikāye sandhi sāmañña, ppasūtīsu kule bhave;

Visese sumanāyañca, jāti saṅkhatalakkhaṇe.

Jāti means: a collection, a connection, generality, and production; a clan, existence, and a specific kind; also the jasmine flower and the characteristic of the conditioned.

793.

793.

Bhavabhede patiṭṭhāyaṃ, niṭṭhā’jjhāsayabuddhisu;

Vāsaṭṭhāne ca gamane, visaṭatte visaratta visadatte gatīritā.

Gati is said to mean: a type of existence, a resort, a conclusion, a disposition, and understanding; also a dwelling place, movement, and diffusion.

794.

794.

Phale vipassanā dibba, cakkhu sabbaññutāsu ca;

Paccavekkhaṇañāṇamhi, magge ca ñāṇadassanaṃ.

The term `ñāṇadassana` (knowledge and vision) is used for fruition, insight, the divine eye, and omniscience; for reviewing knowledge, and for the path.

795.

795.

Kammāruddhana aṅgāra, kapalla dīpikāsu ca;

Suvaṇṇakāramūsāyaṃ, ukkā vege ca vāyuno.

The term `ukkā` (torch/meteor) is used for a smith's furnace, charcoal embers, and lamps made of potsherds; for a goldsmith's crucible, and for the force of the wind.

796.

796.

Kesohāraṇa, [M 0.0067] jīvita, vuttisu vapane ca vāpasamakaraṇe;

Kathane pamuttabhāva, jjhenādo jjhesanādo (sī.) vutta mapi tīsu.

The term `vutta` is used for hair-removal, life, and livelihood; for sowing or for pacifying; for utterance, the state of release, and meditation, etc.—it is also said in these three contexts.

797.

797.

Gamane vissute cā’va, dhārito’pacitesu ca;

Anuyoge kilinne ca, suto’ bhidheyyaliṅgiko.

The term `suta` is used for going and for being famous; for being borne and for being accumulated; for application and for being wet. It takes the gender of the noun it signifies.

798.

798.

Sotaviññeyya satthesu, sutaṃ putte suto siyā;

The term `sutaṃ` is used for doctrines cognizable by the ear; in the sense of 'son', it would be `suto`.

799.

799.

Kappo kāle yuge lese, paññatti paramāyusu;

Sadise tīsu samaṇa, vohāra kappabindusu;

Samantatthe’ntarakappā, dike takke vidhimhi ca.

The term `kappo` is used for time, an age, a fraction, a designation, and the maximum lifespan; for what is fitting, for ascetic conventions, and for allowable marks; in a comprehensive sense, for intermediate aeons and so forth, for reasoning, and for rules.

800.

800.

Nibbāna magga virati, sapathe saccabhāsite;

Tacche cā’riyasaccamhi, diṭṭhiyaṃ sacca mīritaṃ.

The term `sacca` (truth) is declared in the context of Nibbāna, the path, abstinence, an oath, truthful speech, the real, the Noble Truth, and right view.

801.

801.

Sañjātidese hetumhi, vāsaṭṭhānā’karesu ca;

Samosaraṇaṭṭhāne cā, yatanaṃ padapūraṇe.

The term `āyatana` (base/sphere) is used for a birthplace, a cause, a dwelling place, and a source; for a place of assembly, and as a metrical expletive.

802.

802.

Antaraṃ majjha vattha’ñña, khaṇo’kāso’dhi hetusu;

Byavadhāne vinaṭṭhe ca, bhede chidde manasya’pi.

The term `antara` is used for middle, garment, other, moment, opportunity, superiority, and cause; for an interval, what is destroyed, a division, a flaw, and also for the mind.

803.

803.

Ārogye kusalaṃ iṭṭha, vipāke kusalo tathā;

Anavajjamhi cheke ca, kathito vāccaliṅgiko.

The term `kusalaṃ` is used for health and for a desired result; `kusalo` is used likewise. It is also used for blamelessness and for dexterity, and is said to take the gender of the noun it qualifies.

804.

804.

Dravā’cāresu vīriye, madhurādīsu pārade;

Siṅgārādo dhātubhede, kicce sampattiyaṃ raso.

The term `rasa` (taste/essence) is used for liquids, conduct, and energy; for the sweet and other tastes, and for mercury; for the erotic and other sentiments, for the various bodily humors, for function, and for attainment.

805.

805.

Bodhi sabbaññutaññāṇe, riyamagge ca nāriyaṃ;

Paññattiyaṃ pume’ ssattha, rukkhamhi purisitthiyaṃ.

The term `bodhi` is used for the knowledge of omniscience and for the noble path; for a woman, a designation, the masculine gender, the Assattha tree, and for a man or a woman.

806.

806.

Sevito yena yo niccaṃ, tatthāpi visayo siyā;

Rūpādike janapade, tathā dese ca gocare.

`Visaya` (domain) may be that which is constantly frequented by someone; it is also used for the realm of forms and so forth, and likewise for a country, a region, and a resort.

807.

807.

Bhāvo padatthe sattāya, madhippāya kriyāsu ca;

Sabhāvasmiñca līlāyaṃ, purisi’tthindriyesu ca.

The term `bhāva` (state/nature) is used for the meaning of a word, for existence, for intention, and for actions; for intrinsic nature, for grace, and for the male and female faculties.

808.

808.

So bandhave’ttani ca saṃ, so dhanasmi manitthiyaṃ;

Sā pume sunakhe vutto, ttaniye so tiliṅgiko.

The word `so` (from `sva`) is used for a relative and for oneself; it is also used for wealth, in which case it is not feminine. The word `sā` is used for a dog (a masculine noun). In the sense of 'one's own', `so` (from `sva`) is of three genders.

809.

809.

Suvaṇṇaṃ kanake vuttaṃ, suvaṇṇo garuḷe tathā;

Pañcadharaṇamatte ca, chavi sampattiyampi ca.

The term `suvaṇṇaṃ` (neuter) is used for gold; `suvaṇṇo` (masculine) is likewise used for the Garuḷa bird; it is also used for a measure of five `dharaṇas`, and for excellence of complexion.

810.

810.

Varo [M 0.0068] devādikā devādito (sī.) iṭṭhe, jāmātari patimhi ca,

Uttame vāccaliṅgo so, varaṃ mandappiye byayaṃ.

The term `varo` is used for a god or similar being, for a desired boon, for a son-in-law, and for a husband; for the highest or best, it takes the gender of the noun it qualifies. The word `varaṃ` is used for what is inferior, unloved, and for loss.

811.

811.

Makule dhanarāsimhi, siyā kosa manitthiyaṃ;

Nettiṃsādi pidhāne ca, dhanupañcasatepi ca.

The term `kosa` may be used for a bud or a heap of wealth, in which case it is not feminine; it is also used for the sheath of a sword and so forth, and for a measure of five hundred bows.

812.

812.

Pitāmahe jine seṭṭhe, brāhmaṇe ca pitūsvapi;

Brahmā vutto tathā brahmaṃ, vede tapasi vuccate.

The term `Brahmā` (masculine) is used for a grandfather, the supreme conqueror, a brahmin, and also for parents; likewise, the term `brahma` (neuter) is used for the Veda and for austerity.

813.

813.

Hatthīnaṃ majjhabandhe ca, pakoṭṭhe kacchabandhane;

Mekhalāyaṃ matā kacchā, kaccho vutto latāya ca.

The term `kacchā` (feminine) is known for the girth-strap of an elephant, the forearm, the binding of a loincloth, and a girdle; the term `kaccho` (masculine) is used for a creeper.

814.

814.

Tatheva bāhumūlamhi, anūpamhi tiṇepi ca;

Likewise, for the armpit, for marshland, and also for grass.

815.

815.

Pamāṇaṃ hetu satthesu, māne ca saccavādini;

Pamātari ca niccamhi, mariyādāya muccate.

The term `pamāṇa` (measure/standard) is used for a cause, for the scriptures, for conceit, and for a truth-speaker; for one who measures, for that which is permanent, and it is also used for a boundary.

816.

816.

Sattaṃ dabba’ttabhāvesu, pāṇesu ca bale, siyā bale sakkā (ṭī.);

Sattāyañca, janesatto, āsatte so tiliṅgiko.

The term `sattaṃ` (neuter) may be used for substance, individuality, living beings, and strength; also for existence. The term `satto` (masculine) is used for a person. In the sense of 'attached', it is of three genders.

817.

817.

Semhādo rasarattādo, mahābhūte pabhādike;

Dhātu dvīsva’ṭṭhicakkhā’di, bhvā’dīsu gerikādisu.

The term `dhātu` is used for phlegm and other bodily humors; for chyle, blood, and so forth; for the great elements; for light and the like; for relics; for bones, eyes, and other body parts; for verbal roots such as `bhū`; and for minerals such as red ochre.

818.

818.

Amaccādo sabhāve ca, yoniyaṃ pakatī’ritā;

Satvādisāmyā’vatthāyaṃ, paccayā paṭhamepi ca.

The term `pakati` is said of ministers and other constituents of a state, of intrinsic nature, and of origin; of the state of equilibrium of the primary qualities (`guṇa`), of a condition, and also of the original source.

819.

819.

Padaṃ ṭhāne parittāṇe, nibbānamhi ca kāraṇe;

Sadde vatthumhi koṭṭhāse, pāde tallañchane mataṃ.

The term `pada` is known for a place, protection, Nibbāna, and a cause; for a word, an object, a portion, a foot, and a footprint.

820.

820.

Lohamuggara meghesu, ghano, tālādike ghanaṃ,;

Nirantare ca kaṭhine, vāccaliṅgika muccate.

The term `ghano` (masculine) is used for an iron hammer and for clouds; `ghanaṃ` (neuter) is used for the solid part of a palm tree and so forth. For that which is uninterrupted and hard, it is said to take the gender of the noun it qualifies.

821.

821.

Khuddā ca makkhikābhede, madhumhi khudda, mappake;

Adhame kapaṇe cāpi, bahumhi catūsu ttisu.

`Khuddā` (feminine) is used for a species of bee, and `khuddaṃ` (neuter) for honey. The term also means small, inferior, and wretched. It is also used for 'much', and in the context of 'four' and 'three'.

822.

822.

Takke maraṇaliṅge ca, ariṭṭhaṃ asubhe subhe,;

Ariṭṭho āsave kāke, nimbe ca pheniladdume.

The term `ariṭṭhaṃ` (neuter) is used in logic, for a sign of death, and for both the inauspicious and the auspicious. The term `ariṭṭho` (masculine) is used for fermented liquor, a crow, the neem tree, and the soapberry tree.

823.

823.

Mānabhaṇḍe palasate, sadisatte tulā tathā;

Gehānaṃ dārubandhattha, pīṭhikāyañca dissati.

The term `tulā` is used for an instrument of measure and for a weight of one hundred `palas`; likewise for equality. It is also seen used for the wooden framework of a house and for a bench.

824.

824.

Mittakāre [M 0.0069] lañjadāne, bale rāsi vipattisu balarāsi vipattisu (ka.);

Yuddhe ceva paṭiññāyaṃ, saṅgaro sampakāsito.

The word 'saṅgara' is declared for showing affection to friends, for giving bribes, for strength, for a heap, for misfortunes, for battle, and for a promise.

825.

825.

Khandhe bhave nimittamhi, rūpaṃ vaṇṇe ca paccaye;

Sabhāva sadda saṇṭhāna, rūpajjhāna vapūsu ca.

'Rūpa' is used for the aggregate, for becoming, for a sign; for form, for color, and for a condition; for nature, sound, and shape; and for the form-jhānas and for bodies.

826.

826.

Vatthu kilesa kāmesu, icchāyaṃ madane rate;

Kāmo, kāmaṃ nikāme, cā, nuññāyaṃ kāma mabyayaṃ.

'Kāma' is used for objects, defilements, and sensual pleasures; for desire, intoxication, and delight. 'Kāmo' is the masculine noun, 'kāmaṃ' the accusative, and it is also used for strong desire. As an indeclinable, 'kāma' is used for permission.

827.

827.

Pokkharaṃ padume dehe, vajjabhaṇḍamukhepi ca;

Sundaratte ca salile, mātaṅgakarakoṭiyaṃ.

'Pokkhara' is used for a lotus, for the body, and also for the sound-hole of a musical instrument; for beauty, for water, and for the tip of an elephant's trunk.

828.

828.

Rāsiniccala māyāsu, dambhā’saccesva’yoghane;

Girisiṅgamhi sīraṅge, yante kūṭa manitthiyaṃ.

'Kūṭa' is used for a heap, for the immovable, and for illusions; for deceit, untruths, and an iron hammer; for a mountain peak, a plough-share, and a machine. 'Kūṭa' is neuter.

829.

829.

Vuddhiyaṃ janane kāma, dhātvādīmhi ca pattiyaṃ;

Sattāyañceva saṃsāre, bhavo sassatadiṭṭhiyaṃ.

'Bhava' is used for growth, birth, and desire; for elements and so forth, and for attainment; for existence and for saṃsāra; and for the eternalist view.

830.

830.

Paṭivākyo’ttarāsaṅge, su’ttaraṃ uttaro tisu;

Seṭṭhe disādibhede ca, parasmi muparī’rito.

'Uttara' is used for a reply and for the upper robe; 'suttaraṃ' means excellent, and 'uttaro' is used in the three genders; it is used for the best, and for distinctions of direction and so forth; and it is said to mean 'further' and 'above'.

831.

831.

Nekkhammaṃ paṭhamajjhāne, pabbajjāyaṃ vimuttiyaṃ;

Vipassanāya nissesa, kusalamhi ca dissati.

'Nekkhamma' is seen in the first jhāna, in going forth, in liberation, in insight, and in all that is wholesome.

832.

832.

Saṅkhāro saṅkhate puññā, bhisaṅkhārādikepi ca;

Payoge kāyasaṅkhārā, dya’bhisaṅkharaṇesu ca.

'Saṅkhāra' is used for the conditioned, and also for meritorious formations and so forth; for application, and for the volitional activities such as bodily formation.

833.

833.

Ārammaṇe ca saṃsaṭṭhe, vokiṇṇe nissaye tathā;

Tabbhāve cāpyabhidheyya, liṅgo sahagato bhave.

The term 'sahagata' is used for an object, for the associated, for the mixed, and likewise for a support; also for that very state and for the expressible.

834.

834.

Tīsu channaṃ patirūpe, chādite ca nigūhite;

Nivāsana pārupane, raho paññattiyaṃ pume.

'Channa' is used in the three genders for what is suitable, what is covered, and what is hidden; for an undergarment and an upper robe; for what is private, for a designation, and for a man.

835.

835.

Buddhasamantacakkhūsu, cakkhu paññāya mīritaṃ;

Dhammacakkhumhi ca maṃsa, dibbacakkhudvayesu ca.

'Cakkhu' is spoken of for the Buddha's all-seeing eye, and for wisdom; and for the Dhamma-eye, the fleshly eye, and the pair of divine eyes.

836.

836.

Vāccaliṅgo abhikkanto, sundaramhi abhikkame;

Abhirūpe khaye vutto, tatheva’bbhanumodane.

'Abhikkanta' is an adjective used for the beautiful and for progression; it is also said of the attractive, of waning, and likewise of rejoicing.

837.

837.

Kāraṇe desanāyañca, vāre vevacanepi ca;

Pākārasmiṃ pakārasmiṃ (ka.) avasare, pariyāyo kathīyati.

'Pariyāya' is declared to be used for a cause and a teaching; for a turn and also for a synonym; for a wall and for an opportunity.

838.

838.

Viññāṇe [M 0.0070] cittakamme ca, vicitte citta muccate;

Paññatti cittamāsesu, citto, tārantare thiyaṃ.

'Citta' (neuter) is said to be used for consciousness, mental action, and the variegated. 'Citto' (masculine) is used for designation, the mind, and the month of Citta. 'Cittā' (feminine) is used for the constellation of that name.

839.

839.

Sāmaṃ vedantare sāntve, taṃ pīte sāmale tisu;

Sayamatthe byayaṃ sāmaṃ, sāmā ca sārivāyapi.

'Sāma' is used for the Sāma Veda and for conciliation; for the color yellow and for the color dark, in the three genders. As an indeclinable, 'sāmaṃ' has the meaning of 'self'. 'Sāmā' is also used for the sārivā plant.

840.

840.

Pume ācariyādimhi, garu guru (ka. ṭī.) mātāpitūsvapi;

Garu tīsu mahante ca, dujjarā’lahukesu ca.

'Garu' is masculine when used for teachers and so forth, and also for mother and father. 'Garu' is used in the three genders for what is great, hard to digest, and heavy.

841.

841.

Accite vijjamāne ca, pasatthe sacca sādhusu;

Khinne ca samite ceva, santobhidheyyaliṅgikoti.

For what is venerated and what exists; for what is praised, for the truthful and the virtuous; for what is exhausted and also for what is calmed, 'santa' is the term, and it is an adjective.

Iti gāthāanekatthavaggo.

Thus ends the chapter on words with various meanings, in verse.

842.

842.

Devo visuddhidevā’do, megha maccu nabhesu ca;

Athopi taruṇe satte, corepi māṇavo bhave.

'Deva' is used for pure deities and so forth, for clouds, for death, and for the sky. 'Māṇava', in turn, is used for a young being and also for a thief.

843.

843.

Ādi koṭṭhāsa koṭīsu, purato’ggaṃvare tīsu;

Paccanīko’ttamesva’ññe, pacchābhāge paro tisu.

'Ādi' is used for beginning, part, and end. 'Agga' is used for 'in front', 'foremost', and 'excellent', in the three genders. 'Para' is used for an adversary, for the highest, for others, and for the latter part, in the three genders.

844.

844.

Yoni kāma siri’ssare, dhammu’yyāma yase bhagaṃ;

Uḷāro tīsu vipule, seṭṭhe ca madhure siyā.

'Bhaga' is used for the womb, desire, fortune, lordship, the teaching, exertion, and fame. 'Uḷāra' is used in the three genders for what is vast, supreme, and sweet.

845.

845.

Sampanno tīsu sampuṇṇe, madhure ca samaṅgini;

Saṅkhā tu ñāṇe koṭṭhāsa, paññatti gaṇanesu ca.

'Sampanna' is used in the three genders for what is complete, sweet, and endowed. 'Saṅkhā' is used for knowledge, a section, a designation, and for counting.

846.

846.

Ṭhānaṃ issariyo’kāsa, hetūsu ṭhitiyampi ca;

Atho māne pakāreca, koṭṭhāseca vidho dvisu.

'Ṭhāna' is used for lordship, opportunity, causes, and also for stability; also for pride, manner, and a portion. 'Vidha' is used in two genders.

847.

847.

Pañño’pavāsa khantīsu, damo indriyasaṃvare;

Ñāṇe ca somanasseca, vedo chandasi co’ccate.

'Khanti' is used for wisdom and for fasting. 'Dama' is used for restraint of the senses. 'Veda' is said to be used for knowledge, gladness, and for the Vedas or meter.

848.

848.

Khandhakoṭṭhāsa, passāva, magga, hetūsu yoni sā yoni so (ṭī.);

Kāle tu kūle sīmāyaṃ, velā rāsimhi bhāsitā.

'Yoni' is used for an aggregate-section, urine, a path, and causes. 'Velā' is spoken of for time, a bank, a boundary, and a heap.

849.

849.

Vohāro sadda paṇṇatti, vaṇijjā cetanāsu ca;

Nāgo tu’raga hatthīsu, nāgarukkhe tathu’ttame.

'Vohāra' is used for a word, a designation, trade, and for volitions. 'Nāga' is used for serpents, elephants, the nāga tree, and likewise for the supreme.

850.

850.

Seṭṭhā’sahāya saṅkhyā’ñña,

Tulyesve’kotiliṅgiko;

Rāge tu mānaso cittā, rahattesu ca mānasaṃ.

'Eka' is used for the best, for the unassociated, for number, for 'other', and for equals, and is of one gender. But 'mānasa' is used for lust born of the mind, and 'mānasaṃ' (neuter) for Arahantship.

851.

851.

Mūlaṃ [M 0.0071] bhe santike mūla, mūle hetumhi pābhate;

Rūpādyaṃ’sa pakaṇḍesu, khandho rāsi guṇesu ca.

'Mūla' is used for fear, for nearness, for a root, for a cause, and for a gift. 'Khandha' is used for form and so forth, for a part, for a section, for a heap, and for qualities.

852.

852.

Ārambho vīriye kamme, ādikamme vikopane;

Atho hadayavatthumhi, citte ca hadayaṃ ure.

'Ārambha' is used for effort, action, initial action, and disturbing. 'Hadaya' is used for the heart-base, for the mind, and for the chest.

853.

853.

Pacchātāpā’nubandhesu, rāgādo’nusayo bhave;

Mātaṅgamuddhapiṇḍe tu, ghaṭe kumbho dasambaṇe.

In that which is followed by remorse, the underlying tendency (`anusaya`) is in lust and so forth. But `kumbha` is used for the frontal globe of an elephant, a pot, and a measure of ten `ambaṇas`.

854.

854.

Parivāro parijane, khaggakose paricchade;

Ālambaro tu sārambhe, bheribhede ca dissati.

`Parivāra` is used for attendants, a sword-sheath, and paraphernalia. But `ālambara` is used for an undertaking, and is also seen in the beating of a drum.

855.

855.

Khaṇo kālavisese ca, nibyāpāraṭṭhitimhi ca;

Kule tva’bhijano vutto, uppattibhūmiyampi ca.

`Khaṇa` is used for a specific time, and also for a state of inactivity. But `abhijana` is said of a family, and also of a place of birth.

856.

856.

Āhāro kabaḷīkārā, hārādīsu ca kāraṇe;

Vissāse yācanāyañca, peme ca paṇayo mato.

`Āhāra` is used for physical food, for garlands and so forth, and for a cause. `Paṇaya` is considered to be trust, asking, and love.

857.

857.

Ṇādo saddhā, cīvarādi, hetvā’dhāresu paccayo;

Kīḷā dibbavihārādo, vihāro sugatālaye.

`Paccaya` is used for faith, robes and so forth, causes, and supports. `Vihāra` is used for play, divine abodes and so forth, and for the abode of the Sugata.

858.

858.

Samatthane mato citte, kaggatāyaṃ samādhi ca;

Yogo saṅgati saṅge ca (ṭī.) kāmādo,

Jhāno’pāyesu yuttiyaṃ.

`Samādhi` is considered to be calming, consciousness, and one-pointedness. `Yoga` is used for association and for sensual pleasures and so forth. `Jhāna` is used for means and for propriety.

859.

859.

Bhogo sappaphaṇa’ṅgesu, koṭille bhuñjane dhane;

Bhūmibhāge kilese ca, male cā’ṅgaṇa muccate.

`Bhoga` is used for a snake's coils, for crookedness, for enjoyment, and for wealth. `Aṅgaṇa` is said of a piece of ground, of defilements, and of dirt.

860.

860.

Dhanādidappe paññāya, abhimāno mato tha ca;

Apadeso nimitte ca, chale ca kathane mato.

`Abhimāna` is considered to be pride in wealth and so forth, and conceit regarding wisdom. `Apadesa` is considered to be a pretext, deceit, and a statement.

861.

861.

Citte kāye sabhāve ca, so attā parama’ttani;

Atha gumbo ca thambasmiṃ thambhasmiṃ (ṭī.), samūhe balasajjane.

`Attā` is used for the mind, the body, nature, and the supreme self. And `gumba` is used for a thicket, a multitude, and an army.

862.

862.

Antoghare kusūle ca, koṭṭho ntokucchiyaṃ pyatha;

Sopānaṅgamhi uṇhīso, makuṭe sīsaveṭhane.

`Koṭṭha` is used for an inner room, a granary, and also for the stomach. `Uṇhīsa` is used for the coping of a staircase, a crown, and a turban.

863.

863.

Niyyāse sekhare dvāre, niyyūho nāgadantake;

Atho sikhaṇḍe tūṇīre, kalāpo nikare mato.

`Niyyūha` is used for resin, a crest, a turret, and a peg. And `kalāpa` is considered to be a tuft, a quiver, and a multitude.

864.

864.

Cūḷā saṃyatakesesu, makuṭe moḷi ca dvisu;

Saṅkho tva’nitthiyaṃ kambu, nalāṭa’ṭṭhīsu lalāṭaṭṭhīsu (sī.) gopphake.

`Cūḷā` is used for bound hair. `Moḷi` is used for a crown, and is of two genders. But `saṅkha`, in the non-feminine genders, is used for a conch, the forehead bone, and the ankle.

865.

865.

Pakkho [M 0.0072] kāle bale sādhye, sakhī vājesu paṅgule;

Dese’ṇṇave pume sindhu, saritāyaṃ sa nāriyaṃ.

`Pakkha` is used for a period of time, a faction, a thesis, an ally, and wings. `Sindhu` is used for a country and the ocean in the masculine gender, and for a river in the feminine gender.

866.

866.

Gaje kareṇu purise, so hatthiniya mitthiyaṃ;

Ratane vajiro nitthī, maṇivedhi’ndahetisu.

`Hatthī` is used for a male elephant, a female elephant, and a man; its feminine form is `hatthinī`. `Vajira`, in the non-feminine genders, is used for a jewel, a gem-cutter's drill, and Indra's weapon.

867.

867.

Visāṇaṃ tīsu mātaṅga, dante ca pasusiṅgake;

Koṭiyaṃ tu mato koṇo, tathā vādittavādane.

`Visāṇa`, in all three genders, is used for an elephant's tusk and an animal's horn. But `koṇa` is considered to be a corner or tip, and likewise a plectrum for playing a musical instrument.

868.

868.

Vaṇippathe ca nagare, vede ca nigamo tha ca;

Vivādādo’ dhikaraṇaṃ, siyā’dhāre ca kāraṇe.

`Nigama` is used for a trade route, a market town, and the Veda. `Adhikaraṇa` is used for a dispute and so forth, and may be a basis or a cause.

869.

869.

Pasumhi vasudhāyañca, vācādo go pumitthiyaṃ;

Harite tu suvaṇṇe ca, vāsudeve harī’rito.

`Go` is used for cattle, the earth, and speech and so forth, in both masculine and feminine genders. But `hari` is said of that which is green, of gold, and of Vāsudeva.

870.

870.

Āyatte parivāre ca, bhariyāyaṃ pariggaho;

Uttaṃso tva’ vataṃso ca, kaṇṇapūre ca sekhare.

`Pariggaha` is used for possessions, retinue, and a wife. But `uttaṃsa` and `avataṃsa` are used for an ear-ornament and a crest.

871.

871.

Vijjuyaṃ vajire cevā, sani’tthipurise pyatha;

Koṇe saṅkhyāvisesasmiṃ, ukkaṃse koṭi nāriyaṃ.

`Asani` is used for lightning and for a thunderbolt, in both feminine and masculine genders. `Koṭi`, in the feminine gender, is used for an end, a specific number, and the highest point.

872.

872.

Cūḷā jālā padhāna’gga, moracūḷāsu sā sikhā;

Sappadāṭhāya māsī’tthī, iṭṭhassā’sīsanāyapi.

`Sikhā` is used for a top-knot, a flame, that which is chief or foremost, and a peacock's crest. `Āsī`, in the feminine gender, is used for a snake's fang, and also for a blessing for one who is dear.

873.

873.

Vasā vilīna telasmiṃ, vasagā vañjhagāvisu;

Abhilāse tu kiraṇe, abhisaṅge ruci’tthiyaṃ.

`Vasā` is used for grease, a docile cow, and a barren cow. But `ruci`, in the feminine gender, is used for desire, a ray of light, and attachment.

874.

874.

Saññā sañjānane nāme, cetanāyañca dissati;

Aṃse sippe kalā kāle, bhāge candassa soḷase.

`Saññā` is used for perception, for a name, and is also seen as volition. `Kalā` is used for a part or a shoulder, for a craft, for a unit of time, for a portion, and for a sixteenth part of the moon.

875.

875.

Bījakose gharakūṭe, kaṇṇabhūsāya kaṇṇikā;

Āgāmikāle dīghatte, pabhāve ca matā’yati.

`Kaṇṇikā` is used for a pericarp, a house-peak, and an ear-ornament. `Āyati` is considered to be future time, length, and power.

876.

876.

Uṇṇā mesādilome, ca, bhūmajjhe romadhātuyaṃ;

Vāruṇī tvitthiyaṃ vuttā, naṭṭakī madirādisu.

`Uṇṇā` is used for wool, for the space between the eyebrows, and for the hair-relic. But `vāruṇī`, in the feminine gender, is said of a female dancer and of spirituous liquor and so forth.

877.

877.

Kriyacitte ca karaṇe, kiriyaṃ kammani kriyā;

Sunisāyaṃ tu kaññāya, jāyāya ca vadhū matā.

`Kiriyā` is used for functional consciousness, for the act of doing, and for a deed. But `vadhū` is considered to be a daughter-in-law, a maiden, and a wife.

878.

878.

Pamāṇi’ssariye mattā, akkharāvayave’ppake;

Suttaṃ pāvacane riṭṭhe siddhe (ṭī.), tante taṃ supite tisu.
